1 
 
1. 
INTRODUCTION: POLITICAL AND LEGAL CONTEXT 
1.1. 
State aid control policy in the field of sustainable land transport 
Competition is a major driver of growth. It incentivises enterprises to enter markets and to 
innovate, improving their productivity and competitiveness. 
State aid control is part of European Union (‘EU’) rules on competition laid down in the Treaty 
on the Functioning of the European Union (‘TFEU’ or the ‘Treaty’). The basic rationale of State 
aid control is to avoid undue market distortions and subsidy races when aid is granted, to 
safeguard the internal market and to create a competitive landscape with a level playing field1.  
According to Article 107(1) TFEU, State aid is support given by a Member State with State 
resources that provides an undertaking or a group of undertakings with an advantage over 
competitors2. State aid can be granted in a variety of ways, most notably through subsidies or 
financial instruments (loans, guarantees, etc.) granted on terms not available on the market.  
While Article 107(1) TFEU lays down a general prohibition of State aid granted by Member 
States to undertakings, other Treaty provisions allow for exceptions. In accordance with Article 
93 TFEU, aid that meets the needs of the coordination of transport shall be compatible with the 
internal market3. This includes aid that leads to a modal shift from transport modes such as road 
to more sustainable ones involving lower external costs (costs to society which are not borne 
by the transport mode). Sustainable land transport (‘SLT’) encompasses rail and inland 
waterways (‘IWW’) transport, as well as multimodal transport4 where at least one of the 
transport modes is rail or IWW, or where land transport is combined with short-sea shipping.  
State aid control is meant to ensure that any detriment arising from distortions of competition 
by State aid is justified by the public purpose pursued. In accordance with Articles 107 and 108 
TFEU, the assessment of the compatibility of State aid measures is the exclusive competence 
of the Commission. The Commission defines the conditions under which State aid may be 
considered compatible with the internal market by adopting horizontal and sectoral guidelines, 
which set out the approach that it will take when assessing the compatibility of State aid 
measures notified by Member States. The Commission regularly revises such guidelines to 
adapt them to technological, economic, legal and policy-related developments. In the field of 
SLT, the relevant guidelines are the 2008 Community guidelines on State aid for railway 
undertakings (‘RGL’)5.  
The Commission has the possibility to exempt from notification measures for which it has 
developed enough positive enforcement practice, or which pose fewer challenges to the internal

Intermodal transport is a subset of multimodal transport and encompasses all freight transport without a change in 
loading units, i.e. where the actual freight remains within the same loading unit throughout the transport operation.

<!-- page:7 -->
 
2 
market, subject to compliance with rules defined ex ante6. The Commission exempts measures 
from notification notably through Commission Regulation (EU) No 651/2014 of 17 June 2014 
declaring certain categories of aid compatible with the internal market in application of Articles 
107 and 108 of the Treaty (the General Block Exemption Regulation or ‘GBER’)7. The GBER 
is however based only on Articles 107 and 108 TFEU; measures related to SLT are therefore 
only very rarely exempted from the notification obligation today.  
In the years from 2008 until 2024, 258 State aid decisions were adopted pertaining to SLT. The 
number of decisions has increased steadily from around 10 in 2008 to a peak of 34 in 2022 
(peak year of decisions, possibly related to the COVID-19 pandemic). In the years 2021, 2023 
and 2024, the number of adopted State aid decisions on SLT was steady at 25-26. While the 
number of decisions concerning SLT has doubled since 2008, the growth in approved State aid 
amounts was significantly greater still, increasing more than 10-fold between 2009, the first full 
year of application of the RGL (EUR 455 million) and 2024 (EUR 4.7 billion).  
The most relevant aid categories are operating aid to cover the external cost difference or 
infrastructure costs (130 State aid decisions and 70% of State aid amounts approved between 
2008 and 2024), investment aid for interoperability and modernisation (42 State aid decisions 
and 9.5% of State aid amounts approved between 2008 and 2024) and State aid for 
infrastructure in the form of facilities and private sidings (48 State aid decisions and 12.8% of 
State aid amounts approved between 2008 and 2024). Aid for the acquisition of vehicles (16 
decisions and 3.34% of State aid amounts approved between 2008 and 2024) and start-up aid 
(5 decisions and 0.3% of State aid amounts approved between 2008 and 2024) were less 
frequent. The main grantors of State aid to SLT are Germany, France, Poland, Italy and Austria.  
As is to be expected with sectoral rules covering only a sub-sector of the transport sector, the 
aid granted to SLT constitutes a small part of the overall State aid measures and spending, also 
as horizontal rules exist, under which much more significant State aid spending occurs. The 
latest version of the State aid Scoreboard dating from 2024 and containing data up to 20238 
reports 3 222 new measures9 and EUR 186.8 billion overall State aid spending in 2023. The 26 
measures and EUR 4.9 billion of State aid approved10 in SLT in 2023 therefore account for less 
than 1% of new measures and for around 2.6% of overall State aid spending on new measures 
in 2023. There were 597 State aid decisions adopted in 2023 overall (most measures are block 
exempted and do not require a State aid decision for approval)11. Discounting block-exempted 
and crisis measures (pertaining to Russia’s war of aggression against Ukraine and the COVID-
19 pandemic), 211 State aid decisions were adopted overall in 2023. The 26 measures approved 
related to SLT in 2023 then account for 12.3% of measures in that year.

This Impact Assessment Report concerns both the revision of the RGL into the envisaged 
revised guidelines (‘the revised GLs’) and the envisaged parallel adoption of a new Block 
Exemption Regulation in the area of land Transport (‘TBER’)12.  
1.2. 
Relationship with other EU initiatives 
General context: the European Green Deal 
The European Climate Law establishes that the EU needs to reduce its greenhouse gas (‘GHG’) 
net emissions by 55% by 2030 as compared to 1990 levels and achieve climate neutrality by 
205013. This goal cannot be reached without a fundamental transformation of European 
transport, which accounts for 25% of all the EU’s GHG emissions. Therefore, the objective was 
set to (i) achieve a 90% reduction in transport GHG emissions by 2050 through the promotion 
of multimodal transport and less polluting transport modes and (ii) shift 75% of inland freight 
carried today by road to rail and IWW14. 
Other initiatives under the Sustainable and Smart Mobility Strategy 
The Commission Communication of 9 December 2020 on a Sustainable and Smart Mobility 
Strategy – putting European transport on track for the future (‘SSMS’)15 further operationalised 
these objectives. Milestones of the SSMS include the following: 
• rail freight traffic should double by 2050, 
• IWW and short-sea shipping transport should increase by 50% by 2050, and 
• high-speed passenger rail traffic should double by 2030 and triple by 2050.  
The uptake of SLT also depends on available infrastructure capacity, innovation and 
digitalisation. One of the key objectives the SSMS advocates for is the completion of the 
comprehensive trans-European transport (‘TEN-T’) network16, fully equipped with the 
European Rail Traffic Management System (‘ERTMS’) by 2050. The SSMS also aims to 
internalise the external costs of transport at the latest by 205017.     
The revised GLs and new TBER will need to be consistent with other EU initiatives flowing 
from the SSMS, notably (i) Regulation (EU) 2024/1679 of 13 June 2024 on Union guidelines 
for the development of the trans-European transport network (amended TEN-T Regulation)18; 
(ii) the Communication from the Commission of 24 June 2021 on NAIADES III: Boosting

4 
future-proof European IWW transport19; (iii) the Communication from the Commission of 5 
November 2025 on Connecting Europe through High-Speed Rail (‘High-Speed Rail Plan’)20; 
and (iv) potential future rules prescribing the internalisation of external costs. 
This initiative is complementary to the EU’s TEN-T policy, which is a key instrument for 
planning and developing a coherent, efficient, multimodal, and high-quality transport 
infrastructure across the EU. By codifying the rules applicable to investment aid to facilities, 
the present initiative facilitates the granting of aid and allows Member States to promote the 
development of facilities, in particular along the TEN-T corridors. By increasing support 
possibilities for interoperability investments, the present initiative also promotes the 
development of an interoperable European transport network.  
Another example of such complementarity concerns the IWW sector, which will in the future 
benefit from codified rules making clearer and more transparent the conditions under which 
Member States can finance it, in line with the NAIADES III programme aiming to boost future-
proof IWW in the EU. By facilitating modernisation and interoperability aid which enhances 
the efficiency and safety of rail operations, this initiative also supports the High-Speed Rail 
Plan unveiled by the Commission in November 2025, which builds on the TEN-T and aims to 
accelerate the development of high-speed rail across the EU, offering passengers significantly 
reduced travel times. Key actions include removing cross-border bottlenecks, a dedicated EU 
financing strategy, legislation supporting the development of a second-hand market for rolling 
stock, cross-border rail ticketing, simplified train driver certification and a 2026 European 
ERTMS Deployment Plan for interoperability21. 
In addition, while the full internalisation of external costs is a long-term project up to 2050, 
revised and new State aid rules are needed already now, as State aid to promote SLT has a 
decisive role to play in terms of achieving the modal shift objectives in the short and medium 
term. As regards in particular the Emission Trading System 2 (‘ETS2’)22, which also covers 
emissions trading for fuel suppliers in the road sector as of 2027, with a possibility to delay the 
entry into force of the system to 2028 which has recently been agreed by the Council23 and the 
European Parliament24, it will partially address the issue of external climate costs of road 
transport, but not that of other external cost categories. According to the Commission 
Handbook25, external costs relating to climate only account for 10-12% of the overall external 
cost difference between rail and road passenger transport and for 3-16.5% of the external cost 
difference between rail or IWW and road freight transport. Accident, congestion, air pollution 
and noise costs are all more important in terms of the external cost difference. The present 
initiative is thus also complementary to the ETS2 as it will serve as an enabling framework,

laying down possibilities for Member States to support SLT as an alternative to road transport. 
In addition, such State support allows Member States to alleviate the increasing costs of road 
transport for businesses and households by promoting more affordable SLT alternatives, 
thereby incentivising the modal shift. 
The Fourth railway package of 2016  
The Fourth railway package is a set of six legislative texts designed to complete the single 
market for rail services (‘Single European Railway Area’)26. Its overarching goal is to make the 
rail sector more competitive. It comprises a market pillar and a technical pillar.  
The market pillar establishes the general right for railway undertakings established in one 
Member State to operate all types of passenger services everywhere in the EU and introduces 
the principle of mandatory tendering for public service contracts in rail. 
There are two types of rail passenger services in the EU: commercial “open access” services 
and services under public service contracts. The latter stem from the fact that many passenger 
lines are not served by the market at terms that Member States consider appropriate (e.g. outside 
rush hours) and/or are not profitable even when an efficient operator undertakes to provide the 
service. Member States are allowed to lay down public service obligations (‘PSOs’) for such 
routes, thereby compensating operators for running loss-generating services. PSOs for rail 
passenger services are regulated and PSO compensation is block exempted by Regulation (EC) 
1370/2007 of 23 October 2007 on public passenger transport services by rail and by road 
(‘Regulation 1370/2007’)27. In 2022, 58.7% of the total passenger kilometres in the EU27 were 
operated under public service contracts28. In 2021, Member States granted EUR 27 billion in 
State aid under Regulation 1370/200729.  
The Fourth railway package liberalised rail services in the passenger segment (the freight 
segment had already been fully liberalised since 200730). It mandated the end of the legal 
monopoly on the passenger market on 1 January 2019 for “open access” services31 and created 
an obligation to tender public service contracts by 3 December 2019, with a transitional period 
until 24 December 2023, until which date public contracts of at most a ten-year duration could 
still be awarded directly32.

6 
The technical pillar of the fourth railway package was designed to boost the competitiveness of 
the railway sector e.g. by reducing administrative burdens. Among others, it aims to: 
• harmonise a large number of national rules; and 
• create a "one stop shop" in the case of operations beyond one single Member State, by 
enabling the European Railways Agency to issue vehicle authorisations valid 
throughout the EU.  
In 2022, in the EU27 new entrants33 had a market share of 49.6% on average in the rail freight 
market (up 10.7 percentage points from 2018), compared to a market share of 12.6% in the 
“open access” rail passenger market (up from 6.0% in 2020) and 21% in the PSO rail passenger 
sector (up from 18.5% in 2020)34.  
The obligatory tendering of public service contracts in the rail passenger transport sector as of 
2024, the effects of which will unfold over the next years, when the last directly-awarded 
contracts will run out, will allow all railway undertakings to bid for public service contracts, 
attaining scale and bringing additional efficiency to these contracts. This regulatory 
development, as well as the liberalisation overall, will increase competition within the rail 
sector as part of SLT and therefore also boost its cost-effectiveness and competitiveness. This 
initiative is therefore complementary to these developments. 
The impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on the data for the railway sector 
Revenues from passenger rail operations decreased by an estimated 41% in 2020 and 36% in 
2021, while those of rail freight decreased by an estimated 12% in 2020 and 10% in 2021. 
Overall, the revenue loss for the railway sector is estimated at about EUR 50 billion for the two 
years35. This was partially offset by State aid in the form of damage compensation payments. 
Data for 2020 and 2021 therefore have to be assessed with particular caution and often have to 
be considered as outliers. This Impact Assessment Report will use data from 2022 to 2024, 
where available, or, where not and there is a risk of skewed data, from 2019 as the last full year 
before the COVID-19 pandemic.  
Other State aid rules 
Important tools to support the Green Deal in the area of State aid are the Guidelines on State 
aid for climate, environmental protection and energy 2022 (‘CEEAG’)36, the GBER and the 
Framework for State aid measures to support the Clean Industrial Deal (‘CISAF’37). These tools 
include compatibility criteria for aid measures related to the reduction of GHG emissions or air 
pollution. The CEEAG and the GBER include specific rules for aid measures for the acquisition 
and leasing of clean vehicles (including those used for rail and IWW). These tools complement 
the present initiative, as the CEEAG stipulate that in case there are specific provisions applying 
to the very same measure in sector specific State aid guidelines, the latter prevail.

<!-- page:12 -->
 
7 
In any event, none of these instruments directly address the issues of the transport sector. The 
objective of State aid covered by the present initiative is to achieve modal shift, which serves 
the purpose of limiting all externalities, including e.g. congestion and accidents, which are also 
lower in SLT. These additional externalities justify additional support. Also, the SLT sector 
consists of a complex network of relationships between facilities and transport operators and 
between different transport modes. A more targeted design of aid measures is therefore 
necessary to efficiently achieve the modal shift objective.  
EU Funding - The Recovery and Resilience and the Connecting Europe Facilities, Cohesion 
policy funds 
The Recovery and Resilience Facility38 mobilised EUR 650 billion to help Member States 
repair the damage caused by the COVID-19 pandemic. It also contributes significantly to the 
objectives of the SSMS through the mobilisation of a total of EUR 87 billion to support 
sustainable mobility. 
The Connecting Europe Facility for Transport aims to support investments in transport 
infrastructure on the TEN-T. In the years 2021-2027, it makes available EUR 25.8 billion to 
support transport infrastructure projects39.  
Cohesion policy funds (European Regional Development Fund, Cohesion Fund, Just Transition 
Fund, Interreg) provide EUR 59.5 billion in co-financing in the years 2021-2027 for 
investments that mitigate territorial disparities in transport infrastructure and that facilitate the 
green and digital transitions in the transport sector. For example, Member States have allocated 
EUR 14.1 billion to clean urban transport infrastructure and rolling stock, EUR 12 billion to 
TEN-T rail (incl. 0.5 billion for ERTMS), and EUR 2.7 billion to rail rolling stock40. Funds for 
mobility investments are also available under the InvestEU programme41. 
The EU Industrial Maritime Strategy 
The European Commission has started preparations for an EU Industrial Maritime Strategy that 
aims to improve the competitiveness of the EU’s waterborne transport sectors (including IWW). 
The Strategy also aims to tackle difficult access to finance for ship buyers, including SMEs and 
mid-cap businesses specifically42. In view of the provisional information in the CfE and taking 
account of the non-legislative nature of this initiative, it appears that the strategy is likely to be 
coherent with and complementary to the present initiative.

1.3. 
Fitness Check 
The RGL were evaluated as part of the 2020 Fitness Check43 of the 2012 State aid 
modernisation (‘SAM’) package44 (the ‘Fitness Check’). 
The Fitness Check contained findings specifically on the excessively narrow scope of the RGL 
limited to operating aid and aid for investments in interoperability. The Fitness Check also set 
out achievements in terms of modal shift resulting from operating aid schemes, as well as 
problems in applying State aid rules to facilities as a category not covered by the RGL. In more 
detail: 
Section 6 of the RGL on aid for the coordination of transport, which covers (i) operating aid for 
external costs reduction, (ii) operating aid for infrastructure use, and (iii) investment aid for 
interoperability, is the part of the RGL that was the most used. No issues were identified during 
the annual sample-based monitoring exercises45, which verified compliance with Commission 
decisions approving aid under the RGL. The Fitness Check found that the scope of section 6 of 
the RGL has proved in many cases too narrow, as it only refers to railway undertakings. As a 
result, the rules laid down in section 6 have been applied by analogy (directly under Article 93 
TFEU) to beneficiaries on the ‘demand side’, such as logistics companies, and to other sectors, 
such as IWW and multimodal transport. Moreover, assessments for example as regards facilities 
have been carried out directly under Article 93 TFEU, which generally increased the duration 
of such cases and added an additional burden, and created some inconsistencies between 
decisions, as will be further detailed below.  
Besides section 6, Member States have used the RGL to a very limited extent and some sections 
have expired in the meantime. An overview of the sections and the compatibility conditions of 
section 6 of the RGLs is provided in Annex 10. 
In addition, as the RGL were not part of the SAM launched in 2012, they do not implement 
systematically and explicitly the applicable common assessment principles46. The SAM 
imposed the publication of all aid awards above EUR 500 000 on national or regional websites. 
The absence of this obligation in the RGL helps explain why there is little data available on 
State aid measures in the SLT sector. In any case, the Fitness Check concluded that “(t)he 
Railway Guidelines (…) should be adapted to SAM.” The revised GL will therefore have to be 
aligned with the general principles of SAM, which apply across the board to the entire corpus

44 SAM was a reform of EU State aid policy launched in 2012. The objectives of SAM were threefold: (1) to foster 
sustainable, smart and inclusive growth in a competitive internal market by means of so-called ‘good aid’ (aid that 
is well-designed, targeted at identified market failures and objectives of common interest, and the least distortive); 
(2) to focus the Commission's ex ante scrutiny on cases with the biggest impact on the internal market; and (3) to 
streamline the rules and provide for faster decisions. 
45 In line with Article 108 (1) TFEU and Articles 21 (1), 26 (1) and 27 of Council Regulation (EU) 2015/1589 of 
13 July 2015 laying down detailed rules for the application of Article 108 of the TFEU, OJ L 248, 24.9.2015 (‘the 
Procedural Regulation’). 
46 https://competition-policy.ec.europa.eu/state-aid/legislation/modernisation_en. The common assessment 
principles that would apply under Article 93 comprise the need for State intervention, appropriateness, 
proportionality and transparency of the aid, incentive effect, avoidance of undue negative effects on competition 
and trade between Member States, and cumulation. In specific cases, schemes may also be made subject to ex post 
evaluation and be subject to monitoring.  

<!-- page:14 -->
 
9 
of State aid legislation. Such an alignment would therefore not be a real option and will 
therefore not be further assessed as an option in this Impact Assessment Report. 
Overall, the Fitness Check concluded that the RGL, dating from 2008, were not fit for purpose 
and needed a full-fledged review to align them with market and regulatory developments, while 
providing additional incentives to encourage a significant modal shift.  
1.4. 
Sustainable development goals  
This initiative contributes to the following sustainable development goals (‘SDGs’)47: SDG 7 
(affordable and clean energy), SDG 13 (Take urgent action to combat climate change and its 
impacts), as it targets the promotion of more climate friendly transport modes, SDG 9 (Build 
resilient infrastructure, promote inclusive and sustainable industrialisation and foster 
innovation), as it promotes the development of infrastructure for clean modes of transport and 
the utilisation of innovative technologies in the relevant transport sectors, SDG 11 (Make cities 
and human settlements inclusive, safe, resilient and sustainable), as it includes sustainable 
transport in particular in cities, and SDG 12 (Ensure sustainable consumption and production 
patterns), as SLT has superior energy efficiency than road transport.   
1.5. 
The future of European Competitiveness  
The challenges of the railway sector were mentioned in the 2024 report by Enrico Letta, which 
acknowledged that ‘the Single European Railway Area has not yet fully materialised. Rail market 
opening […] has been hampered by the fragmentation of markets and infrastructure, the persistence 
of legal and de facto monopolies, as well as formidable technical barriers’, while stressing that ‘the 
strategic development of the transport sector is indispensable for the EU's ambitious agenda to 
transition towards a green economy’48. 
In addition, the report on the future of European competitiveness (‘the Draghi report’)49 
observes that the EU transport sector contributes 5% of the EU’s GDP and jobs, with a further 
four jobs in other sectors of the economy linked to every job in the transport sector. At 12%, 
transport is the third highest category of household expenditure in the EU, after housing and 
food.  
The Draghi report also finds that the transport sector accounts for one quarter of GHG emissions 
in the EU and that transport stands out as the only big sector where emissions have increased 
since 1990 (see Figure 1), with heavy-goods transport on road being costly to decarbonise and 
its emissions therefore particularly hard to abate.

2. 
PROBLEM DEFINITION 
The problems and their drivers set out in this Section are substantiated by evidence from the 
Fitness Check, a study50, an Inception Impact Assessment (‘IIA’)51, a public consultation52, a 
targeted consultation53, a CfE about the TBER specifically54, and the Commission’s case 
practice in applying the RGL and Article 93 TFEU directly. Further information on these 
sources of evidence is provided in Annexes 1, 2, 6, and 8. 
2.1. 
What is the problem? 
The problem is that the modal share of SLT is too low. As further detailed below, over the past 
years, the modal shares of rail and IWW transport have either stagnated or declined slightly, in 
favour of a further increase in the already dominant modal share of road transport (see Figure 
2).

‘study’).

To reach the ambitious objectives of the Green Deal, as further detailed in the SSMS, the modal 
share of SLT will have to increase significantly. Member States will need to implement a broad 
range of measures, including providing public financing to promote SLT, which in many cases 
constitutes State aid and therefore requires the Commission’s approval or coverage under a 
block exemption.  
The problem of the low modal share of SLT is reflected in the following sub-problems: (i) SLT 
operations are not cost-competitive, (ii) the roll out of interoperability technologies is slow, (iii) 
vehicle fleets are old and ageing and (iv) there is insufficient access to facilities & private 
sidings.

2.1.1. Sustainable land transport operations are not cost-competitive  
The study concluded that for most Member States analysed, operations in the rail freight sector 
were overall loss-making56. As explained in the study57, the cost of rail freight transport is 
influenced by multiple factors that impact its profitability and competitiveness. Key factors 
include the train type and travel distance. 
The travel distance is a key factor of competitiveness, since SLT operations typically include 
fixed transhipment costs. In the case of longer operations, these fixed costs can be spread out 
more. According to a study on transhipment technologies in intermodal transport, the cost of a 
multimodal SLT operation is lower than road transport as of distances of 1000 km or greater58. 
If the external costs of transport were fully internalised for both SLT and road transport, SLT 
would become cheaper as of distances of 600 km. Moreover, according to data from Eurostat59, 
in 2023 85.3% of the total tonne-kilometres in road freight operations in the EU came from 
operations which were less than 1000 km long. SLT being cheaper than road transport as of 
600 km, instead of 1000 km, could therefore generate a very significant modal shift.  
Among the train types, a notable finding of the study is that single wagon load (‘SWL’) services 
are structurally loss-making and have costs per tonne-kilometre that are more than twice as high 
as those for block trains. Definitions of SWL vary, but the general understanding is that they 
are rail shipments where the freight carried does not allow the filling of a full train from the 
origin to the destination (which are called block train operations). According to the study, the 
average train length in Europe is 28 wagons, with costs per tonne-kilometre decreasing by an 
estimated 2% per wagon added (the efficiency gains are higher the shorter the train is, i.e. 
adding wagons to shorter trains reduces costs per tonne-kilometre to a higher extent; for SWL 
operations, adding a wagon decreases costs per tonne-kilometre by 12% on average). Due to 
their low profitability, railway undertakings have been downsizing SWL services significantly.  
2.1.2. Slow roll-out of key interoperability technologies  
Transport undertakings face acute difficulties in offering their services across borders within 
the EU. In the rail sector in particular, transport operators need almost as many control systems 
on board as the number of Member States they cross, given that there are 20 existing national 
rail systems. At the same time, 50% of rail freight tonne kilometres cross at least one border in 
the EU60. This is also an obstacle to high-speed rail connections, for example in the form of 
night-trains. According to the study, crossing a border increases the costs of a freight operation 
by 15-20%. To promote the interoperability of trains within the EU, a system of standards for 
 
56 Austria, Germany, Romania, Slovakia, Sweden. In Czechia, Lithuania and Spain rail freight operations at least 
break even.  
57 Chapter 4 of the study.  
58 European Commission, Comparative evaluation of transhipment technologies for intermodal transport and their

13 
the management and interoperation of signalling for railways was developed: ERTMS. The 
deployment of ERTMS should reduce cost61 and improve safety and capacity62.  
However, deployment has been slow so far: at the end of 2023, only about 15% of tracks on the 
core network corridors63 were compatible with ETCS, the signalling and control component of 
ERTMS64. Outside of the core network corridors, the proportion was well below 10%65. At the 
end of 2020, an estimated 17% of vehicles were equipped with ETCS. Between 2015 and 2019, 
it is estimated that 54% of new vehicles were put on the market without being ETCS-
compatible66.  
The EU railway industry has been equally slow to adopt other productivity-enhancing 
technologies such as digital automatic coupling (‘DAC’), a technology that automates the 
process of coupling and de-coupling wagons. The benefits of an EU-wide rollout of DAC have 
been estimated at EUR 760 million per year67. 
The Draghi report notes that urgent investment is needed to roll out technologies projected to 
boost rail capacity, such as ERTMS and DAC.  
2.1.3. Vehicle fleets are old and ageing  
According to data available in 2019 and analysed in the study68, more than 50% of the freight 
wagon fleet in Europe was older than 30 years, with approximately 10% being older than 50 
years. The average age of rolling stock used for passenger transport is even higher69. 
Meanwhile, various experts estimate that freight wagons have a maximum useful life of 40 to 
50 years and passenger rolling stock of 30 to 40 years. On the basis of national vehicle registers, 
the study has found that 38% of freight wagons, 44% of passenger rolling stock, and 22% of 
tractive rolling stock in the EU are older than their maximum useful life and, based on renewal 
rates, these figures are expected to increase over the next decade. Incumbents’ fleets are 
significantly younger than those of new entrants on average70. 
 
61 ERTMS would provide large savings for infrastructure managers, since they can eliminate equipment such as 
trackside signalling, track circuits and axle counters. Still, the triggered reduction of track-access charges would 
be marginal at best for railway undertakings. 
62 ERTMS can increase capacity on existing infrastructure by up to 40% by reducing headway between trains, 
according to UNISIG, an international consortium of 9 companies contributing to the ERTMS specifications.  
63 Core network corridors cover the most important long-distance flows in the core network, which consists of 
those parts of the network which are of the highest strategic importance. 
64 Besides ETCS, the key components of ERTMS are the system used to communicate between trains and traffic 
control centres (GSM-R or FRMCS, a system designed specifically for railways and meant to replace GSM-R) 
and automatic train operations (ATO).

14 
Similarly, as regards IWW transport, the fleet is relatively old, with the majority of vessels built 
before 2000, and ill-equipped to deal with the planned transition to zero-emission mobility71. 
For example, based on an international fleet database (IVR), about half of the active vessels in 
Belgium, Germany and the Netherlands were built more than 50 years ago. In France, these 
vessels represent approximately 80% of the total fleet. There are still some vessels (19% of the 
European fleet) which were built before 1940, particularly in the Netherlands and in Germany72. 
In addition, most operators are small and medium-sized enterprises (‘SMEs’)73. In Germany, in 
2017 the proportion of IWW undertakings that employed up to nine persons amounted to 82%74.  
2.1.4. Insufficient access to facilities and private sidings 
As regards SLT, the relevant facilities consist of (i) facilities used for unimodal railway 
operations (e.g. maintenance facilities for trains or storage facilities), (ii) transhipment facilities 
(in particular sustainable multimodal terminals)75 involving rail and/or IWW and (iii) private 
sidings, i.e. rail tracks that connect industrial sites directly to the general rail network. This 
initiative does not cover the general rail network (tracks), as investments there typically do not 
constitute State aid76.  
The study finds evidence of a suboptimal number of essential (unimodal) rail service facilities 
in certain Member States, as well as a lack of multimodal terminals and insufficient capacity of 
existing terminals. Moreover, the International Union of Railways suggests that a lack of open-
access terminals and lack of terminal capacity are significant bottlenecks77. The SSMS draws 
specific attention to the pronounced scarcity of transhipment facilities, and of multimodal 
terminals in particular, in certain parts of Europe, concluding that tackling those insufficiencies 
should be given the highest priority. 
The study78 also finds that the number of private sidings has been declining across Europe. For 
instance, in Germany, the number of sidings declined from about 13 000 in 1993 to 1 300 in 
2013, while in Austria it declined from 840 in 2010 to 521 in 2020. The study indicates that at 
least part of the journey of most rail freight transport operations in Europe is spent on private 
sidings. This includes for example almost 85% of transport volumes in Germany, and around 
60% in Austria. In most cases private sidings rely on railway undertakings offering SWL 
services, which are typically loss-making. If freight cannot be moved directly to loading points 
(such as industrial sites or warehouses), the transport operation will necessarily require a road

Why is it a problem? 
Road freight transport emits more than four and five times as much CO2 per tonne-km than 
IWW and rail freight transport, respectively (see Figure 3).

Two important reasons for the comparatively high GHG emissions of road transport and 
aviation are the high energy use (compared to both rail and IWW)81 and the high degree of 
fossil fuel use in propulsion. As of 2022, 82% of train-km operated in the EU used electricity 
as its source of energy and 52% of energy in rail transport was obtained from renewable 
sources82. 
Other external costs in the transport sector include air pollution, noise pollution, accidents, and 
congestion. For both passenger and freight transport, SLT modes involve significantly lower 
external costs than road transport (see Table 1 and Table 2). Data on external costs by transport 
mode and Member State have been compiled and published by the European Commission, most 
recently in 2019 in the Commission Handbook83.

2.3. 
What are the problem drivers? 
This section covers the main problem drivers that are contributing to the low and stagnating 
modal share of SLT. The transport sector is one of the biggest economic sectors, responsible 
for 5% of EU GDP85. The modal share of SLT is the result of market failures (such as negative 
externalities, coordination failures and access to finance difficulties) and regulatory failures 
(such as insufficient internalisation of negative externalities, regulatory barriers to 
interoperability and unsuitable State aid rules). The lack of sufficiently well-designed and 
targeted State aid rules that facilitate and incentivise support to SLT contributes to the chronic 
 
84 Well-to-Tank refers to all emissions caused in the production of the energy that is used by the transport mode 
concerned, such as in the extraction of energy sources, the processing and transmission of energy, and the building 
of energy plants.  
85 Section 1.5.  

<!-- page:23 -->
 
18 
lack of competitiveness of SLT compared to road transport and is one of the regulatory failures 
responsible for the low modal share of SLT.  
2.3.1. Market failures 
Problem Driver 1: Negative externalities of road transport 
The main market failure for SLT is the fact that external costs of transport are insufficiently 
internalised into the costs of transport operators and thus not reflected in the prices charged to 
customers (e.g. passengers or freight forwarders).  
In 2016, the road transport sector in the EU-27 caused EUR 532.0 billion in total external costs, 
while paying EUR 307.8 billion in taxes and charges, leaving a remaining cost to society of 
EUR 224.2 billion. Rail and IWW combined caused a total of EUR 19.3 billion in external 
costs, while paying EUR 18.0 billion in taxes and charges, leaving a remaining cost to society 
of EUR 1.3 billion86. Available data on external costs suggests that the remaining cost to society 
has worsened since 2016. Estimates on external costs in 2022 show that they have increased by 
8.5% for road and by only 4.5% for rail, compared to 2016 levels87.  
The external costs of transport are insufficiently internalised, leading to a very substantial 
implicit subsidy to the road sector of more than EUR 200 billion per year.  
According to the study, the 50% differential of external costs between rail and road close to or 
even exceeds 30% of the total rail freight costs in multiple countries, including Czech Republic, 
Germany, Lithuania, Poland, Romania, and Spain. While current State aid spending levels of 
around EUR 4.8 billion per year (see Section 1.1) fall far short of covering the external cost gap 
of more than EUR 200 billion per year, these figures do show that, where operating aid is 
granted, it has a significant impact on the cost base of the beneficiaries. Given an elasticity of 
rail volumes to rail prices of -1.0 (see section 6.1.1), operating aid covering 50% of the external 
cost differential can have a significant impact on the prices and thus on the modal share of rail 
transport.  
Problem Driver 2: Coordination failures in achieving interoperability 
The slow adoption of ERTMS and other interoperability technologies can be explained by the 
initial lack of any private benefits from installing these technologies and the need for 
coordination between market participants. ERTMS needs to be installed alongside track 
infrastructure and on-board all rolling stock using that infrastructure. In the absence of rolling 
stock with on-board ERTMS units, infrastructure managers do not have any incentive to install 
ERTMS equipment alongside railway tracks, and vice versa.  
The initial private benefits of ERTMS are minimal because it only leads to productivity gains 
if all railway undertakings active on a certain network, as well as the infrastructure manager, 
have equipped ERTMS. This requires a coordination of investment activities.  
 
86 State of play of internalisation in the European transport sector, 2019: https://op.europa.eu/en/publication-
detail/-/publication/696d402f-a45a-11e9-9d01-01aa75ed71a1.   
87 The author of the existing Commission Handbook has partially updated and extended the external cost figures 
from 2016 to 2022, using data from Eurostat, the EEA and the European Union Agency for Railways 
(https://uic.org/support-activities/economics/article/external-costs). The update does not include new figures for 
IWW. 

<!-- page:24 -->
 
19 
The rollout of DAC faces coordination failures as well. According to the study, around 80% of 
the existing freight wagons in Europe belong to signatories of the ‘General Contract of Use for 
Wagons’ and are enlisted for mutual exchange between railway undertakings; this means that 
freight wagons belonging to the signatories end up forming trains with wagons of competitors. 
Accordingly, a railway undertaking that equips its wagons with DAC cannot be sure that it will 
benefit from the efficiencies gained. Indeed, if a train is composed of a mix of DAC-compatible 
and manual wagons, it is not possible for any digital coupling to take place These arrangements 
have resulted in Europe being the last continent where manual coupling is still common.  
Similar market dynamics affect the roll-out of other technologies in rail, but also in other 
sustainable transport modes88. Taking into account these difficulties, in its case practice, the 
Commission has accepted aid intensities for some interoperability investments higher than 50% 
(which are in principle foreseen in the RGL) in at least 12 cases, in some cases even up to 100%.  
Problem Driver 3: Access to finance difficulties for smaller companies and new entrants 
In view of the ongoing liberalisation of the rail sector in the passenger segment, incumbent 
operators benefit from network effects and economies of scale. The near-monopoly occupation 
of the rail network makes it difficult for new entrants and smaller companies89 to enter the 
market. While it has grown over the past few years, the market share of new entrants is still 
only 12.6% in the open access passenger segment and 21% in the national PSO passenger 
market.  
The benefits of incumbency are especially acute as regards the acquisition of vehicles. First, as 
concerns the passenger segment, it is common for public service contracts to include the 
provision of rolling stock, leading to rolling stock used by incumbents mainly being financed 
by public service compensation (for the duration of those contracts). Second, as concerns both 
the passenger and freight segments, incumbents (i) being State-owned enterprises, benefit – 
according to certain research findings – from an implicit State guarantee90; and (ii) are often 
vertically integrated, combining the infrastructure manager and the railway undertaking in a 
single company.  
Data specific to the EU rail sector is difficult to find, but more general findings suggest that the 
implicit State guarantee may lower financing costs by several percentage points (see section 
6.4.1). As regards vertical integration, a holding company may raise debt at group level, 
blending the risk-free conditions of the infrastructure manager with the riskier conditions of the 
railway undertaking.  
The findings of one established credit rating agency confirm that those factors lead to 
incumbents having easier access to finance for acquiring rolling stock and better financing

conditions91. The CfE on the EU Industrial Maritime Strategy also mentions the difficulty of 
access to finance for family-owned SMEs and mid-cap businesses in the IWW sector.  
Rolling stock and IWW vessels are core assets and have long depreciation periods. Forecasting 
the profitability of such investments is subject to many uncertainties. Therefore, smaller and 
less established actors might not be able to raise sufficient funds on the market for such 
investments. In the public consultation, 66% (46 out of 70)92 and 69% (48 out of 70)93 of the 
respondents considered that railway undertakings still face difficulties in accessing freight and 
passenger rolling stock respectively. The study found that in the rail sector ‘the constraints [in 
accessing finance for rolling stock] are mainly financial and small operators in particular may 
not have access to credit on competitive terms’. The IWW sector is composed mainly of SMEs, 
and these companies also more frequently face access to finance difficulties94. 
The market failure of difficult access to finance for SMEs has been established more generally, 
across sectors. The Impact Assessment Report on the Commission Guidelines on State aid to 
promote risk finance investments95 noted (in a more general context) that ‘SMEs, especially 
when they are young, are often unable to demonstrate their credit-worthiness or the soundness 
of their business plans to investors’. The 2024 EU Competitiveness report96 highlights the need 
for targeted support to SMEs. 
2.3.2. Regulatory failures 
Problem driver 4: Insufficient internalisation of negative externalities 
There is currently no legislation nor legislative proposal aiming to fully internalise the external 
costs in the transport sector. At EU level a pertinent initiative is ETS 2, but it only concerns 
external costs related to climate, which only account for 10-12% of the overall external cost 
difference between rail and road passenger transport and for 3-16.5% of the external cost

so (nor does it require Member States to apply tolls in the first place).  
Problem driver 5: regulatory barriers to interoperability 
No EU-wide binding deadline exists for phasing out national signalling systems, which reduces 
the incentive to upgrade the network infrastructure, since trains can continue to operate under 
older signalling systems. Even if ERTMS is installed trackside, this set-up may prolong dual-
system operations, inflating costs and maintaining cross-border barriers.  
Three different track gauges exist in the EU: the broad gauge, used in the Baltics and Finland 
(1 520 – 1 524 mm difference between rails), the Iberian broad gauge (1 668 mm) used in Spain 
and Portugal, and the standard gauge used in rest of the EU (1 435 mm). Different track gauges 
require transhipment or an axle change, which both add considerable costs. Crossing a border 
increases the costs of a freight operation by 15-20% on average (see section 2.1.2), but the study 
found that between France and Spain, costs increase by 38.6% at border crossing points where 
transhipment is required, and by 73.2% at points where an axle change is required. While there 
is no requirement to standardise track gauges across the EU, Member States do have to ensure 
that any new railway line of the core network and the extended core network of the TEN-T 
network provides for the European standard nominal track gauge of 1 435 mm99. In addition, 
the military mobility package100 foresees that when upgrading dual-use sections of military 
mobility corridors, Member States shall prioritise migration to the EU standard gauge101.  
Different electricity systems exist in the EU, both in terms of current type (direct and alternating 
current), in voltage (varying kilovolt) and frequency levels varying hertz levels for alternating 
current). According to the study, the main systems in use are 15 kV 16.7 Hz AC, applicable to 
Germany, Austria, Switzerland, Sweden and Norway; 25 kV 50 Hz AC in Northern France, 
Portugal and in some parts of Belgium, Netherlands and Luxembourg; 3 kV DC in Italy, Russia, 
Poland, Spain and Belgium and finally 1.5 kV DC in Southern France and the Netherlands. At 
this stage, there is no plan at EU level to unify existing voltage systems, although the use of 
alternating current is mandated for new high-speed rail lines102.

If rolling stock is equipped with multi-voltage systems (which adds cost), switching to a

Problem driver 6: State aid rules not suitable to creating modal shift 
The current State aid legal framework does not sufficiently empower Member States to invest 
in SLT and generate modal shift, because (i) the scope of the rules is too narrow, (ii) the 
compatibility conditions are too strict, and (iii) the administrative burdens are high, in particular 
because every measure has to be notified.  
First, the scope of the RGL is too narrow because it excludes types of aid and modes of transport 
that contribute to modal shift. The current RGLs only cover (i) the rail sector, to the exclusion 
of IWW transport and short-sea shipping; (ii) operating and interoperability aid, to the exclusion 
of other kinds of investment aid such as aid to facilities and private sidings; and (iii) transport 
operators (i.e. railway undertakings), to the exclusion of transport organisers such as freight 
forwarders and logistics companies. 
In the public consultation, 64.8% of respondents104 considered that the scope of the RGL should 
include other pivotal actors such as IWW transport operators (with clear majorities of public 
authorities, railway undertakings and civil society in favour). The public consultation also 
confirmed that the absence of codified rules for State aid to facilities and infrastructure (91% 
of those expressing a view) is considered a problem by stakeholders. All categories of 
respondents (business associations, public authorities, civil society and individual businesses) 
shared this view. Of the fifteen stakeholders105 providing feedback on the IIA, 7 specifically 
mentioned in their replies that they welcome the proposed broadening of the scope of the rules 
to cover other actors involved in the multimodal chain, such as logistics operators106.  
The analysis of past decision practice confirms that these other types of aid and beneficiaries 
are important contributors to modal shift. Out of the about 258 decisions issued between 2008 
and 2024 as regards SLT, a significant proportion have fallen outside the scope of the RGL 
(20% of all cases concern IWW, another 20% concern facilities and private sidings and 3.3% 
the acquisition of vehicles, all of which fall outside the scope of the RGL). 
The fact that compatibility criteria for the above-mentioned measures are not set out in the 
RGLs or other instruments and are not readily available for Member States to apply adds to 
legal uncertainty and administrative burden, since it makes it necessary for Member States to 
research existing decisional practice and how it has evolved over time.   
Article 93 TFEU provides that aid in the form of public service compensation for land transport 
shall be compatible with the internal market. The rules on public services in the land transport 
sector set out in Regulation 1370/2007 apply only to passenger transport, while the existing 
Commission decision and framework for services of general economic interest (‘SGEI’)107 do

not apply to land transport. Rail freight is thus one of the few (if not the only) economic sectors 
with no secondary legislation on how to assess compensation for public services under State 
aid rules. There has so far only been one decision108 adopted as regards rail freight PSOs.  
Respondents to the public consultation were split as regards the rules for rail freight public 
services: while the majority (32)109 of respondents abstained from giving an opinion, 16 were 
in favour of introducing State aid rules regarding rail freight public services110 and 23 against111.   
Second, the compatibility conditions of the RGL are too strict to enable a level playing field 
between SLT and road transport.  
For example, to address the market failures faced by operators implementing interoperability 
investments, the RGL in section 6 allow in principle aid up to a threshold of 50% of the 
interoperability investment costs (so-called eligible costs; other conditions also apply)112. 
However, this aid intensity appears to have been insufficient, and the Commission has accepted 
aid intensities for interoperability investments higher than 50% in at least 12 cases, in some 
cases even up to 100%. 
The consultations confirmed the inadequacy of the current rules. In response to the IIA, 20% 
of the stakeholders (3 out of 15) noted, without being asked specifically about this topic, that 
current aid intensities113 are too low, calling for an aid intensity of 100% for interoperability 
aid114. The public consultation confirmed this view with 67% of respondents considering the 
interoperability aid intensity too low and 66% considering the operating aid intensity too low115.   
Another example are the rules contained by the RGL that allow to address the market failure 
created by the lack of internalisation of external costs. Specifically, the RGL allow aid up to 
50% of the avoided external costs116 and 100% of the additional infrastructure costs, with a 
combined ceiling of 30% of the total cost of rail transport (other conditions also apply).  
In their response to the public consultation, stakeholders considered that these aid ceilings are 
insufficient to make rail and IWW transport competitive vis-à-vis road transport: 80% of the 
 
undertakings entrusted with the operation of services of general economic interest, OJ L 7, 11.1.2012. 
Communication from the Commission — European Union framework for State aid in the form of public service 
compensation, OJ C 8, 11.1.2012, (‘the SGEI Framework’). Under the general SGEIs principles, Member States 
may entrust an undertaking with a public service obligation to the extent that there is a genuine need for the service, 
which is not being provided or not provided satisfactorily (e.g. in terms of price or quality) by undertakings 
operating under normal market conditions.

respondents (56 out of 70)117, including majorities across all categories of respondents, 
considered that the total cost ceiling was too low. 
The study118 corroborates the stakeholder views. It finds that the 50% threshold for the external 
cost differential that can be covered by aid would, in most analysed Member States119, not be 
sufficient to cover the cost differential between rail and road freight.  
Finally, industry stakeholders and Member States have regularly called for the introduction of 
a block exemption120, which exists for most other sectors, to reduce the administrative burdens 
associated to the notification procedure. Each notification procedure requires multiple 
exchanges between the Commission services and Member State authorities to gather the 
necessary data with a view to ascertaining that the compatibility criteria are satisfied. Industry 
faces uncertainty and delays, resulting in opportunity costs.  
Yet the Commission has a long-standing practice concerning certain support measures for SLT 
with limited distortion risk. As established in the Fitness Check, certain categories of cases 
concerning aid to rail and other SLT sectors have routinely led to non-objection decisions (in 
particular aid for external costs reduction, aid for infrastructure use and aid for interoperability, 
but also aid for facilities such as intermodal terminals and private sidings). For such measures, 
compatibility conditions could be laid down in a block exemption. 
More information on the potential administrative savings of a block exemption, including 
opportunity cost savings for businesses, is provided in Annex 8. According to the data in Annex 
8, over the 2022-2024 period the opportunity cost savings to businesses of a block exemption 
would have been equivalent to a 0.6% increase in State aid spending.  
2.4. 
How likely is the problem to persist? 
The problem identified in Section 2.1 will persist unless specifically addressed among others 
by the current revision of the State aid rules, i.e. the present initiative.  
As set out in Section 2.1, the modal share of SLT is low and has been stagnating. According to 
the 2022 Strategic Foresight Report121, the demand for transport will continue to grow in view 
of forecasted population growth and higher living standards. In the EU, road passenger and 
freight transport are expected to grow by around 21% and 45% respectively by 2050.  
Some recent market or policy developments could help to increase market efficiencies and bring 
about market-driven investments. For example, the effects of the obligatory tendering of public 
service contracts in the rail passenger transport sector will unfold over the next years, when the 
last directly awarded contracts will run out. It will give the possibility to all railway 
undertakings to bid for public service contracts and is likely to attract some private investments 
in the passenger rail transport sector. Another example is the introduction of the ETS2, i.e. an 
emission trading system for the road sector, to be introduced at the earliest in 2027. ETS2 will

trade unions, 5 other respondents. 
118 Pages 151-155. 
119 Austria, Czechia, Germany, Italy, Lithuania, Poland, Romania, Slovakia and Spain.

121 Communication from the Commission to the European Parliament and the Council, 2022 Strategic Foresight 
Report, COM 2022 289 final.  

<!-- page:30 -->
 
25 
increase the costs of road transport and could conversely increase the competitiveness of more 
sustainable transport modes, which could thus attract more private investments.  
However, these regulatory and market developments are not a panacea. The profit margins in 
the rail sector are low, so the obligation to tender public service contracts is not likely to attract 
massive investments in the sector. As to the ETS2, its actual implementation start is uncertain, 
as outlined in section 1.2. In addition, this system would only help address a limited part of the 
market failure regarding the non-internalisation of external costs, since it would help internalise 
only climate costs.  
Therefore, the market inefficiencies relating to a large part of the external costs, as well as the 
coordination and access to finance issues that hamper the development of SLT (not addressed 
by any ongoing or planned regulatory developments) are expected to continue.  
State aid, designed to be limited to the minimum necessary, is complementary to market-driven 
investments. It is expected to crowd in private investment by being granted when necessary to 
unlock private investments. For example, the definition of the amount of permissible aid to 
investments in facilities and private sidings will be based in principle on a funding gap122 
analysis checking that aid is indispensable and that it is limited to what is necessary for the 
investment to go ahead. Similarly, limiting the aid for the acquisition of vehicles only to 
guarantees means that the aid beneficiaries still must find financing on the market and pay it 
off themselves. 
As regards specifically the regulatory failure related to the sub-optimal design of State aid rules 
applicable to SLT, this would continue absent the initiative. First, even though additional types 
of aid and aid to different actors than the ones specified in the RGL have been approved directly 
under Article 93 of the Treaty, the full set of measures and actors eligible for aid would not be 
codified and would therefore remain untransparent for Member States and companies.  
Second, as regards the conditions applied for assessing compatibility, for those measures falling 
within the RGL, no adaptation would be possible without a modification of the RGL. Aid 
possibilities would therefore remain insufficient. Moreover, for measures not falling within the 
RGL today, the only option outside the proposed revision of the rules would be the adjustment 
of case practice on a case-by-case basis. This would be burdensome and untransparent for 
Member States and companies. Moreover, over time it would risk generating an inconsistent 
case practice to the detriment of the level playing field in the internal market and competition. 
Finally, with respect to the administrative burden, absent a specific dedicated block exemption 
and codification of rules for a larger set of measures in the revised GLs, the current situation of 
an obligation to notify virtually all State aid measures in the sector and to carry out assessments 
on the basis of either outdated rules or a variety of case practice, which developed over the 
years, will keep the administrative burden high, raising the cost of aid schemes and discouraging 
Member States from putting them into place.  
3. 
WHY SHOULD THE EU ACT? 
As indicated in section 1.1, the Commission has exclusive competence for setting out the 
conditions under which State aid may be considered to be compatible with the internal market. 
Therefore, by definition, action at national level is not possible. 
 
122 A funding gap calculation estimates all expected revenues and costs of an investment, discounting them to the 
current date to determine which amount of money is missing to make the investment sufficiently attractive for a 
private investor to engage in it. The missing amount of money is called the funding gap. 

<!-- page:31 -->
 
26 
4. 
OBJECTIVES: WHAT IS TO BE ACHIEVED? 
4.1. 
General objective 
The general objective of the revision of the RGL and the adoption of the TBER is to increase 
the modal share of SLT, in line with Green Deal and SSMS targets.  
4.2. 
Specific objectives 
The initiative will contribute to the achievement of the general objective by pursuing the 
following four specific objectives: 
Specific objective 1: Reduce the external cost gap between road and SLT (SO1) 
As described in section 2.3, the most severe market failure affecting the competitiveness of SLT 
is the lack of internalisation of external costs in land transport. The revised rules concerning 
State aid to SLT should contribute to reducing the external cost gap between road and SLT. The 
improvement of the overall competitiveness of SLT should then also contribute to the other 
specific objectives; for example, a modal shift generated by cheaper SLT services could 
increase demand for facilities, lowering the barrier to profitability for such infrastructure.  
Specific objective 2: Accelerate the rollout of interoperability technologies (SO2) 
The revised rules on State aid to SLT should contribute to incentivising a faster and wider 
rollout of interoperability technologies, to increase productivity and safety while lowering 
costs. The wide adoption of interoperability technologies such as ERTMS and DAC could 
increase productivity and lower costs significantly. Fewer separate systems e.g. for controlling 
and signalling could increase economies of scale (more unified vehicles in manufacturing) and 
lower vehicle costs, which would facilitate access to vehicles for SMEs and new entrants, thus 
creating synergies with SO3. The wide adoption of DAC would lower siding and shunting costs 
and, ceteris paribus, increase demand for such operations, which would lower the barrier to 
profitability of infrastructure such as shunting yards and improve access to facilities (SO4).    
Specific objective 3: Improve access to finance for SMEs and new entrants (SO3)  
The revised rules on State aid to SLT should contribute to level the playing field between large 
enterprises and incumbents, on the one hand, and new entrants and smaller companies, on the 
other, by improving access to finance for the latter. Achieving this objective would also 
contribute positively to SO2 since it facilitates fleet renewal, and newer vehicles are more likely 
to be equipped with interoperability technologies such as ERTMS.  
Specific objective 4: Improve access to facilities and private sidings (SO4) 
The revised rules on State aid to SLT should contribute to addressing bottlenecks in SLT 
infrastructure by improving access to facilities and private sidings.   
4.3. 
Intervention logic 
An overview of the problems, the problem drivers and the general and specific objectives, 
relevant to the present initiative, is provided in Figure 5.  

<!-- page:32 -->
 
27 
Figure 5: Intervention logic 
 
5. 
WHAT ARE THE AVAILABLE POLICY OPTIONS?   
As set out in Section 2 and summarised in Figure 5, the low modal share of SLT is explained 
notably by interoperability challenges when operating across borders, the typically lower cost 
of road transport compared to SLT operations, a lack of quality or availability of facilities and 
ageing fleets. As evidenced in Section 1.1, there is case practice in all these areas since 2008. 
As explained in Section 2.3, there are several market and regulatory failures at the root of this 
problem. The fact that the RGL do not sufficiently enable Member States to grant State aid 
which contributes to a modal shift towards SLT is only one of these failures.  
As explained in Section 2, State aid rules can tackle drivers unrelated to State aid rules only 
partially. It is not in the remit of this Impact Assessment Report to assess whether the recourse 
to State aid is the most efficient means to increase modal shift, as compared to other solutions. 
This is a broader policy question on which EU policy makers have taken a position, notably in 
the SSMS, and will continue to do so, as appropriate. This report assesses the options available 
for the design of State aid rules applicable to the SLT sector, so that they contribute to the modal 
shift, within the framework of the intervention logic.  
5.1. 
Policy options and the intervention logic 
Different option bundles have been defined around the problems and the corresponding specific 
objectives, as depicted in Figure 6, relating to the scope and compatibility conditions of the 
revised GLs and TBER and taking into account the case practice.  

<!-- page:33 -->
 
28 
Figure 6: Linking the options to the problem and specific objectives  
 
Option Bundle A determines the scope, i.e. the set of activities and investments for which 
compatibility conditions will be laid down in the new rules and is in a way a “precondition” for 
Option Bundles B and C, which deal with the specific design of those conditions. Option Bundle 
B sets out different options for the compatibility conditions for those aid categories already 
contained in the RGL. These are operating and interoperability aid measures. Option Bundle C 
relates to aid for the acquisition of vehicles, a newly codified aid category under the initiative. 
Operating and interoperability aid measures have as their primary purpose (but by no means as 
their only impact) to address the cost competitiveness between SLT and road transport, while 
aid for the acquisition of vehicles has as its primary purpose (but not as its only impact) to 
reduce the access to finance issue and rejuvenate the vehicle fleets. 
The choice of the compatibility conditions for these types of aid is the most significant issue 
and different policy choices are possible, as confirmed by stakeholder reactions (public 
authorities, business associations and individual companies) in particular during the targeted 
consultation123. Outside of these aid categories, no significant policy options for other types of 
aid were identified. This is true in particular for aid to facilities and private sidings, as case 
practice and the evidence gathered in the framework of the initiative including the consultations 
is sufficiently homogeneous to determine the appropriate compatibility conditions and does not 
require any policy choices. The same is true for other ancillary categories of aid, for which aid 
intensities in the case practice are consistent.  
5.2. 
What is the baseline in relation to which options are assessed? 
The baseline is that the RGL would continue to apply and there would be no block exemption 
for aid for the coordination of transport.  
In the baseline scenario, there would be no codification of compatibility conditions in line with 
case practice and no block exemption for such aid measures. The RGL would continue to spell 
 
123 Annex 2, Point 4.4.  

<!-- page:34 -->
 
29 
out compatibility conditions only for three types of measures (aid for external costs reduction, 
aid for infrastructure use and aid for interoperability) and only for railway undertakings.  
Under the baseline, Member States can provide operating aid in the form of aid for 
infrastructure use (this aid can cover 100% of the difference in infrastructure costs between less 
and more sustainable transport modes) and aid for external costs (this aid can cover 50% of the 
external cost difference between less and more sustainable transport modes)124. For 
interoperability aid, the RGL presume that aid intensities not exceeding 50% of the eligible 
costs are justified but provide for the possibility to exceptionally accept higher aid intensities 
upon due justification (12 precedents approving aid intensities between 85 and 100%). These 
parameters from the RGL applicable to operating and interoperability aid would be mirrored in 
State aid decisions directly under Article 93 TFEU concerning aid provided to beneficiaries 
other than railway undertakings.  
As regards investment aid falling outside the RGL, the maximum aid allowed for investments 
in facilities would be defined based on a funding gap analysis, as has been consistently the case 
in the past (with the exception of very few outliers in particular in the more distant past, which 
relied on aid intensities). For private sidings, the aid amount would be defined both based on a 
maximum aid intensity and a funding gap analysis, as in the past aid intensities were regularly 
applied, but in recent years a funding gap analysis was almost consistently applied. The aid 
intensity would always be assessed in addition to the funding gap analysis but would not 
constitute the determining factor for the level of the aid and very high aid intensities, even above 
80%, would be accepted, when the funding gap analysis justifies them, in line with the most 
recent case practice.  
As concerns aid for the acquisition of vehicles, aid intensities between 20 and 50% would be 
approved, while for aid for the modernisation and adaptation of vehicles and equipment, aid 
intensities ranging between 20 and 40% would be accepted, as in most cases in the past. 
According to the “EU Reference Scenario 2020”125, rail freight traffic is expected to grow by 
38.4% by 2030 and by 83.1% by 2050, compared to 2015 levels. IWW freight traffic is expected 
to grow by 18.8% and 36.6% respectively. While both passenger and freight rail traffic are 
projected to grow faster than road transport, driven in particular by the gradual completion of 
the TEN-T network, the projected growth rates of rail fall well short of the SSMS freight 
transport targets, namely to grow by 50% by 2030 and double by 2050. The prognosis is even 
worse in the IWW sector, as IWW is expected to grow less than road and fall well short of the 
SSMS targets of growing by 25% by 2030 and by 50% by 2050.  
The EU Reference Scenario 2020 provides a projection of the likely future developments, for 
example with respect to modal shares, absent the implementation of any policy initiatives post 
2020. Thus, the EU Reference Scenario 2020 does not consider initiatives which were adopted 
by the Commission after 2020, which could be relevant in this context. For example, the EU’s 
TEN-T policy is a key instrument for planning and developing a coherent, efficient, multimodal, 
and high-quality transport infrastructure across the EU and could help address the issue of 
insufficient access to facilities. Similarly, the ETS2 aims at internalising at least part of the 
 
124 For both types of operating aid, the RGL impose an additional cap: the aid must not exceed 30% of the total 
cost of transport. The notion ‘total cost of transport’ is however unclear and has not been applied fully consistently 
in the case practice. 
125 The EU Reference Scenario 2020 is the baseline scenario, based on the policy framework in place in 2020,on 
which specific policy scenarios and variants used to assess options informing the policy initiatives in the European 
Green Deal (see footnote 13) have been developed. https://energy.ec.europa.eu/data-and-analysis/energy-
modelling/eu-reference-scenario-2020_en.   

<!-- page:35 -->
 
30 
external costs (climate costs) of road transport thereby helping to address the issue of lesser 
competitiveness of SLT. As set out in section 1.2, the present initiative is complementary to 
these other initiatives, but many factors influence the modal share of SLT and it is complex to 
isolate the effect of any specific intervention. This has been taken into account in section 9. 
Still, it is expected that these other initiatives only partially address the problems and problem 
drivers, as set out in section 2.3. 
While the evolution in modal shares of various transport modes is therefore influenced by a 
wider economic, policy and regulatory context, the baseline scenario in the present initiative 
would result in a lagging modal shift and in State aid rules not contributing to the SSMS 
objectives being met.  
5.3. 
Policy options: scope of the revised Guidelines and TBER (A)  
The design of the options bundle A takes into account the conclusions from the Fitness Check 
(Section 1.3), the case practice and further stakeholder consultations (Section 2.3.2 and Annex 
2) that the current rules are too narrow. The starting point for the design of the options in bundle 
A is the status quo (revised GLs limited to the forms and types of aid covered by the RGL, no 
block exemption). With that starting point, different possibilities of enlargement of the scope 
of the rules were envisaged to address the stakeholders’ input and case practice covering further 
areas and types of aid than those covered by the RGL and this in an increasing order of 
magnitude, from the narrowest overall (A1) to the realistically widest (A3). 
5.3.1. Option A1: Scope of the GLs extended to cover current case 
practice, TBER limited to measures under the RGLs 
Under Option A1 the scope of the revised GLs would be extended to cover (i) transport 
undertakings of all SLT modes (in addition to rail: IWW and multimodal transport, including 
with a short-sea shipping leg); (ii) shippers and transport organisers who contract services from 
those transport undertakings; and (iii) other types of aid for all SLT modes, where there is case 
practice or a clearly identified need for State aid. As substantiated by stakeholder input and the 
study, this notably includes start-up aid for passenger night trains and in the freight segment, 
aid for facilities and private sidings, and aid for the acquisition of vehicles. This is the narrowest 
scope of the revised GLs that could be envisaged, considering the significant case practice that 
has developed in the SLT sector outside the RGL126. The TBER would only block exempt those 
aid measures in the rail sector for which there is experience with compatibility criteria under 
the RGL, i.e. operating aid for external cost and infrastructure use and investment aid for 
interoperability. Only for these categories of aid there is a clear legal framework, which has 
ensured full consistency. 
5.3.2. Option A2: Scope of both the GLs and the TBER to cover current 
case practice  
Under Option A2, the scope of the revised GLs would be the same as under Option A1. The 
block exemption would cover all aid measures where the Commission has developed a case 
practice, even if that case practice is not as abundant or homogenous as in the case of aid 
covered by the RGL. The scope of the TBER under Option A2 would cover the same sectors 
and types of measures as the scope of the revised GLs, as supported by virtually all stakeholders 
 
126 Section 2.3 and Annex 7.  

<!-- page:36 -->
 
31 
during the consultation on the draft legal texts. It would set notification thresholds, above which 
measures would have to be notified under the revised GLs. 
5.3.3. Option A3: Scope of both the GLs and the TBER to cover current 
case practice and rules for public services for rail freight in the 
revised GLs 
Under Option A3, the TBER and the revised GLs would have the same scope as under Option 
A2. The revised GLs would additionally contain explicit rules on public services in the rail 
freight sector to clarify how the general principles governing compensation for the discharge 
of SGEIs apply to rail freight. This is one of very few economic sectors with no secondary 
legislation on how to assess compensation for public services under State aid rules127. Despite 
the fact that only one decision on this subject has been approved in the past, some stakeholders 
(in particular Member States) put this option forward during the consultation activities on the 
initiative.128  
The inclusion of rules on public services in the rail freight sector in Option A3 reflects the way 
of constructing the A options as concerns the scope (namely based on the case practice and 
enlarging the scope of the rules in an increasing order of magnitude). It also reflects the more 
limited stakeholder feedback on this point, despite consultation on public services from the 
inception of the initiative.

options:
