interoperability aid (B) 
The B Options concern the compatibility conditions for operating and interoperability aid 
measures under the revised GLs and TBER. They span from the most restrictive conditions that 
could be envisaged in Option B1 to the most permissive conditions in Option B3. 
 
127 Section 2.3.  
128 Annex 2, Points 4.3 and 4.4. 

<!-- page:37 -->
 
32 
5.4.1. Option B1: Higher aid intensities and a single operating aid 
category, with strong safeguards  
First, Option B1 would simplify the operating aid assessment and increase the aid intensity of 
both operating aid and interoperability aid, based on experience with case practice. Aid for 
infrastructure use would no longer be a separate operating aid category, as in the past it proved 
difficult to assess in case practice and Member States often notified aid for infrastructure costs 
as aid for external cost. Instead, there would be one methodology to define the amount of 
operating aid that could be granted to reward SLT for the lower external costs it causes. 
As concerns aid intensities, the TBER would codify the existing RGL cap for external cost aid, 
with an aid intensity cap of 50%. The aid intensity cap under the revised GLs would be between 
50 and 100%, reflecting on the one hand the need to further close the external cost gap between 
SLT and road transport, as SLT modal shares are low and stagnating, and on the other hand the 
concern that aid should not go beyond compensating SLT for this cost gap129. This was reflected 
in stakeholder responses to the targeted consultation on the draft revised GLs, where 18 
stakeholders (including several Member States, as well as a rail association and two railway 
undertakings) called for higher aid intensities. The same rules would apply also to IWW, 
building directly on the case practice since 2008. 
As concerns the aid intensity for interoperability aid, in view of the acute market failures130, 
under Option B1 higher aid intensities than the current 50% would be set, under both the revised 
GLs and the TBER, with aid intensities up to 90% for key interoperability technologies, 
building on 12 precedents in the case practice, where aid intensities between 85 and 100% were 
accepted. This was also advocated by 9 stakeholders131 in the targeted consultation on the draft 
revised GLs, where suggested aid intensities for ERTMS and DAC varied between 90 and 
100%. Moreover, a closed list of the technologies profiting from these aid intensities as defined 
based on case practice is specifically laid down for the TBER.  
Second, safeguards would be introduced to ensure the proportionality of the higher aid 
intensities.  
For operating aid, Member States would have to address capacity constraints on the 
infrastructure of the supported SLT mode (for example access to train paths or transshipment 
terminals). Such constraints would prevent the expansion of SLT even if it was cost competitive 
compared to road and are therefore liable to impede modal shift making the aid ineffective. 
Directive 2012/34/EU establishing a single European railway area132 (‘Single European 
Railway Area Directive’) defines what “congested infrastructure” is and mandates a capacity 
analysis and capacity enhancement plan in its Articles 50 and 51. 
In addition, there would be distance limits under both the revised GLs and the TBER, below 
which road transport would be presumed to be the preferred transport mode. As rail and IWW 
 
129 Indeed, the aid intensity should be lower than 100% in particular because beneficiaries of operating aid may 
also benefit from investment aid. While that aid would cover different eligible costs, it would still seem appropriate 
to ensure that overall, the support which society provides to SLT does not exceed the benefits that SLT provides 
in terms of avoided external costs.

out of 41 (4.9%) (2 rail associations), 1 trade union out of 2 (50.0%). 
132 Directive 2012/34/EU of the European Parliament and of the Council of 21 November 2012 establishing a 
single European railway area (recast), OJ L 343, 14.12.2012, pp. 32–77. 

<!-- page:38 -->
 
33 
transport becomes more competitive with increasing distances133, above these limits, Member 
States would have to demonstrate under the revised GLs that road is a viable alternative to the 
sustainable transport mode and that therefore SLT operations above these limits should also be 
eligible for aid. Operations above these limits would be excluded from the TBER altogether. 
However, SWL operations would be exempted from this safeguard, since those tend not to be 
competitive with road transport regardless of the distance covered, as confirmed by the study 
and consultation activities134.  
Finally, beneficiaries would be required to make all relevant information on the aid received 
publicly available to raise awareness about the measures available to reduce the competitiveness 
gap between SLT modes and less sustainable competing alternatives. This safeguard would i.a. 
ensure that all players, including smaller or newer companies, are informed about the aid 
measure and reduce the information gap between these players and large companies or 
incumbent operators, which would contribute to levelling the playing field in SLT. 
For interoperability aid, the set aid intensities could be exceeded under the revised GLs when 
the higher aid intensity is confirmed by a funding gap analysis. In case the funding gap shows 
that a higher aid intensity is needed, this would be systematically presumed to be proportionate. 
While the Commission has accepted aid intensities higher than 50% for interoperability aid in 
12 past cases, under Option B1 it would be spelled out in the rules that this is possible by 
demonstrating a higher funding need via a funding gap analysis, creating more clarity and 
transparency for Member States. 
5.4.2.  Option B2: Higher aid intensities and a single operating aid 
category, with limited safeguards 
Option B2 has a more generous approach to the compatibility conditions for the operating and 
interoperability aid categories, focusing on increased permissible aid amounts while keeping 
safeguards to a minimum. It differs in two significant aspects from Option B1: 
• For operating aid, it dispenses with the break-even distance limits, staying closer to the 
case practice, where such distance limits were not applied in the past.  
• For interoperability aid, there would be no need for a funding gap analysis to go beyond 
the 50% aid intensity and up to even 90% for key interoperability technologies (such as 
ERTMS and DAC).  
The choice not to include break-even distance limits is significant. First, despite the simple 
rationale to set distance thresholds to ensure that no aid is granted to SLT operations that would 
be competitive without aid, the actual competitive relationship between SLT and road transport 
is not linear and depends on individual operational specifications, undertakings and Member 
States, meaning that there are various risks of inadvertently introducing distortions. Second, as 
a safeguard related to individual characteristics, the administrative burden implications are 
significant. Third, the majority of aid approved in the SLT sector is operating aid135, to which 
this safeguard refers. Any impact is therefore magnified by the large amount to which it applies. 
Fourth, such distance limits were never applied in the case practice and the Commission has no 
experience with them. Fifth, when the Commission services proposed the inclusion of such 
limits in the TBER and draft revised GLs as part of a targeted consultation of all interested 
stakeholders on the draft texts, this was the most controversial issue of all with 41 stakeholders 
 
133 Section 2.1.1. 
134 Annex 2, point 4.3. 
135 Annex 7.  

<!-- page:39 -->
 
34 
contacting the Commission to express their opposition, amongst which public authorities from 
11 Member States and 28 businesses and business associations136, which pointed to the fact that 
the proposed safeguard was both unsuitable to attain its purpose and entailed a significant 
administrative burden. These comments were taken into account in devising Option B2. 
For core interoperability investments, while very high aid intensities have been accepted in the 
Commission’s case practice in some cases, under the revised rules compatibility would be 
systematically presumed for such measures without need for a funding gap analysis, which 
means at the same time a faster treatment of cases and an alleviation of administrative burden. 
This goes to the heart of the issue of strict compatibility conditions in the RGL not sufficiently 
supporting the roll-out of key interoperability technologies.   
5.4.3. Option B3: Higher aid intensities and aid for infrastructure use, 
minimal safeguards  
Option B3 would have both higher aid intensities for external cost aid (as in Option B2) and 
keep the category of aid for infrastructure use from case practice as a separate operating aid 
category, without a cumulative 30% of total cost aid ceiling and without any additional 
safeguard compared to the baseline. This has been suggested by some Member States and 
companies or business associations during the consultation activities137. As regards 
interoperability, Option B3 is equivalent to Option B2.

5.5. 
Policy options: compatibility conditions for aid for the acquisition of vehicles 
(C) 
The C Options consider different combinations in terms of the form of the aid and the potential 
beneficiaries of aid for the acquisition of vehicles, which have been authorised in past cases or 
put forward by stakeholders during the consultation activities138. They imply different trade-
offs in particular concerning the competitiveness of the SLT sector and the level playing field 
within the SLT sector. 
5.5.1.  Option C1: Acquisition of vehicles – Guarantees to smaller and 
newer companies  
Under Option C1, investment aid for the acquisition of vehicles would be limited to aid in the 
form of guarantees and only smaller companies and new entrants would be eligible for aid. The 
rationale would be to address the high entry barriers these companies face for the acquisition 
of vehicles and to promote a level playing field within SLT sectors, as mentioned by one public 
authority, one business association representing new entrants and a new entrant during the 
targeted consultation on the draft revised GLs139.  
 
138 Annex 2, points 4.1, 4.3, 4.4 and 4.5. 
139 Annex 2, point 4.4.  

<!-- page:41 -->
 
36 
The parameters of the guarantees have been designed to equate to an aid intensity of 20-30% 
of the value of the vehicle that is being acquired, which is the lower range of aid intensities 
observed in the case practice (Section 5.2). 
5.5.2.  Option C2: Acquisition of vehicles - Different types of aid to smaller 
and newer companies  
Under Option C2, investment aid for the acquisition of vehicles would be limited to smaller 
companies and new entrants (as under Option C1) but it would include, in addition to 
guarantees, also loans and grants with a 20-30% aid intensity, in line with the Commission’s 
case practice, thereby allowing further types of support compared to Option C1.  
5.5.3.  Option C3: Acquisition of vehicles – Guarantees to all companies 
Under Option C3, investment aid for the acquisition of vehicles would be open to all 
undertakings, but only in the form of guarantees. The idea under this option would be that the 
aid addresses the specific market failure identified under Option C1, i.e. the difficulty to access 
financing necessary to acquire new vehicles, while keeping the set of beneficiaries in line with 
today’s case practice. This widening of the scope of beneficiaries was proposed by around one 
quarter of stakeholders140 during the targeted consultation on the draft revised GLs141. 
The parameters of the guarantees would be designed in the same way as under Option C1.

5.6. 
Options discarded at an early stage 
Two options that were discarded at an early stage concern (i) the inclusion of maritime transport 
in the scope of the revised rules and (ii) the inclusion of specific provisions on rescue and 
restructuring of undertakings in the railway sector in the revised GLs. 
Maritime transport 
The inclusion of maritime transport in the rules was requested by three public authorities142 
during the targeted consultation. However, State aid to maritime transport does not fall under 
Article 93 TFEU, which is the legal basis for the proposed rules, but under Article 107(3)(c) 
TFEU, which allows aid to support certain economic activities. In addition to legal constraints, 
 
140 6 public authorities out of 29 (20.72%): BE, DE, FR, PL, RO, Flanders, 12 business associations out of 41 
(29.3%), 4 companies out of 12 (33.3%), 1 foundation out of 1 (100%). 
141 Annex 2, point 4.4. 
142 Denmark, Italy and Spain. 

<!-- page:42 -->
 
37 
the current case practice only includes short-sea shipping when it forms part of multimodal 
transport solutions that replace unimodal road transport.  
There is a specific set of rules which sets the conditions under which aid to maritime transport is 
justified143 and which offer many support options to that sector. Furthermore, under Article 
107(3)(c) TFEU, the maritime sector can receive State aid under the GBER and under several 
Commission guidelines144. 
In light of the above, this option was discarded at an early stage. 
Rescue and restructuring of undertakings in the railway sector 
A second option that was discarded at an early stage concerns the inclusion of derogations from 
the rules regarding the rescue and restructuring of undertakings in the railway sector. Such an 
option could have been considered in light of the Commission’s investigations into DB Cargo145 
and Fret SNCF (not concluded yet)146. Such specific rules would concern for example debt 
cancellation or other aid for the restructuring of railway undertakings in difficulty.  
The RGL provided for derogations for the rail freight sector from the rules laid down in the 
Rescue and Restructuring Guidelines (the ‘R&R Guidelines’)147 until 31 December 2009, by 
allowing those rules to apply to a freight division of a railway undertaking, and not only the 
firm as a whole, and they also laid down specific provisions regarding the possibility of debt 
cancellation for debts incurred prior to 15 March 2001 (applicable to both the freight and 
passenger segments)148. These derogations were justified by a reduced risk of competition 
distortions given that the passenger rail transport market was not yet liberalised, that debt 
cancellation applied to debts incurred prior to liberalisation also in the freight segment, and that 
several railway undertakings had not legally separated their passenger and freight activities. 
Both the passenger and the freight segments have meanwhile been liberalised149 and any 
deviation from the general R&R Guidelines therefore entails a higher risk of distorting 
competition. In addition, there is already flexibility in the R&R Guidelines to cater for specific 
circumstances, as they might occur in the rail sector150. 
Furthermore, it should be noted that:

• the derogations in the RGL were rarely used, resulting in only three Commission 
decisions151. 
• there was no clear support for such rules in the public consultation: 23% of 
stakeholders responding to the questionnaire expressed support, while 26% were 
against and 51% undecided152. 
• the Commission has limited case practice in applying the R&R Guidelines to the 
rail sector, with only two decisions adopted under the current R&R Guidelines153. 
In light of these circumstances, this option was discarded at an early stage.  
6. 
WHAT ARE THE IMPACTS OF THE POLICY OPTIONS? 
6.1. 
Methodology 
The analysis of the different policy options will mostly be carried out qualitatively. The limited 
transparency and reporting by Member States of aid to SLT have created data gaps and the State 
aid expenditure data presented in the annually published State aid Scoreboard excludes most of 
the aid to railways and other SLT modes154. This report presents a multi-criteria analysis with 
equally weighted criteria. Moreover, it is Member States, rather than the Commission, who 
determine the actual aid amounts awarded, subject to various considerations, such as budgetary 
constraints.  
Where relevant and possible, some extrapolations from the data relating to past State aid 
measures have been carried out for the assessment of the impacts of this initiative. Moreover, 
an estimate of the administrative simplification entailed by the A Options relating to the scope 
of the block exemption (the part of this revision with the most significant potential impact on 
administrative burden) has been carried out, including a targeted consultation of Member State 
authorities. Finally, quantitative data on environmental impacts has been drawn on as well.  
Economic and environmental impacts were assessed in detail, as further explained in sections 
6.1.1 and 6.1.2 below.

herstructureringssteun ten behoeve van de goederenactiviteiten van de NMBS, OJ C 327 of 4.12.2010; 
Commission decision of 16 June 2017 – Bulgaria BDZ Holding EAD SA, BDZ Passenger EOOD and BDZ Cargo 
EOOD and other measures, OJ L 337 of 19.12.2017.

154 The Commission must publish an annual State aid synopsis or State aid Scoreboard based on the expenditure 
reports provided by Member States, pursuant to Article 6 of Commission Regulation (EC) No 794/2004 of 21 
April 2004 implementing Council Regulation (EC) No 659/1999 laying down detailed rules for the application of 
Article 93 of the EC Treaty, OJ L 140, 30.4.2004.  

<!-- page:44 -->
 
39 
The social impacts of this initiative are less tangible, as society and citizens are the indirect 
albeit ultimate beneficiaries, of the economic and environmental impacts of this initiative. The 
social impacts have been assessed in particular for the B and C Options155.  
Any effects on digitalisation are limited, as most aid to SLT is expected to continue to be 
operating aid156. These effects are also expected to be indirect, as digitalisation is not an 
objective of the initiative per se. The initiative does have an overall positive influence on 
digitalisation. Among others, it facilitates aid to investments that can involve digitalisation of 
SLT, in particular through interoperability aid. Importantly, this initiative does not pose any 
obstacles to digitalisation. Against this background, the specific impacts on digitalisation have 
not been further assessed for each option.   
Finally, the initiative has no impact on fundamental rights. 
6.1.1. Economic impacts 
The economic impact of the different policy options will be assessed through their effects on 
(i) the functioning of the internal market and competition (including various dimensions of 
competitiveness, where relevant157) including the substitution and income effects expected to 
be generated by the initiative, (ii) the administrative burden on public authorities and related 
cost savings for businesses and (iii) the position of SMEs. As a rule, State aid rules do not have 
a direct impact on the administrative burden of businesses, as they do not impose or remove 
any obligations specifically on businesses. However, businesses can benefit indirectly from 
administrative simplification, when State aid procedures are accelerated and the support reaches 
the market faster, generating savings in opportunity costs.  
General considerations about the economics of modal shift 
Granting subsidies for SLT operations and investments is one way to increase the modal share 
of SLT. The largest amounts of aid to SLT have so far taken the form of operating aid. The 
subsidies provided should have led to lower prices charged by SLT operators (rail, IWW), thus 
incentivising a switch in demand for transport services away from road transport (substitution 
effect). As prices of SLT drop, a secondary effect could also be an increase in the total volume 
of transport services used, whether SLT or not (the income effect). As the main aim of this 
initiative is to support the substitution effect (the modal shift), the options discussed include 
also safeguards to ensure maximising this effect. A further analysis is provided in particular for 
option bundle B, which has the highest impact on operating aid.  
More generally, there are several studies showing that, even without safeguards, operating 
subsidies generate mainly a substitution effect. These studies have attempted to quantify the 
two effects and how the changes in prices affect demand volumes for each mode of transport. 
While the resulting range of estimates for the elasticity coefficients is quite large, the studies 
seem to converge in their conclusions, pointing to a prevalence of the substitution effects, 
particularly regarding operating aid to sustainable land transport.  
 
155 The initiative does not have any impact on social standards to be applied at national level.   
156 For the years 2022-2024, on average 75% of aid approved for SLT was in the form of operating aid.  
157 Impacts on competitiveness are assessed as part of the economic impacts. The whole initiative aims at 
improving the competitiveness of SLT compared to road transport and therefore the analysis of this impact partially 
coincides with the analysis on the attainment of modal shift. The options considered in this Impact Assessment 
Report would have only a very limited impact on international competitiveness since they relate to a rebalancing 
of the conditions for different transport modes within the Union. 

<!-- page:45 -->
 
40 
Indeed, for freight transport, the studies158 show that the elasticity of rail volumes to rail prices 
is around -1.0x (with a range of 0 to -1.6) and the cross-elasticity of road volumes to rail prices 
is around 0.3x (with a range of 0.1-0.5). These coefficients mean that a 10% drop in rail prices 
lead to a 10% increase in volumes transported by rail and a 3% decrease in volumes transported 
by road. As volumes of freight transported by road in the EU are about four times bigger than 
the volumes transported by rail, the increase in rail volumes broadly corresponds to the decrease 
in road volumes, showing that the substitution effect is dominant and the income effect of a 
decrease in rail prices is negligible.  
As concerns passenger transport, the elasticity of rail volumes to rail prices is estimated to be 
lower at around -0.4x159 and the elasticity of passenger transport from cars to rail prices (proxied 
by public transport prices) is much lower at 0.08x160. This means that a 10% decrease in rail 
prices leads to a 4% increase in the number of rail passengers and a decrease of 0.8% in the 
usage of cars. Still, considering that the number of road trips is approximately five times higher 
than that of rail trips, the percentage change in rail passengers broadly matches the decrease in 
the number of car passengers. Therefore, also as concerns passengers transport, the substitution 
effect appears dominant.  
Calculating the amounts of operating aid granted on average per year in the EU over the 2022-
2024 period (EUR 3.6 billion) allows for a more precise quantification of the substitution effect 
generated by the operating aid measures approved in 2022-2024 and shows that operating aid 
represents about 4% of the annual revenues of SLT operators. Assuming the subsidies were 
used entirely to reduce the end-user prices, the result would be a 4% price reduction. Applying 
the elasticity coefficients mentioned above, this 4% price reduction can be expected to generate 
a 2% increase in rail volumes (average of passengers and freight combined), most of which 
through a switch from road transport (in line with the conclusion above that the substitution 
effect is dominant). While these economic impacts all result from the cost reduction potential 
for SLT caused by the proposed approach under different options, the effect is predominant in 
the case of option bundle B which assesses different aid intensities for operating aid.   
The budgetary constraints that can limit success 
The extent to which the initiative will be successful can also depend on the budgetary 
constraints of Member States, though political priorities seem to play a more important role. It 
should first be noted that the amounts used for SLT measures over 2022-2024 have been 
relatively small in the total budgets of Member States. The largest category of aid to SLT 
represented by operating aid accounted on average for just 7% of total public subsidies for 
transport (including public support for all transport modes, even if it does not qualify as State 
aid). State aid spending on SLT amounted, on average, to just 0.04% of Member States’ 
budgetary expenditures, with the highest values in Austria (0.22%), Czechia (0.14%), and 
Germany (0.11%).  
 
158 International Transport Forum, Mode Choice in Freight Transport, ITF Research Reports, OECD Publishing, 
2022: 
https://www.oecd.org/content/dam/oecd/en/publications/reports/2022/02/mode-choice-in-freight-
transport_667ea2c1/3e69ebc4-en.pdf 
159 CE Delft, Modal choice criteria in rail transport, 2018: https://cedelft.eu/wp-
content/uploads/sites/2/2021/03/CE_Delft_4S52_Modal_choice_criteria_in_rail-transport_Def.pdf 
160 International Transport Forum, Valuing Convenience in Public Transport, ITF Round Tables, No. 156, OECD 
Publishing. 2014, https://www.oecd.org/content/dam/oecd/en/publications/reports/2014/12/valuing-convenience-
in-public-transport_g1g4525d/9789282107683-en.pdf 

<!-- page:46 -->
 
41 
The level of spending appears to be primarily the result of political priorities of Member States 
when allocating funds to SLT measures. Budgetary constraints seem to play only a secondary 
role, as some countries with high medium-term fiscal sustainability risks161 still allocated 
above-average shares of their total State aid budgets to SLT. The analysis of the budgetary 
expenditures allocated to aid to SLT over time as a proportion of the overall budget of the 
Member States does not reveal any clear pattern, which suggests that political priorities likely 
play a more important role in this context.162  
An important mitigating factor that could lead to an increase of aid amounts in the coming years 
is the introduction of ETS2. Expectations of ETS2 revenues stand at EUR 342-570 billion163 
for five years (compared to just EUR 4.8 billion per year for the SLT State aid measures per 
year on average between 2022 and 2024), of which a large part will need to be earmarked to 
green projects including modal shift164. ETS2 is thus likely to provide an additional source of 
budget revenues for Member States to use on SLT measures and could help alleviate some of 
the pressure from budget constraints when investing in SLT. 
6.1.2. Environmental impacts 
The environmental impact of the different policy options will be assessed by their contribution 
to modal shift towards SLT and therefore the attainment of the Green Deal and SSMS 
objectives. The specific environmental impacts that will be assessed are GHG emissions, air 
pollution and noise, because they are the environmental impacts most affected by modal shift, 
which also means that reliable data is available for them. These environmental impacts all result 
from the modal shift impact caused by each option.  
The below Table 6 shows the main environmental impacts that result from a modal shift from 
road to SLT165.

The table shows that the environmental impacts of modal shift are significant, with GHG 
emissions reduced by about two thirds, for each unit of transport that is shifted from road to 
SLT. Air pollution diminishes by between one third and one half. The impact on noise is more 
mixed, with small gains for modal shift in the freight segment, but with an increase in noise 
pollution in the passenger segment. Overall, the environmental impacts of modal shift, weighted 
by the severity of the impact166, are significantly positive, with reductions of undesirable 
impacts by 34% in the passenger segment and over 43% in the freight segment. The data from 
the Commission Handbook are from 2016 and are currently the latest data available. However, 
to the extent available, data for 2022 show similar impacts167.

6.2. 
Option Bundle A - scope of the revised Guidelines and TBER 
6.2.1. Economic impacts 
Functioning of the internal market and competition 
Under Option A1 more investments and operations conducive to modal shift towards SLT 
would be within the scope of the new rules and would thus benefit from the legal certainty 
offered by the codification of the case practice in the revised GLs.  
Assuming the number of measures would remain the same in the future as those approved 
between 2008 and 2024, under Option A1, in addition to 100 approved measures pertaining to 
rail, also the 17 approved past operating aid measures pertaining to IWW would come under 
the revised GLs. The same would be true for the additional interoperability investment aid 
measures for IWW as well as the 64 investment aid measures for vehicles, facilities and private 
sidings, which fell outside the RGL.  
Transparency in the treatment of all SLT modes under the GLs would entail fewer undue 
distortions of the level playing field in the internal market within the SLT sector compared to 
the baseline, where Member States could be inclined to concentrate financing on the types of 
aid and operators targeted by the RGL. It would also contribute to targeting modal shift in the 
most efficient way, as this would enable a holistic approach, leaving it up to Member States to 
choose within a wide toolbox of measures those most efficient for increasing competitiveness 
depending on the specific situation. By facilitating aid to SLT, Option A1 would also promote 
the competitiveness of SLT, with the ensuing benefits for cost effectiveness and innovation. 
This was particularly stressed in the stakeholder responses to the targeted consultation on the 
draft revised GLs, where 10 Member States expressly welcomed among others the broadening 
of the scope and consolidation of case practice168.

<!-- page:49 -->
 
44 
The TBER would be limited to measures for which compatibility rules are already codified in 
Section 6 of the RGL under Option A1. This would, on the one hand, have further positive 
effects on the internal market and competition, as the measures to be block exempted would be 
straightforward and pro-competitive. On the other hand, the scope of the TBER under Option 
A1 could also have negative impacts on competition within the SLT sector. There is a high 
likelihood that Member States would prioritise measures under the block exemption, as this is 
faster and avoids administrative burden, therefore skewing the playing field to the advantage of 
measures in the scope of the TBER. Block exempting interoperability aid and operating aid 
only in the rail sector could cause competition distortions between SLT modes. Moreover, 
Member States could choose to prioritise such aid over other types of measures, including for 
example aid to facilities or aid to modernise and adapt vehicles, simply because they are block 
exempted. A part of the positive effects on the internal market and competition of the extension 
of the scope of the revised GLs would therefore be lost.  
Option A2 would extend the block exemption to all measures in the SLT sector on which there 
is significant case practice today, in line with stakeholder feedback on the CfE for the TBER169. 
Higher aid amounts, which entail a higher risk of distortion, would still have to be notified, as 
they would exceed the notification thresholds. 
By facilitating the granting of further types of aid and further actors in the SLT sector, Option 
A2 would have a very positive impact on the competitiveness of the SLT sector vis-à-vis road. 
It would entail fewer undue distortions between SLT modes compared to the baseline and 
Option A1, as the codification of rules for aid to all main contributors to modal shift would 
avoid focalising Member States’ attention on only a subset of actors and measures. This option 
would also contribute to levelling the playing field within the SLT sector.  
This option would also entail safeguards to avoid distortions to competition, as only aid 
measures on which there is significant case practice would be block-exempted. This approach 
can be illustrated with two examples.  
First, aid to support the development of telematics applications170 in the passenger sector would 
not be block-exempted because there is insufficient case practice and some of these measures 
might raise antitrust concerns, warranting a more detailed assessment of their impact on 
competition in the context of a State aid notification.  
Second, start-up aid would be limited under the TBER to the freight sector, and to the freight 
sector and passenger night trains under the revised GLs, in light of (i) the more limited case 
practice on passenger connections, which only concerns night trains, and (ii) some concerns of 
abuse raised by stakeholders in relation to the proper identification of “new connections” in the 
passenger segment. The limitation of the scope of start-up aid to those cases helps  preventing 
competition distortions by limiting the benefit of the block-exemption to measures where the 
risk of abuse is low (i.e. the freight sector), while ensuring that aid measures for night trains, 
which face particular challenges (e.g. high upfront investments to adjust the wagons) can be 
approved after a more detailed assessment of their impact on competition with existing 
connections.  
 
169 Annex 2, point 4.2. 
170 Such as systems which provide passengers with information before and during the journey, reservation and 
payment systems, luggage management and management of connections between trains and with other modes of 
transport. 

<!-- page:50 -->
 
45 
Furthermore, option A2 would also codify safeguards stemming from the case practice to avoid 
distortions to competition. For example, as concerns investment aid to facilities (which is not 
included in the RGL), Member States would be required to demonstrate, in line with the case 
practice, that the new capacity is likely to be used, thereby contributing to the increase of the 
use of SLT, and does not divert current SLT flows from existing facilities, which would be an 
inefficient use of public funds and distort competition with the owner or operator of such 
existing facilities. Furthermore, in line with the case practice and secondary EU legislation, the 
revised GLs and TBER would reiterate that access to the aided facility must be open, transparent 
and non-discriminatory and must be provided to all interested users, so as to prevent 
competition distortions amongst SLT operators.  
In addition to the impacts of Option A2, Option A3 could increase the services offered in the 
rail freight sector, as PSOs would cover rail freight services that would not be profitable on 
their own and for which other support possibilities would not be sufficient. Rail freight 
operators are often faced with parts of transport operations where the volume of goods or the 
distance is insufficient for rail to compete with road (SWL). For this reason, rail freight 
operators may ultimately decide not to offer certain services, which could leave gaps in the 
network of connections and render rail transport less attractive and less competitive overall. 
Member States might not consider supporting such services through PSO compensation in the 
absence of clear rules. Laying down rules for the award of rail freight PSOs would therefore 
help address the weaknesses of such rail freight transport operations by making the entrustment 
of PSOs more transparent and accessible.  
Even though there has been only one decision171 in the past, there have been several attempts 
by Member States to put in place rail freight PSOs. It is therefore not unlikely that the impacts 
of this Option actually materialise.  
Administrative burden 
Under Option A1 there would be an insignificant one-off adjustment cost for Member States 
to familiarise themselves with the revised rules. This is expected to be marginal, as the rules 
would refer to measures in the scope of the RGL or existing case practice, with which Member 
States are familiar. After this, there would be a recurring administrative burden reduction for 
each measure for which rules are explicitly codified. The rules in the revised GLs would be 
clear and transparent, and could be found in one place, guiding Member States and businesses 
towards compatible aid designs and the information to be provided for the assessment.  
This would reduce the number of information requests exchanged, and ultimately the burden 
associated with State aid procedures. This means that aid would reach its beneficiaries more 
swiftly. The significance of the related cost reduction is illustrated by the fact that, between 
2008 and 2024, more than half (146 out of 258) of all decisions concerning the SLT sector 
concerned (at least partially) measures outside the scope of the RGL.  
The TBER would further reduce administrative burden compared to the baseline, as many cases 
notified today would benefit from the simplification of block exemption, as was confirmed by 
stakeholders during the consultation activities172. 
 
171 Decision of 24 November 2023 in case SA.32953 Italy: State aid to Trenitalia in form of compensation for a 
public service obligation in the rail freight sector, OJ L 2860, 18.11.2024. 
172 Annex 2, point 4.2 and 4.4 and Annex 8.

Option A2 would entail a higher administrative simplification than Option A1 as the block 
exemption would apply to a higher number of cases.  
This report attempts to estimate the level of the administrative burden reduction of the block 
exemption for Member States for each option under various scenarios173:  
- 
a central scenario, under which State aid spending (and number of measures) would 
remain at the same level as the average of the years 2022-2024 (which could be 
considered as a baseline post-COVID-19),  
- 
a low scenario, which assumes that relevant State aid spending (and the number of 
measures) would go back to the level observed in 2018 (before the COVID-19 
pandemic), 
- 
and a high scenario, where relevant State aid spending (and the number of measures) 
increases by 15% compared to the 2022-2024 average (e.g. due to Member States 
prioritising the decarbonisation of the transport sector via modal shift).

The further extension of the scope of the GLs to cater for rail freight PSOs in Option A3 would 
allow Member States to gain clarity on the specific rules applicable. This effect is however 
difficult to quantify, because it does not follow from a reduction in notified measures, for which 
the Commission has (collected) specific data. The quantification of the administrative burden 
savings for this report is therefore the same for Options A2 and A3.  
Impacts on SMEs 
Option A1 would have a positive impact on SMEs compared to the baseline. SMEs would have 
easier access to first-hand information on compatibility conditions. This is particularly relevant 
for SMEs, which have less resources to deploy for such research. They would therefore more 
easily be empowered to discuss possible support with Member States. In terms of number of 
companies, SMEs constitute most of the IWW sector, 93%174 of the rail sector, and 99% of 
multimodal transport175. Option A1 would further benefit SMEs compared to the baseline, as 
the block exemption of the measures contained in section 6 of the RGL would allow support to 
be put in place faster. This may benefit SMEs in particular as they generally have more difficult 
access to finance.  
Option A2 further benefits SMEs compared to Option A1 and the baseline, as it would extend 
the scope of the block exemption.  
As set out in Section 2.3, currently most rail freight activities which could be subject to PSOs 
are de facto carried out by incumbents, i.e. large undertakings. Still, the inclusion of rules on 
freight PSOs in the revised GLs proposed under Option A3 could improve the situation for 
SMEs. Many new entrants are SMEs, which could compete for the award of public service 
contracts on equal grounds with incumbents under this option, notably at local level for small-
scale operations. The impact of this option on SMEs is nevertheless expected to be marginal, 
as it remains likely that many contracts would be awarded to incumbents also in this context.  
To estimate the level of opportunity cost savings for SMEs, the share of aid received by SMEs 
in each option and scenario was approximated based on their market share in terms of turnover 
in different segments of SLT. With SMEs being more active in the IWW segment, the savings 
are higher in the case of Options A2 and A3, which also include this segment. Further details 
on the calculations and their underlying assumptions are provided in Annex 8.

6.2.2. Environmental impacts 
Option A1 would have a positive impact on modal shift, as all potential contributors would be 
explicitly included in the scope of the revised GLs. As set out in section 6.1, despite the slightly 
higher noise impact of rail transport compared to road and the slightly higher air pollution of 
IWW, the overall environmental benefit of modal shift is strongly positive. Option A1 would 
therefore have a strongly positive environmental impact.  
Option A1 would have a further positive environmental impact compared to the baseline, as 
measures meeting the conditions of the TBER could be put in place more rapidly, to the benefit 
of modal shift. There could be some negative impacts on efficiency, in case Member States 
would engage in a less holistic approach and would not consider other potentially more 
environmentally beneficial measures not pertaining to the categories of section 6 of the RGL, 
because they would not be in the scope of the block exemption.  
Option A2 would have a stronger positive environmental impact than Option A1, as the block 
exemption would extend to all measures for which there is case practice. Many measures 
supporting the reduction of GHG emissions and of external costs in general through a modal 
shift towards SLT could be introduced immediately and simply under the TBER.  
Option A3 is expected to have a positive environmental effect, as it supports an increase in rail 
transport on routes on which it has proven the most difficult to switch to SLT modes and which 
are therefore least likely to shift to SLT modes absent PSOs. As these are often routes on which 
frequencies are lower, the increase in noise would also be less significant.  
6.2.3. Social impacts 
Options A1 and A2 are not expected to have significant social impacts compared to the 
baseline. They concern the coverage of the revised GLs and TBER and not the conditions under 
which aid can be granted. No stakeholder mentioned social considerations during the 
consultation activities. Option A3 could have a slightly positive impact, as it would make it 
possible to fill current gaps in rail freight transport at a limited cost for customers.  
6.3. 
Option Bundle B – operating and interoperability aid 
6.3.1. Economic impacts 
Functioning of the internal market and competition 
For operating aid, Option B1 would compensate for the elimination of the category of aid for 
infrastructure use with a higher aid intensity for external cost aid. In most Member States, this 

<!-- page:54 -->
 
49 
change would allow for more operating aid to be granted overall176. The distance limits 
safeguard would lower the total amount of aid that can be granted, since the longest operations 
could not benefit from aid under the TBER, and Member States might be discouraged from 
applying the revised GLs in view of the high administrative burden of the distance limits177, as 
stakeholders confirmed during the targeted consultation on the draft revised GLs178.  
The distance limits are meant to ensure that State aid is granted only when necessary, and not 
to operations that would have gone ahead anyway in the absence of State aid. Stakeholders have 
however explained that the competitiveness of SLT compared to road transport is not linear. 
There are other constraints such as network capacity, speed of service, and transhipment costs. 
While there may be a distance beyond which SLT is competitive with road transport on average, 
across the EU there may be significant differences between undertakings and Member States. 
For example, undertakings operating in very small Member States may be disadvantaged 
because they have more international traffic, rendering operations costlier for a given distance 
(e.g. needing interoperable locomotives and multilingual drivers). 
The capacity constraints safeguard179 under Option B1 would address the risk that capacity 
constraints in the SLT infrastructure would prevent the increase of the use of SLT. Even though 
some stakeholders raised questions during the targeted consultation on the draft revised GLs, 
they acknowledged that it was already foreseen in the Single European Railway Area Directive.  
As regards interoperability aid, Option B1 would boost the roll-out of interoperability 
technologies compared to the baseline, because aid intensities in excess of 50% would be 
systematically permitted upon demonstration of the need for more aid by a funding gap analysis. 
Overall, the increase in the aid intensity in combination with the distance limits would mean 
that Option B1 would likely result in similar amounts of operating aid being granted as in the 
baseline. The option would still have a small positive impact on levelling the playing field 
between SLT and road transport overall, since the conditions for interoperability aid would 
become more generous. More support for interoperability would strengthen SLT, particularly 
the rail sector as it is more affected by interoperability market failures. Interoperability 
investments lower costs for cross-border operations especially. This increases intra-EU 
competition and promotes the internal market. Stronger competition may lead to lower prices, 
higher quality of service and more innovation, as mentioned by several stakeholders180 in their 
response to the IIA for the revision of the RGL181. It would also lead to an increase in demand

study, for most Member States a higher external cost aid ceiling would result in an increase in the total amount of 
operating aid that can be granted compared to the baseline. 
177 The impact would likely be significant: Italy provided feedback in the public consultation that around half of 
all train-kilometres in the rail sector are beyond the proposed distance threshold. 
178 Annex 2, point 4.4.

increasing or maintaining the modal shift. This condition aims to ensure that the prospects of increasing or

with established capacity constraints, the Member State will have to explain what other measures will be taken to 
eliminate or reduce those constraints based on a capacity analysis or a capacity-enhancement plan, as provided 
under Directive 2012/34/EU of the European Parliament and of the Council of 21 November 2012 establishing a

area

181 Annex 2, point 4.1. 

<!-- page:55 -->
 
50 
for transport services (income effect) and a shift from road to rail (substitution effect), but these 
effects are expected to be limited due to the modest overall increase in aid volumes.  
Option B2 would compensate for the elimination of the aid for infrastructure use category with 
a higher aid intensity, as Option B1 does. It would not introduce distance limits, preventing 
competition distortions in the SLT sector between undertakings in different Member States. 
The capacity constraint safeguard would remain to further ensure modal shift as compared to 
the baseline. Under Option B2, Member States could grant more operating aid under the revised 
GLs than in the baseline, which would improve the competitiveness of SLT.  
Experience shows the potential of operating aid in terms of promoting modal shift. For example, 
the Fitness Check notes that Italy (which in the period 2008 to 2023 had seven regional schemes 
supporting modal shift prolonged up to three times) recorded steady growth in the modal share 
of rail and multimodal transport, from 9.2% in 2011 to 14.7% in 2016.  
By dropping the funding gap safeguard for key interoperability technologies, Option B2 would 
further facilitate the granting of interoperability aid. The competition distortion risks, in terms 
of overcompensating beneficiaries, are minimal, since the higher aid intensities are limited to 
investments with acute market failures and a lack of significant private benefits. Moreover, the 
Commission has in the past already approved aid intensities of 85 – 100% for interoperability 
investments in SLT in 12 cases, finding that such high aid intensities even in the absence of a 
funding gap analysis did not raise concerns regarding a potential distortive effect. 
Overall, Option B2 would increase competition within the SLT sector without risks of undue 
competition distortions and have a strong positive impact on levelling the playing field between 
SLT and road transport.   
The income and substitution effects also play an important role in defining the economic 
impacts of Option B2, with the objective of the initiative being to generate a shift in traffic from 
road to SLT, thereby maximizing the substitution effect rather than merely creating additional 
demand for transport. The expected increase in operating aid volumes and intensities under 
Option B2 would lead to lower prices for SLT compared to road transport and, as explained in 
Section 6.1.1, would increase the demand for SLT and the SLT volumes, predominantly due to 
a shift from road to rail.182 To ensure the substitution effect is maximised, the capacity 
constraint safeguard embedded in Option B2 ensures that aid is directed to rail routes with 
sufficient capacity to accommodate additional traffic and that can generate a switch from road 
to SLT.  
Option B3 is equivalent to Option B2 for interoperability aid but could have a significant 
competition impact, because it would allow higher amounts of operating aid, which may cause 
a further modal shift. This was stressed by 6 stakeholders183 calling for the reintroduction of the 
category of operating aid for infrastructure use during the targeted consultation on the draft 
revised GLs. However, by allowing aid potentially in excess of the avoided external costs, it 
risks doing so at a cost that is higher than the benefits realised. This could also create 
competition distortions between SLT and road transport, if operating aid to SLT exceeds the 
implicit subsidy that the road sector receives due to the lack of internalisation of external costs.  
 
182 Following the calculation presented in section 6.1.1, each 10% increase in operating aid volumes has the 
potential to generate a 0.2% increase in rail transport volumes (passenger and freight combined), the majority 
representing a shift from road transport. 
183 Annex 2, point 4.4. 

<!-- page:56 -->
 
51 
By prioritising modal shift to this extent, this Option would risk creating a dependency on State 
aid, with the competitive pressure on SLT reduced to the point that it might become less 
efficient or implement fewer quality improvements.   
When analysing the income versus the substitution effects in the case of Option B3, the lack of 
safeguards and the significant price decrease of SLT could backfire and lead to an increase in 
overall demand for transport services, rather than merely a shift from road to rail. Without a 
capacity safeguard in place, the aid could be directed towards lowering prices for SLT routes 
that cannot accommodate additional traffic, thus merely generating congestion and shifting the 
increased demand back to road transport.  
Administrative burden 
Under Option B1, fewer aid ceilings would apply to operating aid and there would be no need 
to compare infrastructure costs between transport modes which, as shown by past experience, 
raises some difficulties.  
However, the capacity constraints and distance limits safeguards would apply. The former 
obligation already exists under sectoral legislation184 and would apply only under the revised 
GLs, not the TBER, so its administrative impact would be limited. In contrast to the capacity 
constraints, the distance limits would introduce significant administrative burdens. Operators 
would have to keep track of the distance for each of their operations individually, and not just 
in aggregate (which is necessary anyway under an operating aid scheme). In the rail sector, 
operators would have to separately collect the data for SWL operations, as SWL would not be 
caught by the distance limit. Administrative burdens would also vary for undertakings 
depending on the Member State in which they are active. Undertakings in sparsely populated 
large Member States could be more penalised because long distances may be more common, 
and those undertakings would therefore have to demonstrate more frequently that aid is needed. 
This was also stressed by stakeholders during the targeted consultation on the draft revised 
GLs185. 
For interoperability aid, Option B1 also imposes additional administrative burden, though in a 
more limited manner. A funding gap analysis would be systematically necessary if the aid 
intensity of 50% is exceeded. In the baseline, a funding gap analysis has not been consistently 
applied for interoperability measures with aid intensities above 50%, as other justifications were 
also accepted. 
Overall, Option B1 would impose a significant administrative burden compared to the baseline.  
Option B2 has a positive impact on administrative burden. It simplifies the operating aid 
assessment, while avoiding the very considerable administrative burden of the distance limits. 
The interoperability aid assessment is also simplified, because for certain investments an aid 
intensity beyond 50% can benefit from a proportionality presumption without need for a 
funding gap analysis. 
Option B3 would have a positive administrative burden impact compared to the baseline, 
though the impact is less positive than under Option B2. For operating aid, both main categories 
of aid that already exist in the RGL would be maintained with their different conditions and 
 
184 Directive 2012/34/EU of the European Parliament and of the Council of 21 November 2012 establishing a 
single European railway area (recast), OJ L 343, 14.12.2012, Articles 47 and 50.  
185 Annex 2, point 4.4. 

<!-- page:57 -->
 
52 
applicable ceilings. The 30% of total cost cap would however be removed, partially 
compensating for the additional administrative burden of having two aid categories. For 
interoperability aid, the administrative simplification is the same as under Option B2.  
Impact on SMEs 
Options B1, B2 and B3 do not target SMEs and have no significant impact on them, other than 
the impact on administrative burden, which would be significantly increased under Option B1 
and reduced under Options B2 and B3 compared to the baseline. Such burden affects SMEs 
disproportionately given their lower ability to ensure compliance with burdensome rules.  
6.3.2.  Environmental impacts 
As explained in the economic impacts, Option B1 would overall allow a similar amount of 
operating aid to be granted as under the baseline, with higher aid intensities being offset by the 
introduction of distance limits. Since the changes to operating aid would not clearly generate 
additional modal shift, the environmental impact of the proposed compatibility conditions 
regarding operating aid would be mixed.  
Option B1 would also contribute to accelerating the roll-out of interoperability technologies, 
increasing the competitiveness of SLT, in particular in the rail sector. Moreover, by improving 
the productivity of operations and lowering operating costs, interoperability technologies allow 
for the more intense use of existing vehicles. By allowing modal shift to be generated without 
requiring additional vehicles, this in turn limits resource extraction and material use.  
Since a lack of interoperability affects the rail sector specifically, the environmental impacts of 
Option B1 would be especially strong in terms of reducing GHG emissions and air pollution, 
while the impact on noise would be more mixed, as set out in Section 6.1.2. However, 
interoperability technologies can also significantly reduce noise pollution. For example, DAC 
reduces time spent performing shunting operations, which are particularly noisy, and allows 
electronically controlled pneumatic brakes, which are quieter than the traditional air brakes. 
ERTMS optimises train movements, reducing unnecessary acceleration or braking.  
It is therefore expected that Option B1 would have a positive effect on all three environmental 
impacts considered compared to the baseline.  
Option B2 would have a strongly positive environmental impact compared to the baseline. In 
contrast to Option B1, under Option B2 more operating aid could be granted than under the 
baseline, further encouraging modal shift. Moreover, by further facilitating the granting of 
higher amounts of interoperability aid, as the funding gap would not systematically be required, 
Option B2 promotes interoperability investments even more strongly than Option B1.  
Option B3 would allow even higher amounts of operating aid than Option B2, further promote 
modal shift, and therefore generate additional positive environmental impacts, at least in the 
short term. In the longer term, a potential loss of competitiveness and efficiency in the sector 
due to State aid dependency may reduce those benefits.  
The variation in the environmental impact between Option B3 and B2 comes from the 
difference in the set-up of operating aid. Option B3 involves an additional operating aid 
category for infrastructure use compared to Option B2, which involves only one operating aid 
category related to the external cost difference with higher aid intensities than in the past. This 
means that under Option B3, in addition to a compensation for external costs savings relating 
to SLT at a higher intensity than in the baseline scenario, also the infrastructure use costs can 

<!-- page:58 -->
 
53 
be subsidised. This would lead to higher overall permissible operating aid amounts under 
Option B3 than under Option B2, up to a point where the transport operators could be 
compensated for more than the external cost savings which their activities help to achieve.  
While this could further contribute to modal shift in the short term, this comes at the risk of 
paying out aid and society bearing a cost that is higher than the environmental benefit related 
to SLT, which could create a dependency on State aid and reduce the pressure on SLT operators 
in the longer term to be efficient and implement quality improvements. Thus, in the longer run, 
such high levels of support are likely to disincentivise operators from adjusting to new market 
realities and innovating and would risk ceteris paribus to increase the competitiveness of road 
transport and impede the modal shift.  
6.3.3. Social impacts 
Customers and prices 
Option B1 has a positive social impact. By promoting interoperability, it contributes to 
lowering maintenance cost, improving productivity, and increasing competition on the EU 
market, all of which could lead to lower prices for end-users. The affordability of transport is a 
key factor in causing poor access to transport, which affects socially vulnerable groups 
especially186. A Commission report on essential services found that 5.8% of those at risk of 
poverty could not afford to use public transport regularly187. Moreover, interoperability 
technologies such as ERTMS improve safety, a strong benefit to citizens.  
Option B2 would have a clear positive social impact. In addition to promoting interoperability 
even more than under Option B1, it would allow for more operating aid to be granted than the 
baseline. If there is an increase in the amount of operating aid granted, beneficiaries may pass 
on their lower net operating costs to some extent to end-users, such as companies shipping 
goods or citizens using rail services, in the form of lower prices. 
Option B3 would also have a clearly positive social impact, at least in the short term, because 
it increases the amount of operating aid that can be granted, which could lead to lower prices 
for end-users.   
Employment 
Road transport uses more workers per passenger/tonne-kilometre than rail transport does. For 
example, a freight train can replace up to 52 freight trucks188, each of which has a driver, while 
typically requiring a crew of at most 2 people: a conductor and an engineer189. In 2021, the road 
transport sector employed around 6 million people190, compared to around 911 500 in the rail 
sector (in 2022)191. In other words, the road sector employed around 6.6 times more people than 
the rail sector did, against a modal share that in 2023 was around 10 times higher in the 
passenger segment and 4.5 times higher in the freight segment. When limiting employment to

187 
https://employment-social-affairs.ec.europa.eu/news/access-essential-services-key-challenges-most-
vulnerable-report-2023-06-28_en.  
188 https://www.uirr.com/news/mediacentre/2286-the-optimal-length-of-freight-trains.html.  
189 https://www.trains.com/trn/train-basics/abcs-of-railroading/the-people-who-work-on-trains/. Most freight 
trains in the EU operate with a single worker: https://railnegotiations.com/wp-content/uploads/2022/09/Carriers-
Exhibit-11-Report-of-Oliver-Wyman-Comparison-of-European-and-US-Railways.pdf, exhibit II-1.  
190 https://ec.europa.eu/eurostat/web/products-eurostat-news/-/ddn-20230207-1.  
191 See footnote 28.  

<!-- page:59 -->
 
54 
direct transportation and storage activities (e.g. postal and courier services), the road transport 
sector employed 5.1 million in 2022, against 554 400 for the rail sector, or 9.2 times more, and 
41 400 for the IWW sector192. 
Given that in the road transport sector, employment directly linked to transport operations is 
considerably higher in the freight segment than it is in the passenger segment193, this 
comparison would tend to indicate that modal shift would have a negative impact on 
employment. On the other hand, this only takes into account employment directly linked with 
transport operations and disregards the jobs linked with the construction and maintenance of 
the relevant infrastructure. According to the total employment figures cited above, 85% of 
employment in the road transport sector is directly linked to transport activities as opposed to 
e.g. maintenance and operation of infrastructure, against just 60.8% in the rail sector194. 
Accommodating modal shift in the rail sector would require the construction, maintenance and 
operation of additional infrastructure such as freight terminals, marshalling yards and refuelling 
facilities, which is likely to lead to a net gain in employment in infrastructure related jobs, since 
employment is higher in infrastructure in the rail sector. Therefore, when one considers both 
the jobs related directly to transport operations and those related to the relevant infrastructure, 
the impact of the modal shift on employment is not clear. 
Another consideration that should in the future be taken into account concerns the automation 
of activities. A higher share of employment in the road sector is directly linked to transport 
activities, indicating that employment in the road sector may be more vulnerable to automation. 
The International Transport Forum and the OECD estimate that driverless trucks may reduce 
employment of truckers in Europe by 50-70% by 2030195. Meanwhile, according to both trade 
union and management representatives, automatic train operations will only become relevant 
in the longer term, i.e. after 2030196.  
As regards the impact of the modal shift on employment in other sectors, one needs to consider 
that every job in the transport sector creates 4 more jobs in other sectors according to the Draghi 
report. In case of modal shift, i.e. if the SLT operations were cheaper, faster, or of higher quality 
than road transport in some other way, that would have positive impacts on the competitiveness 
of the EU transport sector as a whole, with a positive impact on employment.  
The impact on employment of improved interoperability would be similarly mixed. DAC 
automates shunting operations and could therefore substitute a part of the workforce active in 
those activities. Similarly, ERTMS is likely to reduce manual maintenance and signalling roles 
through automation. On the other hand, the rollout of such technologies requires significant 
investments in retrofitting existing rail networks, trains, and wagons. This includes installing 
signalling systems, onboard units, and coupling mechanisms across the EU, creating 
employment e.g. in engineering and manufacturing. The main employment impact from 
increased interoperability investments seems to be a move to higher-skilled employment197.

55 
In conclusion, estimating the employment impact of modal shift or increased interoperability 
investments is subject to high uncertainty. Modal shift would necessarily cause job losses in the 
road transport sector while creating jobs in the SLT sector. Moreover, there are future 
digitalisation developments to consider. On balance, the employment impact of all B Options 
is therefore estimated to be neutral.  
6.4. 
Option Bundle C – aid for the acquisition of vehicles 
6.4.1. Economic impacts 
Functioning of the internal market and competition 
Under Option C1, Member States could provide guarantees covering a significant part, but less 
than 100%, of the principal amount of the purchase costs of vehicles to the benefit of smaller 
companies and new entrants, which would allow the financing risk for lenders to be reduced 
significantly. This category of aid would address the specific difficulties faced by these 
enterprises and open up access to funding for these assets to market players of all sizes. It 
therefore would allow competition on more equal terms. At the same time, it would maintain 
some impact of market forces, as not the full amount would be guaranteed. Both the form of 
the aid and the narrow scope of beneficiaries would therefore constitute safeguards against 
distortions to competition and significantly improve the level playing field while being efficient 
in terms of public spending.  
Not allowing grants and allowing only new entrants and smaller companies to benefit from the 
measure has several further potentially positive competition impacts:  
• Overall rolling stock supply in the EU is constrained and could not easily adapt to 
changes in demand, as confirmed by stakeholders in the public consultation, notably by 
the Union of Wagon Keepers. The Draghi report in particular found that rail rolling 
stock has proven scarce when demand has soared198. Significant aid amounts for the 
acquisition of rolling stock would therefore risk diverting demand from unaided projects 
to aided projects at least in the short term, without a clear net overall benefit in spite of 
generous public spending.  
• There is a competitive leasing market in the freight segment. Stakeholders representing the 
leasing market have indicated that grants for the acquisition of vehicles could distort 
competition between leasing companies and railway undertakings, incentivising the latter to 
buy their own rolling stock instead of leasing it. Furthermore, competition distortions between 
leasing companies and railway undertakings are reduced by allowing leasing companies 
qualifying as SMEs or SMCs to benefit from aid under the same terms as smaller railway 
undertakings.  Compared to the baseline, this Option would promote fairer competition within 
the SLT sector and market entry of smaller and newer players. This effect could be significant. 
While data on financing costs in the rail sector in the EU specifically is not widely available, 
an OECD study has found that borrowing rates of companies that are at least 50% State-owned 
are close to base rates, whereas borrowing rates of companies that are less than 10% State-
owned are more than 3 percentage points higher, in spite of those companies having lower debt-
to-equity ratios and higher productivity199. If those findings are applied to the rail sector, and 
with base rates in most Member States currently below 3%, the conclusion is that the costs of 
financing vehicles for new entrants and smaller companies are at least twice as high as those of 
 
198 See footnote 49, page 211. 
199 Quantifying the role of State enterprises in industrial subsidies, 2024. Base rates refer to risk-free rates and are 
published for all Member States by the European Commission.  

<!-- page:61 -->
 
56 
incumbents. Limiting the aid to smaller and newer players thus constitutes a safeguard 
addressing this current competition distortion and preventing it from being further exacerbated. 
Helping reduce this gap in financing costs could lead to additional competition, resulting in 
lower prices, higher quality of services, etc. However, by limiting the possibilities for granting 
aid for the acquisition of vehicles compared to the baseline, both in terms of beneficiaries and 
the form of aid, Option C1 would not promote the competitiveness of the SLT compared to 
road. This was also put forward by stakeholders during the targeted consultation on the draft 
revised GLs200. 
Under Option C2, more types of aid, in particular loans and grants, would be allowed, to newer 
and smaller companies only. While this would increase competition in the SLT sector, grants 
would not be well calibrated to addressing the existing differences in accessing finance for the 
acquisition of vehicles. Depending on the circumstances, such as monetary conditions in a given 
Member State (e.g. prevailing risk-free interest rates), grants may not be sufficient to level the 
playing field between smaller companies or new entrants and incumbents or large enterprises, 
because the advantage from the latter’s implicit State guarantee would be bigger. In other cases, 
grants may be more than what is needed to level the playing field201. 
Compared to the baseline, this Option promotes fairer competition within the SLT sector, 
though less than Option C1, because grants are not as well calibrated to levelling the playing 
field as guarantees are. It prioritises market entry of smaller and newer players. By limiting the 
beneficiaries, Option C2, as Option C1, would not promote the competitiveness of SLT overall.  
Option C3 promotes the level playing field within the SLT sector, by addressing access to 
finance difficulties, which affect smaller and newer companies especially, but does not promote 
market entry to the same extent as Options C1 and C2. Guarantees to all undertakings still close 
the gap between smaller companies or new entrants and incumbents or large enterprises. A State 
guarantee is worth more to the former than it is to the latter, precisely because of the challenges 
smaller companies and new entrants face in accessing finance, and because of the implicit State 
guarantee from which – according to certain research findings – incumbents benefit anyway.  
This Option therefore contributes to levelling the playing field between new entrants and 
smaller companies on the one hand, and incumbents and large enterprises on the other, even if 
it does so to a lesser extent than Option C1. By limiting the form of aid, Option C3 would not 
promote the competitiveness of SLT overall.  
Administrative burden 
Options C1, C2 and C3 would have no significant impact on administrative burden compared 
to the baseline, since both guarantees and grants are simple, well-known and commonly applied 
State aid instruments. 
Impact on SMEs 
 
200 Annex 2, point 4.4. 
201 In the case practice, the aid intensity of grants for the acquisition of vehicles was typically set at 20-30%, and 
the guarantees were designed (e.g. in terms of coverage and duration) to equate to this aid intensity under average 
conditions (see section 5.5.1). However, the actual gross grant equivalent of a guarantee in a given Member State 
depends e.g. on the monetary conditions in that Member State.  

<!-- page:62 -->
 
57 
Options C1 and C2 would impact SMEs in the relevant sectors very positively. The rules 
would benefit SMEs specifically, attenuating the market failures impacting their capacity to 
acquire rolling stock or vessels.   
Option C3 would also have a positive impact on SMEs compared to the baseline, since 
guarantees necessarily level the playing field between large enterprises and SMEs. The positive 
impact on SMEs would however be smaller than under Options C1 and C2, since under this 
Option incumbents would also be eligible for aid. Hence, the amounts available under a given 
aid scheme would not be reserved for SMEs, but open to all companies. In case of limited 
budgets of aid schemes, SMEs may therefore only get a part of the aid they applied for.  
6.4.2. Environmental impacts 
By excluding large enterprises from aid, Option C1 would lower the overall aid amounts that 
could be granted compared to the baseline and the impact on modal shift would therefore be 
lower, at least in the short term, which has a negative environmental impact, as set out in Section 
6.1.2. However, the removal of grants has countervailing, positive environmental impacts, even 
in the short term, if it encourages Member States to grant aid in other ways:  
• The GHG emissions caused by the manufacturing of SLT vehicle stock are high, 
whereas those of using them are low (this is the case for both rolling stock and IWW 
vessels). In other words, the long lifetime of SLT vehicles compared to the alternatives 
on road is a key element of their sustainability. Modal shift through means other than 
the manufacturing of new vehicles, such as investing in the adaptation and 
modernisation of vehicles, would be more environmentally friendly. 
• Aid for the acquisition of vehicles and operating aid both lower the total cost of 
ownership of vehicles, but the former does not incentivise the actual use of the vehicle, 
as the aid is given once for the acquisition of the vehicle and does not depend on the 
degree of its utilisation and therefore does not incentivise modal shift to the same extent. 
• Because grants for the acquisition of vehicles are likely to result at least partially in price 
increases, the modal shift impact per amount of State aid spending under Option C1 is 
higher than in the baseline. 
• Data from the study202 suggests that new entrants tend to maximise the lifecycle of their 
rolling stock compared to incumbents, which tend to scrap their rolling stock early 
without putting it back on the market for sale or lease, where it could be useful to foster 
modal shift. Levelling the playing field between new entrants and smaller companies, 
and incumbents and large enterprises, may thus promote the circular economy.  
In view of the high resource requirements of manufacturing vehicles and the weaker incentive 
to increase transport volumes (as compared notably to operating aid), aid for the acquisition of 
vehicles is considered the least environmentally friendly way of creating modal shift.  
In the longer term, the strengthening of the position of SMEs and new entrants is expected to 
put pressure on incumbents to use vehicles more intensively as well and to cease or reduce the 
early scrapping of vehicles, thereby further promoting circularity. 
 
202 Pages 69-70. 

<!-- page:63 -->
 
58 
Overall, in the short term, the environmental impact of Option C1 is expected to be neutral. In 
the medium to longer term, by strengthening the level playing field in the SLT sector, it is 
expected to be positive.  
The environmental impact of Option C2 is similar: the removal of grants compared to the 
baseline for large enterprises and incumbents would be expected to have mixed environmental 
effects in the short term, but more positive effects in the longer term.  
Option C3 would be expected to have negative environmental impacts in the short term, since 
the possibilities for granting aid are restricted compared to the baseline (no grants). However, 
research shows that incumbents in the rail sector already benefit from an implicit State 
guarantee (section 2.3.1), and therefore aid in the form of a State guarantee would be worth 
more to new entrants, who do not benefit from such an implicit guarantee. By strengthening the 
position of new entrants in the medium to longer term, guarantees promote circularity, as under 
Options C1 and C2.  
Under Option C3, the medium and longer-term positive environmental effects would be lower 
than under Options C1 and C2, because incumbents would be competing for the same budgetary 
resources, and the availability of guarantees specifically to smaller companies and new entrants 
would be reduced.  
6.4.3. Social impacts 
There are no clear social impacts from Options C1, C2 or C3. All C Options improve 
competition within the SLT sector and promote market entry, but the total amount of aid that 
could be granted would not increase compared to the baseline, rather the contrary. The social 
impacts, for example in terms of pricing or employment, are therefore unclear.  
7. 
HOW DO THE OPTIONS COMPARE? 
This Section provides a summary of the Commission’s assessment of the various options 
available against the baseline. The options identified in Section 5 are compared on the basis of 
their effectiveness, efficiency and coherence, based on the analysis in Section 6.  
The options are scored based on the extent to which they fulfil the specific objectives identified 
for the intervention (Effectiveness). The degree to which specific objectives 1-4 have been met 
is assessed by their competitiveness and internal market impacts as well as their environmental 
impacts, i.e. their contribution to modal shift. The Efficiency of the options is assessed by the 
extent to which they contribute to the reduction of administrative burdens, as well as their social 
impacts and impact on SMEs. Finally, Coherence is assessed by the consistency of the options 
with the aims of the Green Deal and SSMS. The mapping from the assessed impacts to 
Effectiveness, Efficiency and Coherence is illustrated in Figure 8. 
Figure 8: Mapping impacts to Effectiveness, Efficiency and Coherence.

59 
These scoring criteria are not weighted. All policy options have been scored on a scale from ++ 
(very favourable) to -- (very unfavourable). A ‘0’ indicates a neutral or no expected impact.
