All A options would have positive impacts (in comparison to the baseline) on competition, the 
environment, administrative burden and SMEs, only the degree differs. Option A3 would also 
have a slightly positive social impact, as it could fill gaps in rail freight transport at limited cost.  

<!-- page:66 -->
 
61 
Option A1 has positive impacts according to all criteria by putting in place a rulebook in the 
revised GLs for all transport modes and actors which contribute to SLT solutions. The positive 
impacts are reduced by the limitation of the TBER to measures in the scope of the RGL, which 
could incite Member States to predominantly put in place such measures, which could be both 
distortive and inefficient in generating modal shift. In this regard, Options A2 and A3 
particularly benefit competition and environmental protection in general and especially SMEs, 
as they would extend the block exemption to the entire SLT sector.  
Option A3 further improves competition and environmental sustainability, and to a lesser 
degree administrative simplification, by tackling the specific questions raised by compensation 
for rail freight PSOs. It also has a slightly positive social impact. 
Finally, it has been estimated that block exempting measures today falling under the RGL under 
Option A1 would reduce annual administrative costs for Member States by EUR 33 407-115 
254 and the Commission by EUR 21 206-73 161, depending on the degree to which Member 
States avail themselves of these opportunities. The related savings for businesses due to the 
earlier availability of the support have been estimated to be between EUR 6 and 39.3 million, 
again depending on the take-up by Member States. If the block exemption is extended to the 
Commission’s case practice in the SLT sector as per Options A2 and A3, the estimated impact 
of administrative cost reduction amounts to EUR 83 518-178 171 for Member States and EUR 
53 016-113 100 for the Commission. The related opportunity cost savings for businesses would 
be between EUR 7.6 and 43.4 million.

All C Options have positive impacts on the internal market and SMEs in particular, since they 
aim to reduce some of the barriers smaller companies and new entrants face in acquiring rolling 
stock and vessels, also levelling the playing field between competitors in SLT. Because both 
guarantees and grants (more typical in the baseline) are simple instruments from an 
administrative point of view, there is no impact on administrative burden. The environmental 
impacts are limited, because of the mixed impacts of incentivising the more intense use of 
vehicles, which is positive for the circular economy and the environment, while allowing less 
aid to SLT in total, which could discourage modal shift.  
Option C2 consists of grants to smaller companies and new entrants only. By allowing the most 
support among the C Options, and by limiting this support to smaller companies and new 
entrants, it has the most positive impact on SMEs. Option C3 makes State guarantees available 
to all undertakings, thereby contributing to levelling the playing field while ensuring that 
competition is not distorted to the detriment of large enterprises, as is possible under Option 

<!-- page:68 -->
 
63 
C2. Option C1 both levels the playing field and promotes market entry of smaller companies 
and new entrants and therefore has the most positive overall impacts.    
8. 
PREFERRED OPTION 
8.1. 
Identification of the preferred policy option 
8.1.1. Option Bundle A - Scope of the revised GLs and TBER 
Option A3 is proposed. As discussed in Section 6.2 and as shown in Table 11 in Section 7, all 
A Options score either positive or neutral compared to the baseline. However, by block 
exempting the measures most commonly accepted in the Commission’s case practice, Options 
A2 and A3 lead to significant administrative simplification and promote environmental 
protection, while providing adequate safeguards against competition distortions.  
By block exempting only the measures that fall within the scope of the RGL, Option A1 scores 
more poorly than Options A2 and A3 in terms of every single specific objective. The 
Commission’s case practice is robust and consistent enough to block exempt additional 
measures, both in terms of the beneficiaries (other SLT operators than only railway 
undertakings) and in terms of the type of projects supported (including, notably, facilities). A 
restrictive block exemption would limit the extent to which the playing field in the internal 
market is levelled between SLT modes and in the transport sector overall (specific objective 2) 
as well as the level of environmental protection, while the reduction in State aid measures that 
are block exempted would reduce administrative simplification (specific objective 1). Given 
the prevalence of SMEs in the SLT sector and in particular in the IWW sector, excluding that 
sector from the scope of the block exemption would also reduce the beneficial impact on SMEs.  
Finally, by setting out the rules for rail freight PSOs (as there already are for PSOs in the 
passenger segment), Option A3 promotes legal certainty and facilitates implementation, which 
in turn may address gaps in the offer of sustainable freight services, further levelling the playing 
field with road transport and improving competition and environmental protection.  
8.1.2. Option Bundle B – operating and interoperability aid 
Option B2 is proposed. It increases operating aid intensities while ensuring that State aid does 
not exceed the avoided external costs, as is possible under Option B3, in which case the net 
impact of the aid on society would be negative. This Option also introduces a safeguard to 
ensure that capacity constraints on the network of the supported SLT mode do not impede 
modal shift. At the same time, this safeguard is carefully calibrated so as not to introduce 
possible distortions of competition or administrative burden, as results from the distance limits 
under Option B1.  
Option B2 also increases the interoperability aid intensity, without systematically requiring the 
funding gap analysis for aid intensities above 50% entailed by Option B1, which imposes a 
higher administrative burden on Member States and can delay the implementation of projects. 
Given the acute market failures in interoperability projects, the risk of overcompensation is also 
limited. As interoperability aid has major positive environmental and internal market impacts 
(see Table 12), it should be facilitated to the maximum extent.  
8.1.3. Option Bundle C – aid for the acquisition of vehicles 
Option C1 is proposed. All C Options have a positive competition impact by reducing intrinsic 
competitive advantages of incumbents and large enterprises over new entrants and smaller 
companies in the acquisition of rolling stock and vessels. Option C1 targets new entrants and 

<!-- page:69 -->
 
64 
smaller companies, without overshooting and risking undue distortions of competition to the 
detriment of incumbents and large enterprises. The Option promotes both market entry and a 
level playing field in the SLT sector. Option C2, by including grants, does not address the 
specific market failure of access to finance for new entrants and smaller companies and may 
therefore cause undue competition distortions within SLT modes. Option C3 addresses the 
market failure of access to finance directly but, by including all undertakings as beneficiaries, 
does not promote market entry of new entrants and smaller companies as compared to 
incumbents and large enterprises as much as Option C1 does.  
8.1.4. Combined impact of the proposed Options 
Taken together, the proposed preferred Options form a comprehensive and consistent 
framework for State aid to SLT going forward. Being mutually reinforcing, their combined 
impact is greater than the sum of the individual preferred Options taken one by one. The 
preferred Options taken together will revamp the framework applicable to State aid measures 
to SLT both in terms of form/procedure (simplification by codification of applicable rules in 
one text and block exemption respectively) and on substance (incentivising SLT by further 
facilitating aid which is well targeted to identified market failures) and will together contribute 
to achieving the modal shift objective set by the Green Deal, i.e. shifting as a “matter of 
priority” a substantial part of the 75% of inland freight carried today by road to rail and IWW. 
By way of example to illustrate: 
• The expansion of the scope of the new rules to include IWW and multimodal transport 
constitutes a consolidation of many years of case practice as well as a recognition of the 
fact that road transport is still by far the dominant mode of transportation. Depending 
on their geographic characteristics and the Member State concerned, IWW and 
multimodal transport can be a superior alternative to rail transport in terms of cost 
effectiveness, enabling those modes to compete with road transport more strongly, 
especially in the freight segment. The more generous compatibility conditions under the 
preferred Options of Option Bundles B and C in conjunction with the enlargement of 
the scope under the preferred Option A3 compared to the baseline will overall 
strengthen the positive impacts on administrative burden, competition, the single 
market, the environment and SMEs. 
• There is a synergy between the more formal and the more substantial aspects of the 
preferred options, i.e. the fact that the initiative marries environmental benefits with 
administrative simplification. Indeed, in some cases the revised compatibility conditions 
entail in themselves administrative simplification, in that previous requirements which 
were unclear or cumbersome to demonstrate are now dropped. This is the case for 
example as regards the capping of operating aid to 30% of total transport costs, a notion 
which was not defined, unclear and inconsistently applied in the case practice, and 
which will be dropped in the preferred option (Option B2). This reinforces the 
simplification entailed by the wide scope of both the new Guidelines and in particular 
the block exemption (Option A3).  
• The introduction of start-up aid for passenger night trains and rail freight transport (A 
options) will reinforce the competitiveness of new entrants also promoted by the 
improved access to finance for vehicles (Option C1).  
• Aid to finance interoperability investments (Option B2), which will make SLT 
operations more secure and efficient, can be complemented for smaller and newer 
operators with aid for the acquisition of vehicles (Option C1), thereby promoting overall 
the development of a modernised fleet.  

<!-- page:70 -->
 
65 
8.2. 
REFIT (simplification and improved efficiency) 
The current initiative ensures a considerable degree of procedural simplification thanks to the 
introduction of a block exemption, as described in Section 6.2. Expanding the scope of the RGL 
to include other SLT modes and other types of investments will lead to further simplification 
of the procedure: after a limited one-off effort to familiarise themselves with the revised GLs 
and the TBER, Member States will have before them a clear legal framework. They will no 
longer have to study the Commission’s approach on a case-by-case basis, researching a body 
of decisions to identify the conditions under which they can grant aid. Moreover, in case a 
measure is covered by the block exemption, there is no administrative burden linked to 
notification. The annual estimated administrative burden reduction of the proposed Option A3 
amounts to EUR 83 518-178 171 for Member States and EUR 53 016-113 100 for the 
Commission. The related savings for businesses (corresponding to the opportunity costs 
avoided due to earlier availability of the support which does not need to be notified and 
approved) are estimated to be EUR 7.6-43.4 million annually. The compatibility conditions 
proposed will also entail administrative simplification. Notably, the conditions applying to 
operating aid are simplified, so that Member States only have to demonstrate that State aid 
granted does not exceed an external cost ceiling and not, in addition, a total cost ceiling, as is 
currently the case.   
8.3. 
Application of the ‘one in, one out’ approach 
This initiative is out of scope of the ‘one in, one out’ approach, as it does not give rise to 
administrative costs for businesses or citizens, only for Member States’ authorities. 
Administrative costs for Member States’ authorities deriving from this initiative, as well as 
possible indirect effects on businesses, have been assessed and as far as possible quantified in 
Sections 6.2.1, 6.3.1, 6.4.1, 7 and 8.1 and 8.2 above, as well as Annex 8.  
9. 
HOW WILL ACTUAL IMPACTS BE MONITORED AND EVALUATED? 
The monitoring and evaluation of the initiative will be based on publicly available indicators, 
notably by including data from the mandatory State aid control system, which this initiative 
implements for the SLT sector, as it exists for State aid in general. This ensures that basic 
characteristics of aid schemes and aid awards are made public and that periodic reviews are 
conducted to identify the effectiveness and the level of compliance of selected measures. This 
data can be used to measure the impact of the initiative on the general and specific objectives 
set out in Section 4.  
9.1. 
The mandatory control system for State aid measures 
In accordance with Article 108 TFEU, ‘the Commission shall, in cooperation, with Member 
States, keep under constant review all systems of existing aid in those Member States’. The 
European Commission Directorate-General for Competition (DG COMP) implements a State 
aid control system based on three main elements: transparency, monitoring, and ex post 
evaluation.  
Transparency: Transparency under State aid rules ensures the publication of individual aid 
awards to allow for peer review, public control and greater accountability. While the details of 
the State aid measures themselves have been published so far also for SLT in the form of non-
confidential versions of Commission decisions, more granular data related to aid granted per 
beneficiary or reporting on SLT in the framework of the State aid Scoreboard are currently not 
available. This initiative introduces those transparency requirements for the SLT sector, 
aligning them with what is required for other types of aid. Thus, Member States will be required 

<!-- page:71 -->
 
66 
to publish all individual aid awards exceeding a specified threshold in a publicly accessible 
repository. Publication requirements are introduced also for the block exempted measures.  
Ongoing annual monitoring of selected State aid cases: DG COMP monitors every year a 
sample of existing aid schemes covering both notified and block exempted aid schemes. This 
ex post monitoring exercise involves a check of the national legal basis for consistency with the 
approval Commission decision or applicable block exemption, and a review of the 
implementation of the scheme for a sample of beneficiaries. This makes it possible to monitor 
Member States’ respect of the State aid rules and detect difficulties with the implementation of 
specific rules. The SLT sector is and will continue to form part of such monitoring exercises. 
Ex post evaluation of certain State aid measures: Ex post evaluation provides an analysis of 
the effectiveness and efficiency of selected aid measures to enable Member States to improve 
the design of future schemes. State aid evaluation in particular makes it possible (i) to verify 
that the assumptions underlying the approval of the scheme are still valid; (ii) to assess whether 
the scheme is effective in achieving the objective for which it was introduced; and (iii) to cater 
for unforeseeable negative effects. This initiative introduces the principle of ex post evaluation 
to the revised GLs and the TBER. The intention is to make use of this tool in the future, where 
appropriate, i.e. in particular for large aid measures with novel characteristics.   
9.2. 
Measuring the success of the initiative against its objectives  
The success of State aid GLs and block exemptions depends on the extent to which they are 
effective at inducing Member States to design aid measures that can achieve the desired policy 
objectives in a cost-efficient manner, while minimising distortions of competition. In the case 
of the revised GLs and the TBER, measuring success will involve examining inter alia the 
effects on the general objective of encouraging modal shift towards SLT modes, as well the 
specific objectives set out in Section 4 (reducing the external cost gap between road and SLT, 
accelerating the rollout of interoperability technologies, improving access to finance for SMEs 
and new entrants and improving access to facilities and private sidings). 
Success of the initiative would mean: First, an increasing trend in the amounts of State aid 
granted by Member States for SLT203 would indicate a contribution to the general objective of 
increasing the modal share of SLT. Second, the number of measures and amounts of aid under 
the TBER should follow an increasing trend, indicating that State aid rules allow Member 
States to allocate larger aid amounts faster. Third, the duration of the notification process 
should show a decreasing trend. Finally, success of the initiative would ideally entail an 
increasing trend in the share of SLT modes in passenger and freight transport204. However, 
this can only be a secondary indicator as the evolution of the indicator is influenced by several 
other factors. These include consumer choices between transport modes, the evolution of the 
costs of different inputs or of the fuels used for providing each transport mode, the difficulty 
users have to access different transport modes depending on location, the importance users 
attribute to the duration of the journey, etc.  
For the specific objective of reducing the external cost gap between road and SLT (SO1), the 
initiative would be considered successful if, following the adoption of the new rules, the

https://competition-cases.ec.europa.eu/search?caseInstrument=SA.

<!-- page:72 -->
 
67 
amount of operating aid granted to cover external costs increases, along with the average 
intensity of aid. A secondary indicator could be a decline in prices for rail or IWW transport 
compared to those of road and air transport (available from Eurostat205 and different market 
reports), though the evolution is affected also by changes in prices of energy and other input 
costs. For the specific objective of accelerating the rollout of interoperability technologies 
(SO2) success will be achieved if the deployment of ERTMS206 shows an increasing trend.  
Improving access to finance for SMEs and new entrants (SO3) would be achieved primarily by 
an increase of the aid granted to new entrants and SMEs (available from the State aid 
transparency database207) while a secondary indicator would be the increase in the market 
shares of SMEs and new entrants in the SLT208. The objective of improving access to 
facilities and private sidings (SO4) will be achieved if the number of multimodal terminals209 
shows an increasing trend, in particular those modernised to handle trains of 750m, if the 
number and length of private sidings increases, and if  the use of multimodal transport 
containers in rail transport increases210.  
Annex 12 shows a full list of envisaged indicators, as well as the relevant sources of data. 
Besides the State aid control system, other indicators will be based on publicly available data 
sources, such as Eurostat or regular Commission reports, avoiding additional administrative 
burden on companies and Member States.

2. Organisation and timing 
The Inter-Service Steering Group (‘ISSG’) was set up in July 2021 and gathered representatives 
from the Commission’s Secretariat General, Legal Service and 12 other Directorates-General: 
CLIMA, CNECT, DEFIS, ECFIN, ENER, ENV, ESTAT, GROW, MOVE, REGIO, RTD and 
TRADE. The ISSG was consulted on the IIA, the terms of reference for the study and the 
questionnaire for the public consultation, as well as the CfE for the TBER. Furthermore, the 
ISSG received a debrief of the results of the public and targeted consultations, the final study, 
as well as a preview of the proposals developed for the draft GLs and TBER and the Impact 
Assessment Report.  
DG COMP organised 8 ISSG meetings on 10 September 2021, 3 December 2021, 6 September 
2022, 12 October 2023, 23 February 2024, 24 January 2025, 4 July 2025, and 11 September 
2025. On a number of deliverables, the group was consulted in writing (details in Table 14 
below).

• No additional public consultation on a questionnaire was carried out for the TBER, as

3. Consultation of the RSB 
An Upstream meeting with the RSB took place on 30 September 2022 to discuss DG COMP's 
Impact Assessment for the revised GLs and new TBER. The RSB members and DG COMP 
discussed the scope of the Impact Assessment, i.e. the focus on the main choices to inform 
policy makers on the key political decisions. In this respect, the RSB made clear that the 
intervention logic was of utmost importance, as it constitutes the backbone of the Impact 
Assessment. Particular attention should be paid to defining the specific objectives, against 
which options are assessed, and to finding the supporting evidence. The RSB reminded DG 
COMP that possible negative consequences of the revised GLs and new TBER should be clearly 
spelled out and assessed as well. An important point raised by DG COMP and taken up by 
several RSB members was coherence with other Green Deal initiatives. Several RSB members 
stressed the need for DG COMP to stay in close contact with other DGs, because decisions 
taken in other Commission initiatives can affect the initiative at hand, but also to spell out the 
contribution of the initiative at hand to achieving the objectives of the Green Deal.  
A second complementary Upstream meeting with the RSB took place on 2 June 2025. It focused 
on the approach to analysing impacts and specifically the level of detail required for analysing 
environmental, social and competitiveness impacts, as well as the methodology for estimating 
 
211 https://ec.europa.eu/info/better-regulation-guidelines-and-toolbox_en.  

<!-- page:76 -->
 
A-4 
 
administrative costs. RSB members emphasised that the future report should assess to what 
extent the intervention would likely impact each problem identified. Additionally, they 
underscored the importance of using modelling techniques to support the Impact Assessment, 
such as scenario analysis, and to explicitly outline key assumptions and uncertainties. RSB 
members recommended the approach of disaggregating key environmental impacts. The 
competitiveness assessment should be tied to the overarching economic analysis. Similarly, the 
future report should provide a clear assessment of the potential impacts on SMEs. RSB 
members noted the value of active participation by Member States and economic operators in 
consultation activities, particularly in estimating costs, to ensure accurate and reliable data. 
The RSB meeting took place on 12 November 2025. In summary, the Board gave a positive 
opinion with reservations, stating that the following aspects had to be rectified: 
• The intervention logic does not present sufficiently clear problems, problem drivers as 
well as the expected contribution of State aid to the uptake of SLT and its interplay with 
other initiatives aimed at addressing market failures in the transport sector. 
• The report does not provide a sufficient economic analysis, including for the 
specification of options. 
RSB comment 
Action taken 
(1) The intervention logic should better 
identify the underlying causes of the 
problems, such as alleged market failures, 
regulatory failures, including the root causes 
of slow processing of state aid, or 
behavioural biases; and describe how the 
intervention will contribute to the increase of 
the share of sustainable land transports. The 
report needs to articulate the relationship 
between this intervention and other EU 
policies in the area of sustainable land 
transport, including analysis of ETS2. 
Sections 2.1, 2.2 and 2.3 were redrafted to 
reflect a reconceptualised intervention logic. 
In addition, Section 4 was also redrafted to be 
coherent with the problems and problem 
drivers. Section 1.2 was expanded as regards 
the relation with other initiatives, and in 
particular to also address ETS2.  
(2) The report should better capture upfront 
the magnitude and scale of State aid in these 
sectors, and its share of the total volume of 
State aid. The experience gained from the 
State aid implementation and modernisation 
should be used more in the report to underpin 
this analysis. The analysis of the case practice 
that is at the basis of the proposed 
modification of the State aid Railway 
Guidelines, should be included in the report. 
Section 1.1 was redrafted to reflect an 
analysis of the information in Annex 7 and 
further additional analysis of case practice. In 
addition, Section 1.3 was expanded and 
clarified. Finally, further analysis of the case 
practice was also added in Sections 5.2 – 5.5.  
(3) The economic analysis should be more 
robust, using quantitative data to substantiate 
the expected impacts of the intervention. It 
should analyse, to the extent possible, the 
substitution but also the income effects of the 
additional State aid measures. 
A new Section 6.1.1 was added to address 
this point. 

<!-- page:77 -->
 
A-5 
 
(4) The potential risks of distortions to 
competition need to be better analysed. The 
report should assess safeguards which could 
mitigate these risks. The report should better 
account for different levels of fiscal 
constraints in the Member States that would 
affect implementation of the framework. It 
should also provide a more detail on the 
funding gap analysis. 
Additional analysis of fiscal constraints was 
added in Section 6.1.1. In addition, the 
funding gap and its impact was better 
explained in Sections 5.2 and 6.3.1.  
(5) The report should more clearly spell out 
the impacts of different policy options. The 
environmental impacts of options bundle C, 
for example, should look into the effects of 
the 
implicit 
guarantees 
enjoyed 
by 
incumbents 
and 
how 
excluding 
large 
enterprises 
would 
incentivise 
different 
commercial 
behaviours. 
Similarly, 
the 
different environmental impacts of options 
within bundle B should be more clearly 
explained. 
Sections 6 and 7 were reviewed; in particular, 
changes were made to Sections 6.4.2 on the 
C options and Sections 6.3.2 and 7 on the B 
options to take into account the examples. 
(6) The monitoring framework, as presented 
in the report and detailed in Annex 12, does 
not include sufficiently RACER indicators. 
Section 9 was revised in line with the 
reconceptualised intervention logic and with 
information from Annex 12. Annex 12 was 
equally updated.  
 
4. Evidence, sources and quality 
The Impact Assessment for the revision of the RGL and the new TBER was supported by an 
external study212. The study was procured under the Framework contract COMP/2017/013 for 
the provision of support studies for evaluations and impact assessments in the area of State aid 
policy signed on 24 May 2018. The request for services was accepted by a consortium of DIW 
Berlin, E.CA Economics, LEAR, Sheppard Mullin Richter & Hampton LLP, and the University 
of East Anglia, who made an offer on 9 December 2021. The contract was awarded and signed 
on 16 and 22 December 2021 respectively. The study mainly covered four areas of interest: 
status of rail infrastructure, accessibility and costs (including modernisation) pertaining to 
rolling stock, profitability and demand elasticity of rail freight services and effectiveness of 
State aid.  
The analysis is based on a multiple sources: academic literature, databases (including those 
developed by EU Bodies (Eurostat, European Railway Agency) and national Registration 
Entities of the EU Member States, surveys and interviews targeting stakeholders in twelve 
Member States and Switzerland, including European umbrella associations, infrastructure 
managers, national market regulators, IWW operators, rolling stock leasing companies, 
granting authorities, railway undertakings and intermodal operators. 
 
212 See footnote 50. 

<!-- page:78 -->
 
A-6 
 
The project was also supported by several consultation activities, including two advisory 
committees and a multilateral meeting with Member States and the European Economic Area 
(‘EEA’) States. More details on this are provided in Annex 2. 
ANNEX 2: STAKEHOLDER CONSULTATION (SYNOPSIS REPORT) 
1. Introduction 
Significant consultation work was carried out to collect information and data for the revision of 
the RGL and design of the TBER.  
2. Outline of the consultation strategy 
The Commission promoted the initiative on the Better Regulation Portal (“Have your say”)213. 
The aim of the consultation activities was to collect supporting information and data on SLT so 
that the rules could be simplified, updated in line with key EU priorities and adapted to the 
sector’s needs, and to support the Impact Assessment for the revised GLs as well as the new 
TBER. In particular, the consultation activities were designed to allow interested parties to 
provide their feedback as regards the potential policy options. 
2.1. Stakeholders 
The primary stakeholders are Member States’ granting authorities and transport regulators 
(yellow), and all possible beneficiaries of State aid measures that support SLT and their 
competitors (green). Attention has been given to business associations and organisations of the 
above categories, as well as to stakeholders possessing detailed expertise.   
Specific stakeholder categories, selected in function of the impact the revision of the RGL and 
the new TBER will have on them (from most to least impacted), include: 
- 
Local and national aid granting authorities 
- 
National and EU regulatory bodies 
- 
Railway undertakings 
- 
Terminal owners and operators 
- 
Multimodal transport operators  
- 
Organisers of multimodal freight transport 
- 
Freight forwarders 
- 
Logistics operators  
- 
Shippers 
- 
Rolling stock leasing companies 
- 
IWW and maritime freight transport operators 
- 
Railway infrastructure managers 
- 
Road hauliers

State-aid-guidelines_en 
and

-

level 
-

- 
Consumers and consumers’ associations 
 
2.2. Methods of engagement 
A balanced and comprehensive coverage of stakeholder views has been ensured through open 
and targeted consultation methods. A set of different consultation tools has been used, each 
focusing on different types of stakeholders. While Member States’ authorities and relevant 
stakeholder associations have been consulted via targeted interviews and meetings, the online 
public consultation reached out to them in a more formalised way, but also to a wider audience, 
e.g. industry, as well as sectoral think-tanks, academics and researchers and individual citizens. 
Given the scope and the stakeholder base, the following activities have been carried out in the 
consultation process:  
• Four-week feedback period on the IIA214 on the revised GLs and the CfE for the 
TBER215; 
• Public consultation (12 weeks) through a questionnaire216. It was published in 
English, French and German;  
• Targeted consultations conducted by the contractor of the study by way of survey 
questionnaires and interviews of key stakeholders; 
• Targeted consultation on the proposed revised GLs and TBER available on DG 
COMP’s website217; 
• Meeting with the Member States on 10 September 2024 during the consultation on 
the proposed revised GLs and TBER drafts and on 18 June 2025 during the second 
consultation of Member States on the TBER draft;  
• Targeted consultation of relevant national and regional authorities regarding the 
administrative burden reduction entailed by the TBER; and 
• Bilateral meetings with Member States, representatives of the sector, stakeholder 
associations, and other interested stakeholders.

<!-- page:80 -->
 
A-8 
 
Moreover, the revision of the RGL and introduction of the TBER also benefitted from the 
results of a consultation already carried out in the framework of the Fitness Check of the SAM 
package, during which further submissions also regarding the specific GLs were received. 
3. Methodology and tools used to process the data 
The Commission used a combination of questionnaires, meetings with interested stakeholders, 
public consultations and working groups with Member States, to ensure a transparent and 
comprehensive methodology for the assessment of the data collected. 
In particular, one public consultation and two targeted consultations were launched to provide 
all stakeholders the possibility to contribute to the initiative submitting backward-looking data 
and forward-looking feedback. More specifically, the feedback from the consultations, as well 
as the feedback to the IIA and CfE were analysed internally by the Commission.  
4. The results of the stakeholder consultations 
This Point summarises the views and evidence collected from the different categories of 
stakeholders during the consultation activities. The fact that for all consultation activities a large 
share of the replies comes from businesses and business associations has significantly affected 
the representativeness of the outcome. The nature of these replies reveals a general bias towards 
a wider scope and increased level of State aid by the likely affected stakeholders, even though 
there are diverging views on specific topics and by sectors competing with the targeted 
beneficiaries. To this extent, Member States and NGOs, albeit outnumbered by the former 
categories, added alternative insights. 
4.1. The feedback on the IIA for the revised GLs 
From 1 to 29 October 2021, the Commission published the IIA for the revision of the RGL in 
order to allow stakeholders to provide their feedback on the initiative. 15 stakeholders provided 
a response, of which 3 were companies and 7 were business associations. Moreover, 1 academic 
association, 2 EU citizens, 1 navigation authority and 1 regional public entity provided 
feedback.  
The relevance of the RGL was not questioned. Moreover, two thirds of the respondents 
specifically welcomed the revision of the RGL or the introduction of a TBER in their reply218. 
Feedback touched on the following points in particular:  
• SLT and decarbonisation: 
Three contributors suggested being more generous as regards operating aid for external 
costs to support sustainable transport and particularly the decarbonisation of rail 
transport219. 
• Level playing field: 
Three respondents highlighted the need to develop a fair, non-discriminatory and 
transparent distribution of State aid, arguing that entry barriers for new entrants are high, 
and that market entry needs to be facilitated220.   
 
218 6 business associations out of 7 (85.7%), 3 companies out of 3 (100%), 1 regional public entity out of 1 (100%). 
219 1 business association out of 7 (14.3%), 1 company out of 3 (33.3%), 1 regional public entity out of 1 (100%). 
220 1 business association out of 7 (14.3%), 1 company out of 3 (33.3%), 1 academic institution out of 1 (100%).  

<!-- page:81 -->
 
A-9 
 
• Access to rolling stock: 
6 respondents provided comments on the acquisition of rolling stock, with half being 
rather against and half rather in favour. Three respondents considered that aid in the 
form of lump sums should be prohibited221 across the sector or in the freight segment 
only, while another three respondents considered that ambitious measures are 
necessary222 either for all vehicles or for clean vehicles only.     
• New technologies: 
Four respondents highlighted the importance of State aid for new technologies, 
including in particular interoperability technologies as well as, in one case, R&D223. 
4.2. The feedback on the CfE for the TBER 
From 6 March to 3 April 2024, the Commission published the CfE on the introduction of the 
TBER in order to allow stakeholders to provide their feedback. 31 stakeholders provided a 
response (of which 8 via e-mail shortly after the deadline for responses on the “Have your Say” 
portal expired), of which 14 business associations and 2 businesses, 9 public authorities, 4 EU 
citizens and 2 NGOs. Business associations relayed also the views of SMEs, with one business 
association representing SMEs only and two business associations representing parts of the 
IWW sector, which is dominated by SMEs. 
The respondents welcomed the introduction of a TBER. More concretely, feedback touched on 
the following points: 
• 13 respondents224 consider that the TBER would considerably simplify and streamline 
the granting of aid while decreasing the administrative burden for Member States and 
accelerate the implementation of State aid measures. 6 stakeholders highlighted the 
importance of the TBER besides the rail sector also for the IWW sector225. 
• Respondents proposed to block exempt operating aid226 and investment aid for 
interoperability227. In addition, many also suggested block exempting investment aid for 
facilities and infrastructure228. Views were divided regarding aid for the acquisition of 
rolling stock, where market failures were invoked by some stakeholders as a reason to 
include such aid measures in the TBER229, while others pointed to a high distortive risk 
for such aid, warranting a notification230. Few stakeholders also mentioned PSOs for 
rail freight as a candidate for block exemption231.

• In general, 6 stakeholders advocated for higher aid intensities in the TBER than in the 
RGL in particular for operating aid232, some up to 100% of eligible cost. For investment 
aid in facilities and infrastructure, the funding gap approach was put forward by one 
business association and one public authority.  
• 7 respondents233 emphasized the need to limit any distortion and to let the market 
deliver, where this is most efficient. Three respondents234 specifically mentioned open 
and non-discriminatory allocation of aid and the use of aid schemes under the TBER. 
 
4.3. The public consultation on the basis of a questionnaire 
The public consultation was carried out through a questionnaire published on EU Survey, which 
ran from 22 December 2021 until 16 March 2022. The objective of the consultation was to 
gather input from all interested stakeholders on problems existing in the field of SLT, and on 
the way a review of the RGL and the introduction of the TBER could help address these 
problems in line with the Green Deal and the SSMS. The consultation contained 26 questions 
on different topics of relevance, with the additional possibility for the contributors to elaborate 
on their answers. The questionnaire was answered to by 70 stakeholders235, divided as follows: 
• 41 replies (58% of the respondents) were received from the business sector, of which 
26 replies by companies236 (37%), 12 replies (17%) by business associations, and 3 
replies (4%) by trade unions. 
• 15 replies came from public authorities (21%), 5 from NGOs (7%), 3 from EU citizens 
(4%) and 1 from an academic institution (1%).   
• 5 respondents identified themselves as not belonging to any of the above categories 
(‘other’). 
In terms of geographical distribution, answers were received from 20 countries. The Member 
States in which most respondents are based were Italy (11 replies), Belgium (11), France (9), 
Austria (6) and Germany (5). Among the 20 countries, 4 countries are not members of the EU 
(the UK, the US, Norway and Switzerland). 
From a thematic point of view, the questionnaire was divided in eight sections, in regard of 
which the stakeholders took the following positions:

only those replies that were submitted via EU Survey. However, where additional qualitative remarks were made 
in the responses received later, those are included in the presentation in this Point.

A) Support to modal shift towards less polluting and more sustainable transport modes 
Stakeholders were of the view that the rules of the RGL were not sufficiently generous, with 
66% of respondents (46 out of 70)237 considering that the existing operating aid rules were 
insufficient to support a sustainable transfer of traffic, including all responding companies238, 
regardless of their size and date of market entry, and all responding relevant business 
associations.239 11% (8 out of 70)240 thought they were sufficient, and the remaining 23% (16)241 
were unsure. Public authorities were particularly divided, without any identifiable pattern based 
on the level of governance (e.g. regional or national) of the authority, its geographical location, 
its size or its budgetary expenditure: FR, HU, CZ, LV as well as two IT regions indicated that 
the existing operating rules were insufficient, while NL, FI, IT and an IT port authority 
considered them sufficient, and RO, LT, a FR region and two IWW authorities did not know. 
The respondents who replied that the RGL rules on operating aid are not sufficient specified 
the sectors that in their opinion would require more far-reaching public measures: 80% (37 
replies)242 indicated intermodal freight services, another 78% (36 replies)243 indicated first 
mile/last mile only rail services and 63% (29 replies)244 indicated SWL services. The reasons 
for widening the public support to these services, according to the stakeholders, lie mainly with 
the unprofitability of the service (54% - 38 out of 70)245, the insufficiency of available 
infrastructure (51% - 36 out of 70)246 and its inadequacy (49% - 34 out of 70)247. As to the 
current level of the total costs of rail transport threshold (30%), a large majority of 80% of 
respondents (56 out of 70)248 considered that this threshold was too low, while only 3% (2 out 
of 70)249 deemed it appropriate and the remaining 17% (12 out of 70)250 were unsure. Similarly,

C) Access to rolling stock 
A clear majority of respondents (69% (48 out of 70)271) considered that railway undertakings 
face difficulties in accessing passenger rolling stock, including all relevant272 public authorities 
except one, and all relevant273 undertakings, regardless of their size and date of market entry, 
except one incumbent. The main difficulties cited were the purchase or renewal of electric 
multiple units, locomotives and coaches, particularly due to price levels (including leasing 
costs) and the technical barriers hindering interoperability and preventing railway undertakings 
from freely operating in different parts of the European railway network. In line with these 
concerns, a majority of 61% of the stakeholders (43 out of 70)274 expressed their support for 
State aid rules favouring the purchase or lease of passenger rolling stock, particularly through 
grants (34 out of 70275 or 49%) and guarantees (28 out of 70276 or 40%). Conversely, 4% (3)277 
were not in favour of such rules and the remaining 34% (24)278 were unsure.  
Similar positions were expressed with regard to freight rolling stock. Once again, price levels 
represented the main source of concern for respondents, together with access to finance (loans 
and guarantees) and the lack of suitable offer from the railway supply industry. Accordingly, a 
majority of 54% of the stakeholders (38 out of 70)279 supported the introduction of State aid 
rules favouring the purchase or lease of freight rolling stock, including all relevant280 companies

G) Financial transactions concerning railway undertakings 
With regard to State aid granted to vertically integrated railway undertakings, 37% of 
stakeholders (26 out of 70)304 considered that there is a risk of cross-subsidisation between 
commercial and non-commercial activities, whereas 36% (25 out of 70)305 did not see such risk. 
Amongst public authorities, all responding306 Member States except one indicated that they did 
not see such risk. Furthermore, amongst the 12 companies replying that they did not see such 
risk, 10 were publicly owned. By contrast, amongst the 9 companies seeing such risk, 6 were 
independent railway undertakings. As regards the identification of State aid in their practical 
experience, 47% of the respondents (33 out of 70)307 stated not to have had issues and only 13% 
(9 out of 70)308, including only 1 Member State, stated that they had encountered issues. 
H) Regional bonuses

contain aid intensity bonuses, for example to investments in assisted areas, while 20% (14 out 
of 70)310 were against. Amongst public authorities, no clear pattern can be identified based on 
the level of governance (e.g. regional or national) of the authority, its geographical location, its 
size or its budgetary expenditure. For instance, amongst public authorities active at national 
level, 3 expressed themselves against regional bonuses (FI, HU and CZ), while IT, RO and LV 
expressed themselves in favour, and FR, NL and LT did not know, evidencing a disparity of 
views not linked to geographical location or budgetary expenditure. Similarly, one IT region

4.4. The targeted consultation on the draft revised GLs and TBER (including the 
meeting with Member States on these draft texts) 
From 18 June to 20 September 2024, the draft revised GLs and TBER were published on the 
DG COMP website for comments311. 87 stakeholders provided a response on these draft texts, 
of which 41 business associations and 12 companies, 29 EU public authorities (including 19 
Member States authorities)312, 1 EFTA authority, 1 EU citizen, 1 foundation and 2 trade unions. 
Business associations relayed also the views of SMEs, with some business associations 
specifically representing new entrants in the rail sector or parts of the IWW sector that are 
dominated by SMEs. 
On Tuesday, 10 September 2024, DG COMP held an Advisory Committee and Multilateral 
Meeting on the draft texts. Participants came from all 27 EU Member States and Norway, the 
EFTA Surveillance Authority and the Commission (General Secretariat, DG REGIO, DG 
MOVE, DG AGRI, DG CLIMA, DG ENV, DG CNECT, DG NEAR, DG JUST and DG 
MARE. 
After a presentation of the proposals by DG COMP, several Member States313 intervened to 
present first reactions and questions. The comments echoed the comments provided in writing 
as part of the targeted consultation and are therefore presented together with the summary of 
the written responses provided below. 
General comments 
10 Member States314 expressly welcomed the new package, in particular the broadening of the 
scope and the consolidation of the case practice in the field of land transport.   
8 stakeholders315 asked for clarifications about the interplay between the texts and other State 
aid instruments (General Block Exemption Regulation or ‘GBER’316. / Guidelines on State aid 
for climate, environmental protection and energy 2022 or ‘CEEAG’317, / Regulation (EC) 
1370/2007 of 23 October 2007 on public passenger transport services by rail and by road or 
‘Regulation 1370/2007’)318.), particularly in terms of aid cumulation. Moreover, 11

A-19 
 
stakeholders319 raised concerns about the obligation foreseen in the revised GLs to adjust 
existing aid schemes to the new rules within 1 year from their entering into force and asked 
either for its deletion or amendment (e.g. allowing for a transition period of 18 months or two 
years instead). 
Operating aid to cover part of the difference in external costs (GHG emissions, pollution, 
congestion, etc.) between road transport and sustainable land transport modes 
Stakeholders expressed concerns regarding the introduction of certain conditions which, in their 
view, would create an administrative burden and called on the Commission to consider higher 
aid intensities. 
The inclusion of break-even distances in the draft texts was the single-most commented issue. 
41 stakeholders (47.1%) opposed the use of break-even limits, with 26 (30%) stakeholders 
calling for them to be abolished320, and 15 (17.2%) stakeholders calling for them to be 
increased321. No stakeholder expressed support. Among other arguments, stakeholders 
considered that identifying the distance travelled for every single operation would present a 
considerable administrative burden, that there was no “one size fits all” break-even distance 
since the cost of an operation depended on many factors unrelated to distance, such as the 
capacity of the network and the availability of terminals, and that break-even distances would 
discriminate between Member States based on the size of their territories.  
DE and a rail association considered that the proposed provision on capacity constraints should 
be abolished because the Single European Railway Area Directive already sets rules for 
enhancing capacity in case of congested infrastructure. Stakeholders also raised questions on 
how capacity constraints would be assessed and what kind of measures Member States could 
take to address such constraints. 
18 stakeholders, including DE, FR, AT, a rail association and two railway undertakings, called 
for the aid intensities in the draft texts to be increased, particularly under the TBER. Among 
the 18 stakeholders, 6 also called for a separate category for operating aid for infrastructure use 
to be introduced. ES expressed support for the proposed 50% TBER ceiling. A rail association 
noted that operating aid should not be the primary method to support the modal shift and that it 
would be better to focus on aid for infrastructure use.

Operating aid that covers part of the operating losses of operators starting new routes, in the 
first years of operation  
6 Stakeholders322 had questions or comments about the eligible operations and the kinds of 
costs that would be considered eligible. Several stakeholders also expressed concerns about 
possible abuse of the rules to grant aid for operations that are not genuinely “new”.  
A rail association called for this aid category to be limited to new entrants. Two other rail 
associations argued that the aid should be strictly controlled, and a railway undertaking called 
for start-up aid to be allowed under the revised GLs only. On the other hand, DE and a business 
association representing the IWW sector expressed support for the block exemption of this 
category of aid. 
Five stakeholders323 called for start-up aid in the passenger segment to also be available to new 
services connecting passenger stations less than 400 km apart even if they would not be cross-
border. Some stakeholders called for the extension of the eligible costs to situations in which 
the new commercial connection would be contemplated but ultimately not launched, to 
compensate operators for costs related to feasibility studies or experimentation. 
RO and organisations representing the IWW sector called for the IWW sector to be included in 
the scope for passenger start-up aid. 
Operating aid for public services in the rail freight sector  
Four stakeholders324 expressly welcomed the inclusion of freight PSO compensation in the draft 
revised GLs. 
Only a few stakeholders asked for changes, notably: 
 
• Four stakeholders325 asked for a block-exemption of PSO compensation in rail freight 
transport, without however providing any specific arguments in support.  
 
• Three stakeholders326 asked to remove PSO compensation from the draft revised GLs 
considering that market forces should be the main driver of market growth in the rail 
freight transport sector. FI indicated to be open to accept PSO compensation in the case 
of very specific market failures (e.g. SWL).

Investment aid for the construction, upgrade and renewal of rail and IWW facilities such as 
freight terminals or maintenance facilities  
Reactions to the proposed conditions were generally positive. 
6 stakeholders327 asked for an increase in the notification thresholds to further reduce 
administrative burden. PL and FR proposed EUR 20 million for ad hoc aid and DE, PL and FR 
proposed EUR 30 million or even EUR 50 million for awards under aid schemes. 
There was a call for further simplification of proportionality rules, with stakeholders asking 
either (i) to remove the funding gap requirement328 or to increase the aid amount below which 
only an aid intensity applies - i.e. for small projects329 or (ii) to increase the aid intensity 
threshold330 . 
Investment aid for the construction, upgrade and renewal of private sidings, i.e. privately 
owned and operated pieces of rail infrastructure, such as rail tracks, which connect to the 
public rail network    
Reactions to the proposed conditions were generally positive. 
Both FR and AT asked to include loading and unloading facilities and equipment as eligible 
costs. FR in particular pointed to the fact that these costs were eligible for support under an 
approved scheme (SA.107140). 
4 stakeholders asked for an increase of notification thresholds to further reduce administrative 
burden: FR and a railway undertaking asked for an increase to EUR 2.5 million, a business 
association to EUR 4 million and DE to EUR 5 million. 
There was a call for further simplification of proportionality rules, with stakeholders asking 
either i) to remove the funding gap requirement331 or to increase the aid amount for small 
projects below which only an aid intensity applies332 or ii) to increase the aid intensity 
threshold333. 
Investment aid for the acquisition of vehicles for rail or IWW transport 
Around a quarter of stakeholders334 advocated in favour of enlarging the scope of this type of 
aid to all undertakings, including large undertakings and incumbents. Companies sharing this 
view were only incumbent railway undertakings and a publicly owned local transport company. 
In the same vein, business organisations representing incumbent railway undertakings and the

A-22 
 
rail supply industry supported this view. 6 business organisations (i.e. 50% of the business 
organisations expressing support) were exclusively linked to AT or DE undertakings. The main 
arguments raised in support of this claim pertained to (i) the contribution of large undertakings 
and incumbents to the achievement of the Green Deal objectives, (ii) an alleged difficulty to 
access finance experienced by undertakings of all sizes in the land transport sector (due to high 
upfront investment costs, low profitability, long depreciation), (iii) the need to modernise the 
rail fleet to adapt it to interoperability technologies, (iv) the “arm’s-length conditions” of loans 
between incumbents and their subsidiaries, allegedly excluding any advantage over new 
entrants, and (v) an allegation that the proposed rules would infringe Article 345 TFEU, which 
prohibits discrimination between public and private ownership. Three respondents335 expressed 
themselves in favour of the proposed limitation of this type of aid to SMEs and new entrants, 
namely one public authority, one business association representing new entrants and a new 
entrant. 
One business association representing rail freight wagon keepers suggested distinguishing 
between freight and passenger transport services, and to abolish any aid to freight transport. To 
support that claim, the stakeholder held that the two markets had not been liberalised at the 
same time and were factually in a different situation. It also argued that the rail freight market 
functioned well without aid to rolling stock: railway undertakings and leasing companies 
worked together satisfactorily, irrespective of their size. By contrast, an incumbent railway 
undertaking requested that all undertakings providing commercial rail passenger services would 
be excluded from the benefit of the aid, alleging that new entrants recently managed to raise 
capital to acquire rolling stock and that the proposed rules would thus create a mere deadweight 
effect. 
Around a quarter of stakeholders336 (showing significant overlap with the stakeholders who 
expressed a view on the scope of beneficiaries) called for not limiting the form of the aid to 
guarantees and allowing direct grants and loans. Companies sharing this view were, here also, 
only incumbent railway undertakings and a publicly owned local transport company. In the 
same vein, business organisations representing incumbent railway undertakings and the rail 
supply industry supported this view. 5 business organisations (i.e. 41.7% of the business 
organisations expressing support) were exclusively linked to AT or DE undertakings. 25% of 
the business organisations expressing support were active in the IWW sector. The arguments 
raised pertained to the fact that (i) other aid categories within the revised GLs and the TBER 
(in particular interoperability aid) and within the CEEAG/GBER (in particular aid for the 
greening of the fleet) were not limited in terms of form; (ii) the absence of limitation in terms 
of form would bring flexibility and reduce administrative burden for Member States; and (iii) 
guarantees were not sufficient to foster the acquisition of vehicles by new entrants and SMEs. 
Three respondents337, namely one public authority; a new entrant and a business organisation 
representing rolling stock lessors, supported the limitation in the form of the aid. 
France was the only stakeholder, with Ireland, to express a view on the proportionality of the 
guarantee conditions. FR requested that the maximum duration of the guarantee be extended

4.5. The targeted consultation of Member States’ authorities concerning the 
administrative burden reduction associated with the introduction of the TBER 
To collect more specific data on the administrative burden of the different State aid procedures 
in the SLT sector, the Commission carried out a targeted consultation between 24 June and 20 
September 2024. Member State authorities were approached with a questionnaire and asked to 
assess the time spent on the different steps of notification (including both the preparatory phase 
ahead of formal notification and the phase following formal notification) and block exemption 
procedures345, as well as the implied delays of the notification procedure on the implementation 
of State aid measures.  
In total, the Commission received 15 replies from 10 different Member States (some sent 
several replies from different regions or different authorities)346. The details of these responses, 
as well as the analysis of administrative costs, which is based on the results of the targeted 
consultation, is presented in Annex 8. 
4.6. The second targeted consultation of Member States on the draft TBER (and 
meeting with Member States on the draft TBER) 
On Wednesday, 18 June 2025, DG COMP held a second Advisory Committee Meeting on the 
revised draft TBER, which had been shared with Member States on 16 April 2025, and 
presented the main changes that the Commission services planned to make to the revised GLs. 
Participants came from all EU Member States, the EFTA Surveillance Authority and the 
Commission (General Secretariat, DG REGIO, DG MOVE, DG ECFIN, DG CLIMA, DG 
CNECT, DG RTD and ESTAT). 
After a presentation of the proposals by DG COMP, several Member States intervened to 
present first reactions and questions.

5. Contributions received outside the formal consultation context 
The Commission services have been available to meet stakeholders during the entire period of 
the initiative (2021-2025). In particular, the Commission services regularly met sector 
representatives and companies affected by the initiative who provided arguments in favour of 
their eligibility for aid and on the appropriate design of State aid schemes, as well as Member 
States to take into account their experience with State aid measures. All presentations and 
supporting studies provided on these occasions were taken into account in the context of the 
initiative. 
6. Use of the information gathered 
The results of the consultations allowed the Commission to collect a very significant number 
of views and opinions on the initiative. These may not be representative at a statistical level, 
due to the relatively small number of answers from some categories of stakeholders, but are 
significant in terms of quality.

ANNEX 3: WHO IS AFFECTED AND HOW? 
1. Practical implications of the initiative 
The revision of the GLs and introduction of the TBER aim at increasing the modal share of 
SLT by making it more competitive compared to road transport. The costs and benefits of the 
preferred options are expected to fall differently on different stakeholder groups. The initiative 
will mainly affect the following stakeholders: State aid granting authorities in Member States, 
operators of SLT, road hauliers, transport organisers, and passengers of SLT.    
1.1. Member States 
Member States and European Free Trade Association (EFTA) States would have to take into 
consideration the updated rules in the revised GLs and the new TBER. Public authorities would 
have to comply with the standard reporting obligations introduced in line with the Procedural 
Regulation352. In particular, Member States would be required to submit annual reports to the 
Commission in respect of aid measures approved under the revised GLs. However, the initiative 
is not expected to create significant additional administrative burdens on authorities since the 
process is the same as under other State aid GLs and Member States are therefore already 
familiar with it.  
The administrative burden linked to the obligation to notify to the Commission new or updated 
schemes is an intrinsic feature of State aid control pursuant to Article 108 (2) TFEU. The 
initiative would benefit Member States (and EFTA States), because it improves clarity and 
consistency about the way national State aid schemes should be designed and it provides 
necessary visibility as to the Commission’s State aid assessment for emerging aid designs and 
aid to new technologies. In addition, administrative burdens would be significantly reduced 
thanks to the introduction of the TBER, permitting State aid measures that present few 
competition distortion risks to be exempted from notification.  
Member States (and EFTA States) have the choice, whether to implement a State aid scheme: 
the initiative therefore would not introduce any mandatory costs. Any impact on Member 
States’ budgets allocated to State aid for transport would result from a decision of the relevant 
Member State. 
1.2. SLT operators 
SLT operators, such as railway undertakings, IWW vessel operators and maritime operators 
participating in a multimodal transport operation would benefit from the increased scope, 
additional legal certainty and generally higher aid intensities permitted by the revised GLs and 
TBER, where they are taken up by Member States. An increase in modal shift would lead to 
higher volumes and turnover, and create new investment opportunities. 
The initiative does not introduce any mandatory costs for businesses. It does reduce indirect 
opportunity costs for businesses, where Member States decide to introduce block exempted aid 
schemes, as the aid can be granted quicker due to the exemption from the notification procedure. 
 
352 See footnote 45.  

<!-- page:100 -->
 
A-28 
 
1.3. Transport organisers  
In freight transport, goods are owned and/or shipped by shippers, who have an interest to send 
the goods from the place of departure to the place of arrival. The shippers may organise the 
transport themselves or engage a logistics company or freight forwarder to do the organisation 
for them. These transport organisers choose the transport mode for the operation based on 
various considerations, such as cost, speed of delivery and flexibility.  
Transport organisers may benefit from the initiative, depending on the transport solutions they 
use. Transport organisers who already use SLT for all or part of their operations would benefit 
from the additional support that could be made available to the sector. Transport organisers who 
would switch to SLT for all or part of their operations would enjoy the benefits of more 
sustainable transport solutions at competitive terms with road transport. Transport organisers 
who would continue using only road transport would not benefit from this initiative.  
1.4. Passengers of SLT 
By enabling additional support in and facilitating implementation of e.g. operating aid and 
interoperability aid, the initiative may benefit citizens using SLT, if Member States decide to 
provide higher support. This could translate for example into rail passenger transport with lower 
prices, faster travel times and increased safety.  
1.5. Road hauliers  
An increase in modal shift in the freight segment, if Member States take up the possibilities 
offered under the initiative, would come at the expense of road only transport. Road hauliers 
who currently carry out road only transport may thus experience relatively lower demand for 
their services as a result of the initiative. Road hauliers would still be in a position to adapt to 
the multimodal market and offer multimodal transport solutions, i.e. the transportation of goods 
with both a SLT and a road leg.

ANNEX 4: ANALYTICAL METHODS 
The main analytical method used in this Impact Assessment Report is multi-criteria analysis, 
based on stakeholder consultations, a study carried out specifically for the initiative, policy 
documents from the EU and the Commission’s extensive case practice in State aid in the 
transport sector. Administrative simplification made possible by the TBER has been quantified 
through a specific and separate targeted consultation of Member States’ authorities.   
To assess the impacts of the changes in the scope of the RGL (Option Bundles A), the Impact 
Assessment Report relies on the consultations and the study. The study compared the impacts 
of demand-side schemes granting operating aid (those targeting transport organisers) to the 
impacts of supply-side schemes (those targeting transport operators such as railway 
undertakings). The study also looked into the availability and sufficiency of facilities in the EU 
and analysed structurally loss-making rail freight services and the profitability of SWL 
transport.  
To assess the impacts of the proposed compatibility conditions (Option Bundles B and C), the 
Impact Assessment Report relies on the consultations, the study and the case practice in State 
aid in the transport sector. In particular, the study assessed the degree of standardisation of 
rolling stock between Member States, the costs and challenges faced in accessing rolling stock, 
and the age and state of economic obsolescence of the rolling stock fleet in the EU. The study 
further analysed the acuteness of market failures affecting interoperability technologies.  
To assess the impacts of the proposed TBER regarding administrative simplification (Option 
Bundles A), in addition to information from the CfE and the further consultations, a dedicated 
targeted consultation was sent to Member States’ authorities to quantify the administrative and 
opportunity cost savings that may be realised. The details of the responses to the targeted 
consultation, as well as the analysis of administrative costs, which is based on the results of the 
targeted consultation, are presented in Annex 8. 
For the consultation of stakeholders, the Commission used a combination of questionnaires, 
meetings with interested stakeholders, conferences and public consultations, to ensure a 
transparent and comprehensive methodology to collect data for the initiative. Stakeholders 
representing the SLT sector also shared position papers and other assessments presenting their 
views on the scope and compatibility conditions for the revised GLs and TBER to further 
support modal shift.

2. Synthetic assessment  
2.1. Cost and price competitiveness 
The objective of the initiative is to reduce negative externalities from transport through modal 
shift, which is achieved in particular by increasing the cost and price competitiveness of the 
SLT sector as compared to the road only sector in the EU. State aid, supported by the 
administrative burden reduction in particular due to the TBER, can help to make SLT more 
price competitive.  
The current initiative would not only improve the competitiveness of SLT directly, e.g. by 
allowing additional support for avoided external costs, but also indirectly and in the longer term, 
by simplifying and strengthening support possibilities for transport facilities and 
interoperability technologies that lower costs and increase efficiency.  
The road only transport sector competes with SLT, and the initiative may therefore have 
negative impacts e.g. on road hauliers. In this respect, it is worth noting that the initiative is 
without prejudice, and complementary, to possibilities for granting aid in support of clean 
mobility and related infrastructure, such as electric vehicles or hydrogen refuelling 
infrastructure, under either the CEEAG or the GBER, and that road hauliers can be a part of 
multimodal transport operations, which would be included in the scope of the retained Option 
A3.   
An increase in the competitiveness of SLT operations would be expected to have knock-on 
effects throughout the entire economy, leveraging the initial impact on the transport sector as a 
network industry, which supports many other economic sectors (see Point 2.2 below for more 
detail). This would also be expected to lead to positive impacts on GDP. Indeed, the EU 
transport sector contributes 5% of GDP and 5% of all direct jobs, with a further four jobs in 
other sectors of the economy linked to every job in the transport sector353. The potential impact 
on GDP is however estimated to be small, as the initiative is not expected to generate significant 
new transport activity, but rather ensure redistribution of operations between the modes of 
transport and its effects depend on Member States putting into place support schemes. These 
 
353 See footnote 49. 

<!-- page:107 -->
 
A-35 
 
impacts would be expected to arise mostly thanks to positive effects on logistics companies and 
transport organisers, on the activities of facilities, and on the engagement of more companies 
per transport operation for multimodal transport instead of unimodal transport. 
2.2. International competitiveness 
This initiative would likely have a positive impact on international competitiveness as increased 
competition in a sector or sub-sector – as is the likely effect of the preferred options – is 
generally liable to increase output of more competitively priced goods or services. Moreover, 
as the transport sector is a network industry supplying the industrial sector as a whole, increased 
competition in the SLT sector should in particular be able to provide more competitive transport 
services when supplying the EU manufacturing sector, increasing the international 
competitiveness of the latter. Research has found that pro-competitive regulation in such input 
markets, such as energy or logistics, also lowers costs and leads to higher quality of inputs, 
contributing to export competitiveness of downstream companies354. Such impacts on 
international competitiveness can be expected to increase over time as the required 
infrastructure and equipment is upgraded, put in place or replaced under more generous State 
aid rules. Also, by facilitating investment aid to facilities and by potentially improving the 
efficiency of the SLT sector in the EU, transport activity currently taking place in countries 
bordering the EU (e.g. for transhipment) may move inside of it.  
2.3. Capacity to innovate 
The initiative would have a positive impact on capacity to innovate. It allows for stronger 
support with less administrative burden for innovative interoperability technologies such as 
ERTMS and DAC, which are technologies that are necessary for the implementation of further 
digitalisation. The initiative could be expected to lead to a wider adoption of these technologies, 
which would in turn increase the profitability of other innovations that build on those 
technologies and make them attractive internationally.  
The SLT sector would therefore have a stronger incentive to invest in innovation.   
2.4. SME competitiveness 
The initiative would improve SME competitiveness in several ways. Retained Option A3 puts 
the IWW sector, which is dominated by SMEs, on an equal footing with the rail sector in terms 
of legal framework allowing for support measures. Moreover, compatibility conditions of 
retained Option C1 help address market failures that affect SMEs specifically, while the 
simplification entailed by the block exemption is particularly relevant for SMEs. It has been 
estimated that retained Option A3 would allow opportunity cost savings specifically for SMEs 
of EUR 1.19-5.5 million annually. 
3. Competitive assessment of the most affected sectors 
The present initiative concerns the transport sector, in particular the SLT sector as potential 
beneficiary of State aid and the road only transport sector as its main competitor. In general,

competitiveness of the entire EU economy. Transport is also a priority sector for the EU’s

ANNEX 6: SME CHECK  
OVERVIEW OF IMPACTS ON SMES 
Relevance for SMEs  
Based on the SME filter and the ISG discussion, this initiative is relevant for SMEs.   
 
(1) 
IDENTIFICATION OF AFFECTED BUSINESSES AND ASSESSMENT OF RELEVANCE 
Are SMEs directly affected? No In which sectors? Not applicable. 
 
Estimated number of directly affected SMEs 
Not applicable. 
Estimated number of employees in directly affected SMEs 
Not applicable. 
Are SMEs indirectly affected? Yes In which sectors? What is the estimated number of 
indirectly affected SMEs and employees? 
This initiative is considered relevant for SMEs, as there is a strong presence of SMEs in the 
targeted sectors, as well as in the competing ones. However, generally the SMEs in the scope 
of the present initiative are affected in the same way as larger businesses (except for the 
retained Option C1 for the acquisition of vehicles, which is limited to the benefit of SMEs 
and new entrants) and no specific direct issues for SMEs have been identified, as the initiative 
does not impose any compliance or administrative obligations on businesses. Finally, the 
impact of the initiative depends on the actions of Member States, as it is at the latters’ 
discretion to design and put in place State aid measures. 
SMEs are within the scope of the initiative in their role as potential aid beneficiaries (for rail, 
IWW and road freight as part of multimodal transport operations), as competitors of potential 
aid beneficiaries (in particular unimodal road freight transport operations) and as being 
indirectly affected by the aid (e.g. hospitality sector around intermodal terminals).  
In all of the affected sectors, SMEs have a strong presence: 
It has been estimated that SMEs produce 44.6% of value added and represent 58.1% of jobs 
in the “mobility transport automotive” ecosystem of the EU industry355.  
 
355 Annual Report on European SMEs 2023/2024, DG GROW and JRC, https://single-market-
economy.ec.europa.eu/document/download/2bef0eda-2f75-497d-982e-
c0d1cea57c0e_en?filename=Annual%20Report%20on%20European%20SMEs%202024.pdf.  

<!-- page:111 -->
 
A-39 
 
SMEs are prominent in the sectors, for which support possibilities are considered for the 
revised GLs and TBER. In IWW, the majority of the businesses are SMEs356. More detailed 
data is available for some Member States, where there is significant IWW activity. For 
example, it is estimated that more than 80% of the companies in the IWW sector in Germany 
are SMEs and even micro enterprises357. In the rail sector, it is estimated that 93% of 
companies are SMEs358. 
SMEs are particularly relevant in the road freight sector, where they account for 99% of all 
enterprises359. In this sector, companies are positively affected when they perform activities 
which are eligible for support, as they pertain to multimodal transport. They would be 
negatively affected to the extent they perform unsupported activities competing with 
supported SLT activities. It has been assessed that approximately 27 000 companies could be 
involved in multimodal transport, most of them SMEs in the road sector360. It should be noted 
however that as access to terminals often requires special training of drivers and large road 
vehicle fleets, dedicated companies dominate the multimodal road sector and micro 
enterprises are not as active in multimodal transport, though they are sometimes 
subcontracted. 
Also, many SMEs are active on the demand side, i.e. businesses like logistics companies and 
freight forwarders, which are shipping goods via transport operators. The initiative is 
particularly relevant for SMEs in these sectors, as it is intended to make SLT more accessible 
and affordable for smaller and less sophisticated customers, bringing them into the scope of 
this initiative.  
The number of affected SMEs in other sectors will be even larger, but is not quantifiable. For 
example, increased use of multimodal transport often results in creation of new supporting 
services, in particular around terminals (hospitality sector). 
 
 
(2) CONSULTATION OF SME STAKEHOLDERS 
How has the input from the SME community been taken into consideration? 
There has been ample consultation of SMEs in the framework of this initiative.  
• 
Inception Impact Assessment 
Out of 15 stakeholders responding to the IIA, 7 were business associations representing 
various stakeholders in the rail sector. Two of the business associations specifically represent 
SMEs.   
• 
Call for evidence of the TBER 
 
356 https://www.europarl.europa.eu/RegData/etudes/BRIE/2022/698918/EPRS_BRI(2022)698918_EN.pdf and 
Section 2.1.3.
