A-40 
 
Out of 28 stakeholders responding to the CfE of the TBER, 14 were business associations. In 
the rail sector, one association representing SMEs exclusively and another mainly 
representing SMEs replied. In addition, there were replies from two associations mainly 
representing SMEs in IWW transport and three associations representing road transport, 
which consists almost exclusively of SMEs.  
• 
Public consultation 
Out of 70 stakeholders responding to the public consultation, 23 were companies and 22 were 
business associations/organisations. In the rail sector, 3 business associations mainly 
representing SMEs replied. Two business associations representing SMEs in the IWW sector 
also replied. Of the 23 companies replying to the consultation, 6 were SMEs, mainly logistics 
companies and railway undertakings.  
• 
Targeted consultation on the draft texts 
87 stakeholders replied to the targeted consultation, including 41 business associations and 
12 companies. Among the business associations replying that mainly represent SMEs, there 
are 5 business associations each in the rail and IWW sectors, 4 in the road sector and 1 
representing logistics companies (i.e. 15 business associations in total). One SME in the rail 
sector also replied. 
Are SMEs’ views different from those of large businesses? Partially. 
In addition to confirming comments by the general business community regarding the need 
for increased and rapid support for the SLT sector, SME representatives particularly insisted 
on open, transparent and non-discriminatory allocation of aid and the preference for aid 
schemes open to all instead of ad hoc aid to the largest market participants. They called for 
support schemes that do not create artificial barriers to entry, such as a high administrative 
burden. Additionally, they pointed to the specific market failures impacting SMEs concerning 
the financing of rolling stock and vessels. SMEs specifically supported the exclusion of large 
enterprises and the exclusion of grants from aid for the acquisition of vehicles.

What are the estimated direct costs for SMEs of the preferred policy option? Not 
applicable: As set out above, the initiative does not impose any compliance, reporting or 
administrative obligations on businesses, no matter their size. The introduction of aid 
schemes is at the discretion of Member States.  
Qualitative assessment 
Not applicable. 
Quantitative assessment

Not applicable.  
What are the estimated direct benefits/cost savings for SMEs of the preferred policy 
option362? 
Qualitative assessment 
Not applicable. 
Quantitative assessment 
Not applicable. 
What are the indirect impacts of this initiative on SMEs?  
SMEs are impacted in their role as potential aid beneficiaries or their competitors. The 
impacts of all considered options on SMEs have been assessed in detail in Sections 6.2.1, 
6.3.1 and 6.4.1 of this Impact Assessment Report and constitute one of the criteria for the 
choice of the preferred options.  
As set out, SMEs (but also other businesses) are indirectly positively affected by the block 
exemption, as it allows aid to be granted more swiftly, which is particularly relevant for 
SMEs. The indirect savings (opportunity cost reduction) from this acceleration have been 
estimated at about EUR 1.19-5.5 million annually for SMEs specifically363.  
 
(4) MINIMISING NEGATIVE IMPACTS ON SMES 
Are SMEs disproportionately affected compared to large companies? No. 
If yes, are there any specific subgroups of SMEs more exposed than others? 
No. 
Have mitigating measures been included in the preferred option/proposal? Yes.  
The initiative improves SME competitiveness in several ways. Retained Option A3 puts the 
IWW sector, which is dominated by SMEs, on an equal footing with the rail sector. 
Moreover, compatibility conditions of retained Option C1 help address market failures that 
affect SMEs specifically, while the block exemption ensures an administrative simplification 
which is particularly relevant for SMEs. Even though road only freight transport businesses, 
which are often SMEs, could be indirectly negatively impacted as competitors of potentially 
aided SLT modes, they could also profit from the present initiative, if they become part of 
multimodal transport operations, which in addition is likely to improve employment 
conditions of road hauliers, involving for example short first-/last-mile transport distances 
instead of long unimodal drives.  
 
362 The direct benefits for SMEs can also be cost savings. 
363 More details on the calculations are provided in Annex 8.

as the design and putting in place of State aid measures is at the latters’ discretion.

ANNEX 8: QUANTIFICATION OF ADMINISTRATIVE BURDEN AND 
RELATED COST REDUCTIONS 
In the targeted consultation of Member State authorities concerning the administrative burden 
reduction (see Point 4.5 of Annex 2), Member States were asked whether a block exemption 
would relieve administrative burden. They agreed nearly unanimously:  
 
Indeed, Member States consider that the (pre-)notification procedure is significantly more 
burdensome than the block exemption procedure:  
 
The (pre-)notification procedure has implications for administrative burden not only in terms 
of the amount of work involved in those procedures for Member States and the Commission, 
but also opportunity costs for businesses entailed by the delay between the design of a State aid 
measure within the Member State and its final implementation. One Member State noted that 

<!-- page:120 -->
 
A-48 
 
the notification procedure is long and that the Commission asks numerous questions at various 
stages in the procedure, which makes it very unreliable to estimate the timeline of 
implementation for new measures.   
The questionnaire asked Member States to quantify the time involved in (pre-)notifications 
which could be saved with a block exemption. The replies are displayed in the following Table 
16.

In summary, Member States estimate that the amount of work required to complete (pre)-
notification discussions and the preparation and submission of notification documents in SANI, 
the Commission’s IT tool used for that purpose, is equivalent to 40.5 working days per State 
aid measure on average. The median estimate is 24 working days. For block exempted 
measures, Member States estimate that the amount of work required to complete procedures 
involving the Commission (the preparation and submission of an information sheet in SANI) is 
equivalent to 5.8 working days per State aid measure on average, meaning that nearly 7 times 
less work is required than in case of notification. The median estimate for the time needed for 
a Member State to complete the procedures involving the Commission in the case of a block 
exempted measure is 0.8 working day implying a workload more than 20 times smaller for 
block exempted measures.  
Member States further estimate that the notification procedure on average adds 8.5 months to 
the process of adopting a State aid measure from start (designing a State aid measure in the 
Member State) to finish (implementing that measure). The median estimated time added is 7.5 
 
364 The source data was adjusted in the following ways: Where ranges were provided, averages were used. Where 
no value was provided, a smaller sample size was used. Where several authorities from the same Member State 
responded (Malta, Czechia and Italy), the average of the replies was used.  

<!-- page:121 -->
 
A-49 
 
months365. This additional time needed from the moment a measure is designed by a Member 
State until the moment it can actually be disbursed to the beneficiaries involves opportunity 
costs, which a block exemption would allow to reduce. 
In the following, the cost implications (in terms of administrative costs and opportunity costs) 
for each option in Option Bundle A, which are the options relating to the introduction of a block 
exemption, have been estimated for three scenarios:  
• a central scenario, implying that relevant State aid spending (and number of measures) 
would remain on the same level as the average of the years 2022-2024 (which could be 
considered as a baseline post-COVID-19),  
• a low scenario in case relevant State aid spending (and the number of measures) would 
go back to the level observed in 2018 (before the COVID-19 pandemic, due to Member 
States’ possible spending constraints and other priorities), and 
• a high scenario in case relevant State aid spending (and the number of measures366) 
would increase by 15% compared to the 2022-2024 average (due to Member States 
prioritising the decarbonisation of the transport sector via modal shift). 
Estimates were then derived for (a) the administrative burden reduction for Member State 
authorities, (b) the administrative burden reduction for the Commission and (c) the saved 
opportunity costs for businesses from the swifter implementation of State aid measures. 
(a) Quantification of administrative burden reduction for Member State 
authorities 
Taking the median results of the targeted consultation as a conservative basis for quantification, 
it follows from the replies that Member State authorities save about 23 working days367 per 
State aid measure which is block exempted.  
To estimate the administrative burden reduction for Option A1 (the block exemption would 
only extend to measures for which compatibility conditions are defined in section 6 of the 
RGL), the central scenario is based on the fact that, as the analysis of case practice shows, 
during the years 2022-2024, the Commission approved 18 State aid measures on average (per 
year) which fall under section 6 of the RGL. Based on the objective to block exempt 85% of 
aid measures368, this would mean that 15 measures would fall under the block exemption 
 
365 The reliability of this estimate is supported by the Commission’s internal data, which shows that for the 85 
State aid decisions in the SLT sector adopted in 2022-2024, the time between the creation of a case and the adoption 
of a decision is 7.6 months on average.   
366 Assuming measures would be distributed across categories in the same proportion as in the past. 
367 Median of 24 for notification – median of 0.8 for block exempted measures = 23.2, which is rounded to 23. 
Based on the average figures, Member State authorities would save 35 working days (40.5-5.8).  
368 Under this option, the notification thresholds would be designed in a way to achieve a block exemption of 85% 
of the measures, in line with GBER experience. 

<!-- page:122 -->
 
A-50 
 
annually. Member States would on this basis have annual administrative savings of EUR 98 
256369.  
Under the low scenario, the Commission has approved 6 State aid measures in 2018 which fall 
under section 6 of the RGL. Because of the objective to block exempt 85% of aid measures, 
this would mean that 5 measures would fall under the block exemption annually. Member States 
would on this basis have annual administrative savings of EUR 33 407370. 
Under the high scenario, there would be 21371 State aid measures on average (per year) which 
would fall under section 6 of the RGL. Given the objective to block exempt 85% of aid 
measures, this would mean that 18 measures would fall under the block exemption annually. 
Member States would on this basis have annual administrative savings of EUR 115 254372.  
For Options A2 and A3 (the block exemption extends to measures covered by today’s case 
practice in the SLT sector), the central scenario is based on the 28 State aid measures approved 
on average in the SLT sector per year for the years 2022-2024. Again, 85% of State aid 
measures would be block exempted. This would mean that 24 measures would fall under the 
block exemption annually. Member States would on this basis have an annual administrative 
saving of EUR 157 210373. 
Under the low scenario, the Commission has approved 15 State aid measures in 2018 in the 
SLT sector. Based on 85% of aid measures being block exempted, this would mean that 13 
measures would fall under the block exemption annually. Member States would on this basis 
have annual administrative savings of EUR 83 518374.  
Under the high scenario, there would be 32 State aid measures on average (per year) 375 in the 
SLT sector. Since 85% of aid measures would be block exempted, this would mean that 27 
measures would fall under the block exemption annually. Member States would on this basis 
have annual administrative savings of EUR 178 171376.

(b) Quantification of administrative burden reduction for the Commission 
Based on the Commission’s internal data for the 85 decisions adopted in the SLT sector in the 
period 2022-2024, the Commission spends on average 141.9 man-hours per decision, with a 
median of 116.8377. To provide a conservative estimate, ironing out extreme data points, the 
median estimate will be used to quantify the administrative savings for the Commission.  
From the low scenario of Option A1 to the high scenario of Options A2 and A3, the block 
exemption would reduce the number of cases handled by the Commission by between 5 and 27 
cases per year (see analysis above for Member State administrations). Applying the same 
standard hourly rate of EUR 35.6, that would equate to savings of between EUR 21 206378 and 
EUR 113 100379 per year.

(c) Quantification of opportunity cost savings for businesses  
Businesses would benefit from the simplification mainly because the approval (and subsequent 
payment) of support would be faster. Taking the median results of the targeted consultation as 
a conservative basis for quantification, it follows from the replies to the targeted consultation 
that Member State authorities save 7.5 months per measure that is block exempted, instead of 
having to go through the notification procedure. It is assumed that, since the Commission is not 
involved in the implementation of State aid measures outside of the notification procedure 
(including the adoption of State aid decisions), these results also imply that applicants can

<!-- page:124 -->
 
A-52 
 
benefit from the payment of State aid measures 7.5 months earlier, if those measures are block 
exempted.  
For Option A1 (the block exemption would only extend to measures for which compatibility 
conditions are defined in section 6 of the RGL), it has been estimated that the faster approval 
and implementation of measures under the central scenario would apply to 85% of State aid 
measures which currently fall under section 6 of the RGL going forward. However, 85% of 
measures being block exempted does not mean 85% of the spending being block exempted. 
Indeed, block exempted measures have generally lower budgets than notified measures, as the 
block exemption does not apply beyond certain thresholds. Based on data from existing block 
exemption regulations, one can estimate the proportion in the overall spending for this type of 
measures to which 85% of block exempted measures correspond. Under various block 
exemption regulations (including the GBER), where in 2023 88% of measures were block 
exempted, this translated to 38% of spending380. On that basis it is estimated that 36.7%381 of 
the relevant State aid spending will be able to benefit from the block exemption going forward.  
Under the central scenario (implying that relevant State aid spending would remain on the same 
level as the average of the years 2022-2024), one would take as a basis the average annual State 
aid spending on measures falling under section 6 of the RGL during the years 2022-2024 of 
EUR 4.2 billion, as the analysis of the case practice shows. Taking into account an acceleration 
of the procedure by 7.5 months thanks to block exemption and the average discount rate for the 
years 2022-2024 of 2.8% as a proxy for the discount rates going forward, the savings of 
businesses due to the quicker availability of support have been estimated to amount to EUR 
27.6 million annually on average382, or 0.6% of State aid spending383. 
For the low scenario, State aid spending on measures falling under section 6 of the RGL in 2018 
amounted to EUR 2.4 billion. Taking into account an acceleration of the procedure by 7.5 
months thanks to block exemption and using the average discount rate for the years 2022-2024 
of 2.8% as a proxy for the discount rates going forward, this would translate to savings for 
businesses due to the quicker availability of support of about EUR 15.6 million annually.  
For the high scenario, State aid spending on measures falling under section 6 of the RGL would 
amount to 115% the average State aid spending in the period 2022-2024, i.e. EUR 4.8 billion. 
This would translate to savings of businesses due to the quicker availability of support of about 
EUR 30.9 million384.

(d) Quantification of opportunity cost savings for SMEs  
In order to quantify the opportunity cost savings for SMEs, the same methodology as above has 
been applied, while approximating the share of aid received by SMEs in each option and 
scenario based on their market share in terms of turnover in different segments of SLT. The 
market shares in terms of turnover have been calculated based on Eurostat data related to 
Structural Business Statistics, classified by size and NACE Rev.2 activity. Market share data 
for some of the segments which have not been readily available from the above statistics have 
been approximated based on the case practice. SMEs have a relatively higher market share in 
the IWW sector and in the operation of facilities, while the rail transport sector activity is 
dominated by larger enterprises.  
Under the central scenario (implying that relevant State aid spending would remain on the same 
level as the average of the years 2022-2024), the average annual State aid amounts received by 
SMEs would amount to EUR 385 million in the case of Option A1 (i.e. 10% of State aid 
amounts overall) and EUR 657 million in case of Options A2 and A3 (i.e. 14% of State aid 
amounts overall). Taking into account an acceleration of the procedure by 7.5 months thanks to 
the block exemption and the average discount rate for the years 2022-2024 of 3.2% (higher than 
the average including large businesses which have easier access to bank financing) as a proxy 
for the discount rates going forward, the savings of SMEs due to the quicker availability of

ANNEX 9: RELEVANT PROVISIONS OF THE TFEU 
Article 93 
(ex Article 73 TEC) 
Aids shall be compatible with the Treaties if they meet the needs of coordination of transport 
or if they represent reimbursement for the discharge of certain obligations inherent in the 
concept of a public service. 
Article 100 
(ex Article 80 TEC) 
1. The provisions of this Title shall apply to transport by rail, road and inland waterway. 
2. The European Parliament and the Council, acting in accordance with the ordinary legislative 
procedure, may lay down appropriate provisions for sea and air transport. They shall act after 
consulting the Economic and Social Committee and the Committee of the Regions. 
Article 106 
(ex Article 86 TEC) 
1. In the case of public undertakings and undertakings to which Member States grant special or 
exclusive rights, Member States shall neither enact nor maintain in force any measure contrary 
to the rules contained in the Treaties, in particular to those rules provided for in Article 18 and 
Articles 101 to 109. 
2. Undertakings entrusted with the operation of services of general economic interest or having 
the character of a revenue-producing monopoly shall be subject to the rules contained in the 
Treaties, in particular to the rules on competition, in so far as the application of such rules does 
not obstruct the performance, in law or in fact, of the particular tasks assigned to them. The 
development of trade must not be affected to such an extent as would be contrary to the interests 
of the Union. 
3. The Commission shall ensure the application of the provisions of this Article and shall, where 
necessary, address appropriate directives or decisions to Member States. 
Article 107 
(ex Article 87 TEC) 
1. Save as otherwise provided in the Treaties, any aid granted by a Member State or through 
State resources in any form whatsoever which distorts or threatens to distort competition by 
favouring certain undertakings or the production of certain goods shall, in so far as it affects 
trade between Member States, be incompatible with the internal market. 
2. The following shall be compatible with the internal market: 

<!-- page:128 -->
 
A-56 
 
(a) aid having a social character, granted to individual consumers, provided that such aid is 
granted without discrimination related to the origin of the products concerned; 
(b) aid to make good the damage caused by natural disasters or exceptional occurrences; 
(c) aid granted to the economy of certain areas of the Federal Republic of Germany affected by 
the division of Germany, in so far as such aid is required in order to compensate for the 
economic disadvantages caused by that division. Five years after the entry into force of the 
Treaty of Lisbon, the Council, acting on a proposal from the Commission, may adopt a decision 
repealing this point. 
3. The following may be considered to be compatible with the internal market: 
(a) aid to promote the economic development of areas where the standard of living is 
abnormally low or where there is serious underemployment, and of the regions referred to in 
Article 349, in view of their structural, economic and social situation; 
(b) aid to promote the execution of an important project of common European interest or to 
remedy a serious disturbance in the economy of a Member State; 
(c) aid to facilitate the development of certain economic activities or of certain economic areas, 
where such aid does not adversely affect trading conditions to an extent contrary to the common 
interest; 
(d) aid to promote culture and heritage conservation where such aid does not affect trading 
conditions and competition in the Union to an extent that is contrary to the common interest; 
(e) such other categories of aid as may be specified by decision of the Council on a proposal 
from the Commission. 
Article 108 
(ex Article 88 TEC) 
1. The Commission shall, in cooperation with Member States, keep under constant review all 
systems of aid existing in those States. It shall propose to the latter any appropriate measures 
required by the progressive development or by the functioning of the internal market. 
2. If, after giving notice to the parties concerned to submit their comments, the Commission 
finds that aid granted by a State or through State resources is not compatible with the internal 
market having regard to Article 107, or that such aid is being misused, it shall decide that the 
State concerned shall abolish or alter such aid within a period of time to be determined by the 
Commission. 
If the State concerned does not comply with this decision within the prescribed time, the 
Commission or any other interested State may, in derogation from the provisions of Articles 
258 and 259, refer the matter to the Court of Justice of the European Union direct. 
On application by a Member State, the Council may, acting unanimously, decide that aid which 
that State is granting or intends to grant shall be considered to be compatible with the internal 

<!-- page:129 -->
 
A-57 
 
market, in derogation from the provisions of Article 107 or from the regulations provided for 
in Article 109, if such a decision is justified by exceptional circumstances. If, as regards the aid 
in question, the Commission has already initiated the procedure provided for in the first 
subparagraph of this paragraph, the fact that the State concerned has made its application to the 
Council shall have the effect of suspending that procedure until the Council has made its attitude 
known. 
If, however, the Council has not made its attitude known within three months of the said 
application being made, the Commission shall give its decision on the case. 
3. The Commission shall be informed, in sufficient time to enable it to submit its comments, of 
any plans to grant or alter aid. If it considers that any such plan is not compatible with the 
internal market having regard to Article 107, it shall without delay initiate the procedure 
provided for in paragraph 2. The Member State concerned shall not put its proposed measures 
into effect until this procedure has resulted in a final decision. 
4. The Commission may adopt regulations relating to the categories of State aid that the Council 
has, pursuant to Article 109, determined may be exempted from the procedure provided for by 
paragraph 3 of this Article. 
Article 109 
(ex Article 89 TEC) 
The Council, on a proposal from the Commission and after consulting the European Parliament, 
may make any appropriate regulations for the application of Articles 107 and 108 and may in 
particular determine the conditions in which Article 108(3) shall apply and the categories of aid 
exempted from this procedure. 
 
 

<!-- page:130 -->
 
A-58 
 
ANNEX 10: SUMMARY OF THE PROVISIONS OF THE RGL 
The RGL388 contain 8 sections, of which only section 6 on coordination of transport is still 
relevant today. This Annex provides a short description of the content of the other sections of 
the RGL, explaining why they are not relevant anymore, followed by a detailed description of 
the compatibility criteria for section 6. 
1. The other sections of the RGL 
Sections 1 on the context and 8 on final provisions frame the text without setting out any 
measures or compatibility conditions. Section 2 provides a short description of when public 
financing of railway infrastructure could constitute aid to railway undertakings. Section 3 on 
the purchase and renewal of rolling stock refers to the compatibility conditions of other GLs. 
Section 4 on debt cancellation refers only to debt incurred prior to 15 March 2001. Section 5 
on restructuring was only applicable to rail freight divisions of railway undertakings and 
expired on 1 January 2010. Section 7 calls for the removal of unlimited State guarantees to 
railway undertakings.  
2. The compatibility criteria for the measures falling under section 6 of the RGL 
Section 6 refers to coordination of transport and lays down compatibility criteria for three types 
of measures389: 
• Operating aid for infrastructure use, i.e. aid granted to railway undertakings which 
have to pay charges for the infrastructure they use, while undertakings providing 
transport services from other modes do not have to do so (or have to do so to a lesser 
extent); 
• Operating aid for reducing external cost due to rail transport generating lower 
external costs than other transport modes, such as road transport; and 
• Investment aid for interoperability and for promoting greater safety, removing 
technical barriers and reducing noise pollution in the rail sector. 
The aid is considered compatible, if it is necessary, proportionate and not unduly distorting 
competition. Section 6.3 of the RGL operationalises these compatibility conditions further. 
For aid for infrastructure use, necessity and proportionality are presumed, as long as the aid 
amount remains below 30% of the total cost of rail transport and 100% of the additional cost 
for infrastructure use paid by rail transport compared to a more polluting competing transport 
mode.  
For aid for the reduction of external costs, necessity and proportionality are presumed, as long 
as the aid amount remains below 30% of the total cost of rail transport and 50% of the part of 
 
388 See footnote 5. 
389 In addition, section 6.4 concerns aid for research and development. However, specific horizontal rules for such 
measures have since superseded the RGL in the Communication from the Commission, Framework for State aid 
for research and development and innovation, most recently from 2022, OJ C 414, 28.10.2022 and previously from 
2014 (OJ C 198, 27.6.2014). Both texts state that the Commission will apply the principles set out in the 
Framework concerned for the ‘compatibility assessment of all notified R&D&I aid’.  

<!-- page:131 -->
 
A-59 
 
the external costs, which rail transport makes it possible to avoid compared with competing 
transport modes.  
For both, in case there are several competing transport modes, the highest cost differential can 
be applied. The calculation has to be based on a transparent, reasoned, quantified and public 
cost analysis. 
As regards aid for interoperability investments, necessity and proportionality are presumed, as 
long as the aid amount remains below 50% of the investment costs relating to the installation 
of safety systems and interoperability or noise reduction, in particular investments associated 
with the deployment of ERTMS and any like measures. 
Aid amounts above the thresholds are possible for all three categories, but Member States must 
then demonstrate the necessity and proportionality of the measure which they propose.  
For all three categories, Member States have to prove that the aid has the effect of encouraging 
the modal shift, i.e. that it is reflected in the price demanded from passengers or shippers (so-
called pass-on), as it is the latter who make the choice of the transport mode. For the two 
operating aid categories, Member States also have to demonstrate that there is a realistic 
prospect of keeping the traffic transferred to rail. 
Finally, aid schemes can be approved for a maximum of 5 years. 
 
 
 

<!-- page:132 -->
 
A-60 
 
ANNEX 11: DATA AND METHODOLOGY USED FOR DISAGGREGATING 
THE ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACTS 
The starting point for the calculations in Table 6 is the Commission Handbook on external 
costs390, which contains data on environmental impacts for various transport modes. However, 
additional data is required to compare the overall environmental impacts of SLT to those of 
road transport. 
The figures for the railway sector combine data on diesel and electric trains, and assume that 
80% of rail traffic in the EU is electrified. The Draghi report noted that 80% of rail traffic takes 
place on electrified tracks391. Moreover, in 2022, the share of electricity in the energy 
consumption in rail transport was 78.9%, compared to 20.4% for diesel trains (the remaining 
0.7% coming from biofuels)392. Since electric engines are more efficient than diesel ones, the 
share of electric rail traffic should be higher than 78.9%. In the absence of more granular data, 
this proportion is applied for both freight and passenger transport.  
The figures for SLT in the freight segment combine the figures for the rail and IWW sectors. 
This calculation assumes that SLT in the freight segment consists of 77.3% rail traffic and 
22.7% IWW traffic, consistent with the latest data from the EU statistical pocketbook for the 
year 2025393. Data on the modal share and environmental impacts of short-sea shipping is not 
available.   
In the freight segment, it is assumed that SLT competes specifically with heavy-goods vehicles, 
i.e. trucks. Data on the environmental impacts of light commercial vehicles, such as delivery 
vans, exists as well, and the environmental impacts of these vehicles per tonne or passenger-
kilometre are worse than for heavy-goods vehicles. This assumption is therefore conservative, 
in terms of demonstrating how much more environmentally friendly SLT is compared to road 
transport.  
The data on passenger road transport combine separate data from passenger cars, buses, coaches 
and powered two-wheelers. It is assumed that in the passenger segment, the modal shares of 
these modes are 88.7% for passenger cars, 8.9% in aggregate for buses and coaches (traffic is 
split equally among both modes, in the absence of more granular data), and 2.4% for powered 
two-wheelers, consistent with the latest data from the EU statistical pocketbook for the year 
2025394. 
Lastly, the costs of energy production or well-to-tank emissions consist essentially of emissions 
of air pollutants and GHG emissions, and are spread equally across those two categories.

The indicators above will be compared primarily to their own level in the period before the adoption of the revised 
rules, in order to quantify how the revision of the rules impacted them. A disaggregation by Member State is also 
possible. In addition, in some cases, other indicators can be used as basis for comparison or as control factors in 
order to better isolate the effect of the changes in the rules. These are described below:    
(a) The indicator can be compared also with the changes in amounts of State aid approved for all sectors 
and types (source: the State aid Scoreboard https://competition-policy.ec.europa.eu/state-
aid/scoreboard_en). 
(b) The changes in the value of total public investments in SLT infrastructure (excluding State-aid 
approved investments) can be used as control factor (source: Monitoring report on the development 
of 
the 
rail 
market

A-63 
 
(c) The amounts of aid granted under block exemptions compared to those granted under notifications 
in other sectors (e.g. energy, environment, agriculture etc.) can be used as control factor. (source: 
Database of State aid cases https://competition-cases.ec.europa.eu/search?caseInstrument=SA). 
(d) The deployment of track-side ERTMS can be used as a control factor for this indicator for an analysis 
at country level (source: https://transport.ec.europa.eu/transport-modes/rail/ertms/state-play_en). 
(e) The degree of market opening measured as the market share of non-incumbent rail operators can be 
used as control factor for an analysis of the indicator at country level (source: Monitoring report on 
the development of the rail market https://transport.ec.europa.eu/transport-modes/rail/market/rail-
market-monitoring-rmms_en). 
(f) The evolution of aid amounts under schemes compared with individual ad hoc aid for all sectors can 
be used as control factor. (source: Database of State aid cases, https://competition-
cases.ec.europa.eu/search?caseInstrument=SA).
